# Supplementary Notes

## Note S1. Scoring provenance

Evo 2-40B was run zero-shot with no fine-tuning, and all scoring ran on a cloud GPU. The 1,001-bp and 8,192-bp readouts are different scores of the same variants and differ by +0.065 AUROC by the species mean (+0.092 pooled); every number in this paper is tagged with the readout (8,192-bp mean log-likelihood, or 1,001-bp single-token variant-delta) that produced it.

## Note S2. Equivalence testing

No null on which this paper rests a conclusion is supported by a failure to reject alone. Three descriptive nulls are reported as bare non-significance and are named as such where they appear (the within-window cattle correlation, the divergence-grading test and the unsigned-magnitude ranking); none carries a conclusion on its own. Where we report the absence of a difference that does carry one, we either bound it with an interval or test it with two one-sided tests (TOST) at α = 0.05, declaring equivalence only if the 90% interval lies wholly inside ±δ. TOST yields two one-sided p-values; the value we report is the larger of the two, which is the one that governs the equivalence decision.
**Choosing δ without a domain standard.** No published source supplies a bound on "an AUROC difference that would matter" for variant-effect prediction. Rather than import one, we set δ = 0.02, after the data were seen, from this study's own scale: it is the smallest increment we elsewhere treat as real (the pooled orthogonal contribution the foundation model adds beyond conservation, +0.0202). The criterion is therefore self-binding: we cannot declare a difference negligible that is larger than a difference we ourselves called meaningful. Sensitivity at δ = 0.01 and 0.05 is in Table S5.
**The estimand.** The equivalence test is run on the pooled Evo 2 − GERP difference over the 9,532 co-scorable variants, which is +0.0024. It is not run on the species-mean difference of +0.047 that the Analyses report: those are two different summaries of the same comparison, and only the pooled one is being tested for equivalence against δ. We state this explicitly because reading the +0.047 as the tested quantity makes the reported p = 0.28 look impossible, a point estimate more than twice δ could not sit near the equivalence boundary, when in fact the tested estimate is well inside it.
**The unit of analysis decides the answer.** Holding that estimand fixed and varying only the resampling unit: a variant-level bootstrap gives a 90% interval of [−0.010, +0.015] and would declare equivalence. A species-clustered bootstrap gives [−0.024, +0.056] and does not. Species are this paper's unit of generalisation, so the species-clustered interval is the one consistent with that unit for this between-species comparison, and we report that equivalence is not established. We note for completeness that species clustering is used for this equivalence interval specifically; the within-species readout, ΔFM and ΔECE intervals reported elsewhere are variant-level or within-species bootstraps, appropriate to those within-species estimands. Quoting the narrower variant-level interval here would have manufactured a conclusion out of an analysis choice.

## Note S3. Reproduction and open items

**Reproduction, stated precisely.** All ten main-text figures rebuild from deposited artefacts, together with Figures S1, S2, S4, S7 and S8; Figures S3, S5 and S6 are built from the primary score and label tree, which is not deposited (Note S58 sets out what regenerates without it). The archive records what can and cannot be run from it in docs/REPRODUCING.md; unavailable steps are reported as unavailable, never as passing.
Open items.
1. The cause of class-dependent conservation missingness is unresolved. The marginal class gap in reach is large (up to +0.489 in horse) and its deployment cost is real, but under a consequence-matched control no species keeps a positive gap separable from zero (dog's reverses, −0.120 [−0.228, −0.037], across consequence categories that disagree with one another), and only 5 of 9 panels have a matchable stratum at all. We report the cost, not a mechanism.
2. Panel size is confounded with alignment quality in these data, so "invariant to study effort" and "invariant to alignment quality" are both supported readings of the same slope contrast.
3. Supervised baselines are point estimates without paired intervals (Table S3).
4. The cross-clade bat arm is pilot grade. Ten libraries bound the allele count at 20, placing a floor near minor allele frequency (MAF) 0.05, which is an allele-count floor rather than a fixed threshold, one that varies with how many of the ten libraries are called and reaches 0.05 only at full allele number, and which is precisely the window in which the cattle relationship is itself null. It shows a gradient of the same sign, which is narrower than recurrence, in a lineage with no curated resource; it does not transport the cattle effect size, and no additional sequencing depth would change that, because the limitation is the number of libraries sequenced (Note S26).
5. A weaker backbone did not behave as expected. Substituting Nucleotide Transformer through the same Mondrian pipeline preserved coverage (NT 0.981 against Evo 2 0.970, each the mean over the three held-out target species; over all nine panels the pair is 0.965 against 0.944) at a comparable abstention rate. The intuitive prediction, that a weaker model should abstain more, did not hold, so we claim only that coverage is model-agnostic, not that abstention tracks backbone quality.

## Note S4. Controls on the eQTL-panel null, and the bound the model's own paper places on it

Three controls constrain, but do not isolate, the source of the near-chance result on the pig cis-eQTL panel (AUROC 0.488; Note S17). First, scale does not close the gap: across a 40-fold parameter range (Evo 2-1B to Evo 2-40B; the 1B is a short-context experimental release that [19] says "should be avoided owing to overall weaker performance", and the three checkpoints differ in training data as well as parameter count, 7B was trained on 2.4 trillion tokens and 40B on 9.3 trillion, both with context extension to 1 Mb, so this is a parameter ratio rather than a controlled scaling sweep), whole-atlas macro discrimination gains +0.075 (+0.043 without human, whose 1B scores are anomalous; Note S15) while the eQTL effect size |AUROC − 0.5| falls from 0.008 to 0.002, moving toward chance rather than away from it, with overlapping confidence intervals (Supplementary Table S4). Second, within the tested 1,002- to 4,097-bp window arm, context alone was not identified as the dominant factor, and the two factors can be separated cleanly because each is varied on its own. Holding the readout fixed at a single-token variant delta, the window moves from 1,002 bp to the 2,049-bp rung for a change of 0.002 in AUROC (0.485 to 0.487), and to 4,097 bp for −0.001 (0.484); holding the window fixed at 1,002 bp, switching aggregation from single-token to mean log-likelihood moves it by 0.011 (0.485 to 0.496). The protocol choice therefore matters more than the context length, though both effects are small on this panel. The 40B eQTL scores for the 20,000-variant panel are read at the 1,001-bp single-token readout, from 1,002-bp windows (Note S30), while the consequence-matched 2,000-variant subpanel is scored at 8,192 bp and its 0.498 is recorded at that window in the deposited artefact (Note S17); the matched 1,002-bp mean-log-likelihood arm reported above is the comparison that holds the window fixed. Third, all tested sequence-based scorers were near chance on the consequence-matched 2,000-variant subpanel: Evo 2-40B 0.498 (95% CI [0.473, 0.523]), Nucleotide Transformer [60] 0.486, and three GERP flavours spanning 0.473–0.532 (a span of three point estimates, not a confidence interval; Table S4 lists the three GERP flavours as separate rows, each with its own interval, while the deposited artefact's lo/hi fields hold the span). This is a different and smaller panel from the 20,000-variant one that gives 0.488, and the two figures should not be read as the same estimate. On the same variants, the |z| association statistic reaches 0.594. We do not treat that as a positive control. SuSiE-inf computes the posterior inclusion probability from the association statistics, so |z| separating high-PIP from low-PIP variants is guaranteed by construction and demonstrates nothing about whether the labels are recoverable from sequence. It establishes only that the fine-mapping is self-consistent. The consequence is a limit on what the eQTL result can identify: our data would be equally consistent with the model being blind to regulatory variation and with these labels not being a recoverable target at all, were it not for a control that separates them. Distance to the transcription start site (TSS), a predictor using no sequence whatsoever, separates the candidate causal variants from their within-eGene controls at AUROC 0.651 (95% CI [0.642, 0.660]; n = 19,341 of the 20,000 matched to their gene's TSS in the Ensembl Sscrofa11.1 annotation), and at 0.665 averaged within eGene across 1,380 genes; the median distance is 163 kb for candidate causal variants against 370 kb for controls. The labels therefore carry some recoverable signal, which bounds the comparison without settling it: a panel a trivial positional baseline reads at 0.651, on which a 40-billion-parameter sequence model reads 0.488, is informative about the model. The corollary cuts the other way and we state it: 0.651 is not high, so a capability gap cannot be cleanly separated from labels that carry little signal, and this is not a cleanly sequence-learnable target either; we treat neither reading as dismissed (Note S21) and do not claim the model ought to have matched a positional baseline. A second constraint comes from the model's own paper, and it bounds our claim rather than supporting it. Reference [19] reports Evo 2-40B on the DART-eval regulatory tasks at AUROC 0.58 for chromatin-accessibility QTLs (caQTLs) and 0.66 for DNase-I-sensitivity QTLs, above Nucleotide Transformer (0.52, 0.61) and below the sequence-to-function model ChromBPNet (0.77, 0.89). Those are regulatory variants, scored zero-shot, on the same checkpoint, and they are not negligible. So the null we report at 0.488 is not a general inability to read regulatory sequence. It is specific to this panel: pig cis-eQTLs, fine-mapped from expression association, in a non-model species. What distinguishes them from human caQTLs is some combination of species, label semantics (accessibility and DNase sensitivity are closer to sequence than expression association is), and panel construction. We cannot separate those three here, and we do not state the limit more broadly than the evidence allows. A stronger control still would be a positive control the model should pass, a set of experimentally validated causal regulatory variants from massively parallel reporter assays (MPRA) or CRISPR interference (CRISPRi), or a supervised regulatory model such as Enformer or Borzoi run on these same variants, and we do not have one. We therefore report the eQTL result as a null at the single-token readout on this panel, not as a demonstrated failure of the model.

## Note S5. Specialist and supervised baselines, and the composition control on the full atlas

The specialist baselines are limited by the accounting of reach as well. A protein language model [109] can be run on only 3 of 9 panels, because in the others one class has fewer than ten missense variants; only human is adequately powered and only human is tabulated (Supplementary Table S3), and on that panel it beats both alternatives. The human ESM-2 panel is the 912 variants carrying an ESM-2 score, of which 652 are positive, a 71.5% positive rate unlike either the human atlas panel (50%) or the OMIA panels (9%), because ESM-2 scores only protein-coding variants and those are enriched for positives. It is therefore not comparable to the atlas panels and we do not pool it. The three headline AUROCs are not on one variant set, and we do not present them as one: ESM-2 0.887 and Evo 2 0.838 are computed on all 912, while GERP 0.860 is computed on the 881 that carry a finite GERP value, against which Evo 2 reads 0.837. The matched GERP margin is +0.023 [−0.011, +0.058] and does not exclude zero, so on this missense-enriched human panel GERP leads Evo 2 on the point estimate only, and charging GERP for the 31 variants it cannot call brings it to 0.835, while the paired difference ESM-2 − Evo 2 = +0.049 [+0.019, +0.079] is the difference of the two AUROCs on all 912 variants ESM-2 can score (0.8871 against 0.8380; the deposited artefact), not a computation restricted to the 752 of those carrying a missense annotation. The headline three are therefore not a like-for-like ESM-2 comparison, and a matched-subset difference is not among the deposited statistics. Supervised models trained on the labels themselves (Supplementary Table S3) fare worse: across the eight species with sufficient positives, a one-dimensional convolutional neural network (1D-CNN) loses to zero-shot Evo 2 in all eight once its own species is held out, falling to 0.482, below chance, in human. We do not present this as evidence that supervised learning fails on this task. A simple k-mer classifier (gradient boosting on k-mer features), tabulated alongside it (Supplementary Table S3), performs comparably to the convolutional network in most species and beats it in four of eight (sheep, cattle, dog and human, the largest margin being human at +0.090), which is itself evidence that neither is extracting much beyond composition. We ran the direct control this implies (deposited), computed on the full nine-species atlas (11,130 variants) with Evo 2 alone. Trinucleotide context, the variable the negatives were explicitly matched on, is matched in every species (χ² p = 1.0). Local GC, matched only approximately by nearest-neighbour, is not: positives run about 0.07 higher in GC than negatives in eight of the nine species, all but human, whose negatives are higher, 0.5185 against 0.5099, because most human negatives are coding, unlike the non-human ones, so GC follows the consequence mix. The Mann–Whitney test is significant in eight as well, but not the same eight: goat is the one that is not (p = 0.075), and human is significant (p = 0.048) while running the other way. GC alone separates the classes at a macro AUROC of 0.67. Trinucleotide matching is therefore effective but a residual coding/GC composition signal remains, the same confound the pooled missense stratum, 0.812 (Fig. 10b), isolates and the conservation-matched control (0.834) survives; we do not claim the matching removes all composition signal. The k-mer classifier's higher AUROC reflects the still broader k-mer content of the whole scored window, which carries the coding-versus-noncoding signal. As a further panel-level control, 118 of 8,890 negatives (1.3%) fall within 1 kb of a same-chromosome positive, so some 1,001-bp window overlap exists; but the macro AUROC is unchanged (0.878 → 0.878) after excluding all 656 negatives within 50 kb, so proximity between classes is not inflating the result (section 19 of recompute_atlas_strata.py, Additional file 3, which keys chromosomes through refseq_chromosome_map.csv; on the raw identifiers of reports/atlas_controls.json in Additional file 2 the counts are 116 and 629). A convolutional network trained on the pooled positives of seven species and tested on an eighth is data-starved by construction: for the human fold highlighted here that is 731 positives, not the 2,240 nine-species total, because 1,500 of those are the human variants being held out and goat's 9 enter no fold. (731 is also, by coincidence, the count of missed positives in Table S28.) We note one result that complicates this reading: in four of the eight species (pig, sheep, cat and dog) the leave-one-species-out network outperforms the network trained with that species' own labels included, which is not what pure data starvation predicts and points to a specification or regularisation failure alongside it. We therefore claim only that these labels are too few to train a competitive supervised predictor by this route, not that data volume is the sole limit, which is the premise of the paper rather than a finding about architectures. It is a floor, not a competitor.
**A cross-modality calibration arm, for completeness.** Running the identical leave-one-out isotonic transport over 644,387 ProteinGym deep-mutational-scan single mutants scored with ESM-2 (experimental fitness labels, a protein backbone, 197 held-out assays) moves median expected calibration error from 0.141 to 0.139 and beats no calibration on 116 of 197 assays (median zero-shot AUROC 0.744). That is a similarly small observed difference: the transport neither helps nor harms much, on a different model class and on wet-lab labels. Finally, the selective-prediction behaviour reported in Notes S45 and S66 is not specific to Evo 2. Substituting a weaker Nucleotide Transformer backbone through the same Mondrian class-conditional conformal pipeline preserved coverage (0.970 for Evo 2 against 0.981 for NT, each the mean over the three held-out target species) at a similar cost in abstention. Two honest limits attach. The comparator does not itself clear the coding task, so it hints at a model-class effect without supplying a clean second data point. And the intuitive expectation that the weaker backbone would abstain more did not hold, so we do not present abstention rate as a proxy for model quality.

## Note S6. Relation to concurrent cross-species work, and the overlap we assume rather than claim

Our claim is narrower than the atlas might suggest. That Evo 2 discriminates pathogenic from population variants across several vertebrate species is already established, including in concurrent cross-species work [26] that is closer to this atlas than a passing citation conveys, and we set out the comparison. That study evaluates Evo 2 on 721 high-quality OMIA single-nucleotide variants (SNVs) across the same eight species we use (chicken, dog, cat, goat, horse, pig, sheep and cattle) scored with the Evo 2-7B checkpoint (which they used for all analyses) in 8,192-bp windows by zero-shot log-likelihood, which is our headline readout. It reports AUROC 0.934 against variant-type-blind controls and 0.717 against variant-type-matched controls; it also reports the precision-recall summary (area under the precision-recall curve, AUPRC), which we quote because under class imbalance it is the decision-relevant summary and it moves far more, 0.832 blind falling to 0.166 matched, close to the precision floor of a ~9%-positive panel. The AUPRC collapse is stronger evidence for the shared thesis, that matched controls change the conclusion, than the AUROC drop. Our corresponding figures, from the larger Evo 2-40B checkpoint, are 0.943 against trinucleotide-matched population negatives and 0.812 under composition-matching towards missense. The unmatched comparison replicates closely, but that pair is not checkpoint-matched: their 0.934 is 7B over eight species and our 0.943 is 40B over nine. Checkpoint- and species-matched, our 7B scores over the same eight species at 8,192 bp read 0.937 pooled and 0.924 by the species mean, against their 0.934 and 0.909 (recompute_macro_ci.py and fig1_atlas_ladder.parquet, Additional file 3). The matched comparison differs, and we attribute that to the matching being defined differently rather than to a discrepancy in the model. The atlas should therefore be read as a replication of that result at a larger panel size, not as an independent discovery, and the contribution this paper claims is the accounting around it (the readout, the reach, the calibration and the abstention layer), not the discrimination number itself. Their variant list is not public: the study's repository (github.com/jiang18/evo2-omia) holds its analysis code and a table of assemblies, and neither its 721 positives, its controls nor its per-variant scores are released, so the comparison above uses its published summary figures. Given a common source database (OMIA) and an identical species set, the variant-level overlap between their 721 and our 740 positives is likely substantial, and we assume so rather than claim independence. What we add is a correction to how these models are measured, and it changes the answer. The two accounting choices that drive it, the scoring readout and reach, are stated in the Background and quantified in the Analyses; we do not restate them here, and neither is a robustness check. Two qualifications on the selective layer belong with this comparison rather than with the framing. Its apparent failure in human is largely a panel-composition effect (human is the only 1:1 panel) rather than a property of that species' ClinVar labels (Table S27). And under a cost matrix that prices a missed variant ten times a false alarm, blanket abstention beats every learned rule including ours when costs are pooled across species, though not under the species-mean aggregation we otherwise take as primary. We present the layer as a deployable procedure with stated limits, not as the paper's main claim.

## Note S7. Composition of the consequence gradient, and the stability of its lowest interval

Partitioning the atlas positives by molecular consequence and scoring each class against the same pooled, trinucleotide-matched negatives (within-species z-scored, so that species with different score variances contribute on one ruler; this is a choice, and it changes the pooled statistic relative to pooling raw scores, which is why the pooled atlas AUROC of Note S8 uses raw scores and is stated separately) exposed a steep, ordered gradient in discrimination. The composition of this analysis needs stating, because it is not the full atlas. Consequence annotation is keyed on OMIA variant identifiers, so human is excluded entirely: its positives come from ClinVar and carry no OMIA identifier, which removes both its 1,500 positives and its 1,500 negatives. The pooled negative set is therefore n = 5,510 across the same seven species, and consequence-annotated positives number 525 across those seven. The reason is the consequence annotation itself, which is taken straight from OMIA's curated Variant Effect field and not from any per-species annotator build: the annotation step invokes none. Human carries no OMIA record at all, and cattle carries none for this SNV set, so both species drop out of the positives and the negatives together rather than one side only. The four classes below account for 524 of those 525. The gradient is thus a seven-species result on both sides, and it excludes the only species with adjudicated clinical labels. Per-consequence AUROC, at the 1,001-bp single-token readout throughout this note, fell from nonsense/stop-gain (0.954, n = 112, 95% CI [0.919, 0.980]) to missense (0.896, n = 324, [0.868, 0.921]) to splicing (0.772, n = 70, [0.714, 0.832]) to regulatory variants (0.579, n = 18, [0.438, 0.669]), the intervals quoted throughout being the pooled variant-level ones, which are the wider of the two schemes the artefact carries in three of the four classes (splicing is the exception, 0.118 wide against the stratified 0.121) (2,000 bootstrap resamples). No submitted figure plots this four-class gradient: Figure 10b's strata are a coarser composition-matched partition and its legend says so explicitly, so it is a table result, not a figure one. Each class's positives are scored against their own species' negatives and the per-species AUROCs are combined as a positive-count-weighted mean, so no cross-species composition difference enters the comparison; the four values, their n, both interval schemes and the loss-of-function grouping are deposited. These four classes are reported separately, and the top cell is nonsense specifically, not loss-of-function as a whole: grouping nonsense with splicing into a single loss-of-function class (n = 182) gives an AUROC of 0.884 by the positive-count-weighted-mean identity below, which sits below missense, so a loss-of-function-versus-missense ordering does not hold and the monotone gradient is across the four separated consequence classes rather than a loss-of-function supergroup. The 525th annotated positive, unaccounted for by the four classes above, is a single stop-lost (extension) variant. The intervals matter as much as the point estimates here. The regulatory pooled variant-level interval is 0.231 wide and contains 0.5, so that cell alone does not establish a departure from chance (the species-stratified interval matching the point estimate is [0.504, 0.651] and would exclude 0.5, and we quote the conservative one) and its upper bound (0.669) sits below the lower bound of splicing (0.714), so the two percentile intervals do not overlap. That interval is also the least stable quantity in this paragraph: on 18 positives a percentile bootstrap moves in the third decimal between resample schemes, and the deposited artefact carries both the species-stratified and the pooled variant-level interval so the difference is visible rather than implicit. We do not lean on that: the regulatory interval is 16% narrower than the Hanley–McNeil analytic interval [110] for the same cell ([0.440, 0.716]), which is where a percentile bootstrap on 18 positives is least trustworthy, and under the analytic interval the ordering is not resolved. What the gradient establishes firmly is the separation between the coding classes and the regulatory one; the fine structure at the bottom is underpowered, which is why the independent eQTL panel below carries the regulatory claim. Pooled across the nonsense and missense classes, discrimination remained strong (AUROC 0.911); adding splicing to that pool gives 0.892, and we give both because against a common negative pool the pooled AUROC is exactly the positive-count-weighted mean of the class values, so the figure depends entirely on which classes are counted as coding. Regulatory discrimination (0.579) was barely above chance either way. Because the negative set was held fixed across classes, this gradient reflects the model's differential competence across variant types rather than differences in the null.

## Note S8. The reference-implementation readout: AUPRC, the cross-species share of ranked pairs, and the window-versus-aggregation split

At what we call, for brevity, the reference-implementation readout, the mean log-likelihood over an 8,192-bp window, which is the protocol implemented in the Arc Institute reference notebook for this model and matches the 8,192-token context of its first-stage pretraining as described in [19], and is the readout the concurrent cross-species study on these same species also adopts (Background; we use the term in that specific sense, and not as a claim about the majority of published evaluations), Evo 2-40B reached an unweighted mean AUROC across the nine species of 0.943, with a pooled value of 0.973 (95% CI [0.968, 0.978], variant-level bootstrap; n = 11,109 variants). Because AUROC is optimistic under the class imbalance of the eight OMIA panels (9% positives each, against 50% in human and 20.1% pooled) and for a screening use case, we also report the precision-recall summary: macro AUPRC is 0.854 against a 0.137 base rate, a 6.3-fold lift over no-skill, and pooled 0.940, with per-species values (0.735 in goat to 0.971 in human) in Supplementary Table S10. The discrimination is therefore not a base-rate artefact at this readout. The pooled figure should be read with care and we give it only alongside the species mean: of the 19,846,664 positive–negative pairs it ranks (2,237 positives × 8,872 negatives on the 8,192-bp panel), 83.0% are cross-species, so it is substantially a measure of whether the model separates one species' positives from another species' population variants, which is not the question this paper asks. That is a further reason, beyond panel-size dominance, why the unweighted species mean is our primary summary, alongside a per-species range from 0.974 in cattle to 0.863 in pig (Fig. 9a; human 0.974, dog 0.970, sheep 0.962, goat 0.959, chicken 0.954, horse 0.941, cat 0.890). Two of those deserve naming in the text rather than only in the legend. Chicken, the single deep anchor at ~319 My, reads 0.954, as high as the mammals, which is why the divergence test of Note S11 finds nothing to grade. And human attains 0.974 with the lowest effect size of any species on the score itself (Cohen's d = 1.09, where every other species falls between 2.35 and 3.65), because its positives, half its panel against 9% elsewhere, bring their long right tail into the pooled standard deviation; on the inverse hyperbolic sine scale of Figure 9b, which compresses that tail, human's d is 2.64, inside the other species' range of 2.26 to 3.16. We report the unweighted species mean as primary throughout: species are the unit of generalisation, and pooling allows the largest panels to dominate while letting per-species miscalibration cancel. On the same variants, the single-token variant-delta readout, logP(ref) − logP(alt) over a 1,001-bp window, gave 0.878 by the species mean and 0.881 pooled. The two protocols differ in window (1,001 vs 8,192 bp), flank, aggregation (single-token delta vs mean log-likelihood) and scorer, and changing them together is worth +0.065 AUROC by the species mean (95% CI [+0.032, +0.097], t on eight degrees of freedom; [+0.040, +0.092] by the species-clustered percentile bootstrap) and +0.092 pooled (95% CI [+0.083, +0.101], paired variant-level bootstrap; n = 11,109; per-species values in Supplementary Table S1). The effect is strongly heterogeneous across species (from +0.009 in goat and +0.015 in pig, where the readout barely matters, to +0.093 in chicken and +0.149 in human, a 17-fold range) so it is a real but species-dependent effect, not a uniform one, and the lower macro bound (+0.032) is less than half the pooled point estimate. We quote the species mean first because it is this paper's primary aggregation, and give both because the two differ by more than the width of the pooled interval and a single unlabelled figure would be ambiguous. That is a larger effect than most model-versus-model differences reported in this literature, and one that is invisible unless the scoring protocol is stated. On a 2,599-variant atlas subset we read the mean log-likelihood over the central 1,001 bp of the same 8,192-bp pass, which lets us separate the window from the aggregation contribution (Supplementary Table S9): the decomposition is path-dependent and its two orderings disagree, so we report both and the interaction rather than a single split, and the mean over the central 1 kb already captures most of the gain (details in Note S42 and Table S9); the point is that the protocol as a whole carries this much.

## Note S9. The eQTL permutation null, precision@1, and the rank-percentile chance level

On the 20,000-variant pig cis-eQTL panel the deviation of Evo 2's AUROC, 0.488, from chance is statistically resolvable, so what follows is not a failure to reject a null hypothesis, an outcome that low power would explain equally well. The permutation supporting that is run at 20,000 resamples, with labels permuted within eGene so the gene structure is preserved under the null; it returns p = 0.00025 (Monte Carlo SE 0.0001) against a permutation null whose mean is 0.4991 with SD 0.0034 (both deposited artefacts record it at 0.4991). A 2,000-resample permutation would carry a Monte Carlo SE of about 0.0004 on its own, too coarse for a number carrying this much weight, which is why we use 20,000. It is a negligible effect, and the sampling interval is narrow enough to call it negligible. That interval bounds sampling error only. Because we concede in Note S17 that four uncontrolled confounds could each move an AUROC by more than the 0.012 deviation we report, the magnitude claim is what survives (near chance, orders of magnitude below coding) while the sign of the deviation and its nominal precision are not claims we defend. The deviation from chance, |AUROC − 0.5|, is 0.012, against 0.378 for coding variants scored at the same 1,001-bp single-token readout (nine-species mean 0.878): more than an order of magnitude at a matched readout. We do not quote the ratio itself, because the denominator sits close to zero and the ratio is correspondingly unstable. Across the 1,430 eGenes that carry both a candidate causal variant and a control, within-eGene precision@1 was 0.224 against a per-eGene candidate causal base rate of 0.241. That difference is not resolvable: the binomial 95% interval on 0.224 at n = 1,430 is [0.202, 0.246] and contains the base rate, so precision@1 establishes the absence of usable signal rather than a deficit, and we do not read its sign. The rank percentile is defined per eGene as the fraction of that eGene's cis-variants scoring strictly below the candidate causal variant, with variants ordered by predicted deleteriousness. Higher is better: it approaches 1 when the candidate causal variant outranks every neighbour and falls to 0 when it ranks below them all. The denominator counts every cis-variant including the candidate causal one, which is why chance sits below 0.5 rather than at it: a variant cannot score below itself. For this panel's eGene-size distribution (mean 12.44 cis-variants across the 1,430 eGenes that enter the statistic, which hold 17,783 of the 20,000 variants; the remainder sit in eGenes that are all-positive or all-control and cannot contribute a within-gene ranking) the analytic chance value is 0.438, confirmed by within-gene label permutation at 0.4379 (SD 0.0048). That 0.438 is the mean of (n − 1)/2n weighted by candidate causal variants, which is the weighting the observed statistic uses; the unweighted mean over eGenes is 0.430 and is not the right null here. The chance value is a weighted mean of (n − 1)/2n over eGenes and not a function of the mean eGene size alone, so it is not recoverable by substituting the average size. The observed mean was 0.421, below the 0.438 chance value for this eGene-size distribution (not below 0.5, which is not the chance level here); the shortfall is small but reproducible (within-gene label permutation, p < 0.005). Both are consistent with a ranking that carries no usable causal information.

## Note S10. Limitations, stated in full

**Limitations.** (1) The advantage over free conservation depends on the readout: by the species mean it is +0.047 (95% CI −0.009 to +0.104) at the 1,001-bp single-token readout and +0.108 (+0.058 to +0.159) at 8,192 bp, a paired change of +0.061 (+0.027 to +0.095), and Evo 2 leads by sign in all nine species only at 8,192 bp. Where annotation and a conservation track already exist, the model adds +0.008 (+0.002 to +0.014) at 8,192 bp over impact and GERP together (Note S15). That advantage is itself composition-dependent, and this is the harder bound of the two: on the composition-matched missense stratum the two readouts read 0.799 and 0.812 on the same 1,285 variants, a difference of +0.013 (−0.017 to +0.042), so the readout gain is a property of the unmatched panel's composition and not of the matched comparison. (2) Cross-species calibration transfer is not a novel phenomenon (Note S48); it is measured and reported in Note S66, not in this note. (3) The Mondrian (class-conditional) conformal coverage reported in Note S66 is expensive: attaining roughly 0.97 coverage on zero-label species required abstaining on 50.5–65.7% of variants, and the less conservative near-singleton construction preserved marginal coverage while letting positive-class coverage fall to 0.222 in goat, 0.444 in pig and 0.571 in chicken, the first two under a coin flip on exactly the variants a clinician most needs to catch, and chicken barely above a coin flip. The coverage claim is therefore conditional on cross-species exchangeability rather than distribution-free, as Note S63 states. (4) Consistent with that caution, a cost-sensitive analysis under an asymmetric matrix found that a trivial always-abstain policy (pooled cost 0.500) beat every learned rule including our own (0.540). That result is pooled and prevalence-driven: macro, the Evo 2 conformal cell reads 0.489 and the raw-threshold cell 0.447, both below the 0.500 floor, so blanket abstention dominates under the pooled aggregation and not under the species mean. We report both because the pooled figure is the one that constrains a clinical claim and the macro one is the paper's declared primary; the selective layer's contribution either way is that it identifies which calls to withhold. (5) The regulatory evidence rests disproportionately on the eQTL panel, because OMIA regulatory positives are few (n = 18), and that panel is one panel in one species: the regulatory conclusion is not replicated in a second species anywhere in this paper. (6) The shared collapse on that panel establishes that the near-chance result is not unique to Evo 2. Separately, substituting Nucleotide Transformer through the same Mondrian pipeline (Notes S3 and S5) does not establish that abstention scales with backbone quality, and the intuitive expectation that a weaker backbone would abstain more did not hold. (7) The cross-clade bat arm is pilot grade rather than a matched replication of the cattle result: ten sequencing libraries, six individually sequenced bats and four DNA pools, not ten individuals, place a floor at a minor allele frequency of 0.05 only where all ten are called, and 1/6 ≈ 0.167 at the minimum allele number of 6, which is precisely the window in which the cattle relationship is itself null, so the two frequency axes are not the same quantity, and the efficacy of selection itself depends on effective population size and demographic history [111,112]. Its scope is set out in Note S26, and is narrower than recurrence: it shows a gradient of the same sign in a second clade with no curated resource, which neither transports the cattle effect size nor establishes that the cattle relationship recurs, and more sequencing depth would not change that, because the limitation is the number of libraries. (8) Two species carry fewer than 30 positives, so their intervals are wide and their point estimates should not be ranked against the others. (9) Panel size is confounded with alignment quality in these data, and the supervised baselines are point estimates without paired intervals. (10) Our positives are curated disease variants and our negatives are population variants (in human, ClinVar benign or likely-benign records) matched only on trinucleotide context, not on breed or discovery cohort, so a residual composition signal remains in every atlas number: what the panel measures is separation of catalogued positives from population negatives, and not pathogenicity within a matched sequence class. The size of that gap is visible in our own figures: at the same 8,192-bp readout the nested missense stratum of 1,285 variants in nine species reads a pooled 0.812, against a pooled 0.973 for the atlas it is nested in; the atlas's 0.943 is the nine-species mean over those same 11,109 variants, so it is not the number that falls. (11) The 85% coverage operating point is in-sample. It was located on the same pooled panel on which its benefit is then reported, so it is an empirically located knee and not a validated threshold, and a user adopting it should expect to relocate it on their own data. Selecting the knee on eight species and re-measuring on the ninth returns 85% coverage in all nine cases, and leaves no species worse off (Note S66). (12) The abstention layer's benefit is earned largely outside the species that carry most of the errors. Human contributes 61% of the pooled errors, and within human the confidence ordering is at chance on error detection (AUROC 0.474), though the same deposit records a human refusal lift of 1.71 [1.52, 1.93], an interval excluding 1, so the two statistics point in different directions and we quote the adverse one; the pooled improvement is not evidence that the layer would help most in the species a human-genetics reader would most want it to. (13) These reference assemblies are very likely in a pretraining corpus of roughly 9.3 trillion nucleotides; no variant-effect labels are, which is why we describe the scoring as zero-shot, but sequence familiarity may differ by species. (14) Label quality was audited on the human panel only, and the non-human positives were not stratified by evidence tier. (15) OMIA's causal variants were found largely by candidate-gene and coding-focused studies, so the atlas's positives are enriched for the coding, conserved variants such studies look for, which favours every score that reads coding sequence or conservation, the annotation baselines and GERP as well as Evo 2; the positives were not stratified by discovery route.

## Note S11. The 8,192-bp head-to-head against conservation, per species

At the reference-implementation 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood readout, the head-to-head on the co-scorable variants that is a near-tie at 1,001 bp (Note S12) resolves in Evo 2's favour: Evo 2 led GERP in all nine species (macro 0.939 against 0.831, a difference of +0.108, 95% CI [+0.058, +0.159], t on eight degrees of freedom; [+0.060, +0.162] by the paired species-clustered bootstrap; Supplementary Table S7). The inferential claim is the macro difference and its interval. Per-species intervals exist for this arm as well, and we give them here. DeLong intervals for the paired difference on the same co-scorable variants, in descending order of margin, are chicken +0.231 [+0.083, +0.380], goat +0.178 [+0.066, +0.290], cattle +0.149 [+0.113, +0.185], human +0.100 [+0.086, +0.114], dog +0.089 [+0.063, +0.114], sheep +0.086 [+0.022, +0.149], pig +0.075 [−0.087, +0.238], cat +0.045 [−0.002, +0.092] and horse +0.023 [−0.032, +0.077]. Six of the nine exclude zero, and seven of nine survive Benjamini–Hochberg on the one-sided test. These intervals treat variants as independent; with positives resampled by 100-kb locus, as for Table 2, five of the eight species where the test can be estimated exclude zero (cattle, chicken, dog, human and sheep) and the same five survive Benjamini–Hochberg, cat dropping out at a one-sided p of 0.051, while goat, whose eight GERP-scorable positives sit in one locus, cannot be estimated. Pig, cat and horse are the unresolved ones at the two-sided interval, so the nine-of-nine count is a sign count over point estimates and not nine resolved tests. The multiple-testing policy is this. Benjamini–Hochberg correction is applied to the nine-species Evo 2 versus conservation comparison, which is the family of tests carrying a headline claim. The readout head-to-head, the three-species phyloP comparison, the four-class consequence gradient, the twelve-estimator calibration sweep, the per-species selective-prediction operating points (per-species lift, sensitivity and the per-species leave-one-species-out intervals) and the per-species Mondrian (class-conditional) conformal results are reported uncorrected, because each is a descriptive breakdown rather than a family of hypotheses being screened for significance, and we draw no conclusion from an individual cell in any of them. We state the consequence of that choice plainly rather than let a reader over-read a per-species cell: at a Bonferroni threshold of α = 0.05/9 several individual per-species results would not survive, chicken's selective lift (already flagged not distinguishable from random refusal), goat's sensitivity (interval [0.000, 0.667]) and the narrower per-species leave-one-species-out intervals among them, which is exactly why the counting claims ("nine of nine", "seven of nine") are offered as descriptive summaries and the load-bearing significance rests on the corrected nine-species conservation comparison. Chicken contributes the largest single margin (+0.231) and is the species whose GERP track is not the same estimator as the mammals', so we report the comparison without it as well: the macro difference becomes +0.093 (Evo 2 0.937 against GERP 0.844), still favouring the model in all eight remaining species. Human, which trails conservation by 0.048 at the single-token readout, leads it by 0.100 here. The cause is the readout, not the panel. On the 9,529 variants carrying both an 8,192-bp score and a finite GERP value, moving Evo 2 from the single-token to the mean-log-likelihood readout raises its margin over GERP from +0.047 (leading 7 of 9 species) to +0.108 (9 of 9), while GERP itself, computed on that same set, is identical in both comparisons at macro 0.831. That +0.047 is a within-set margin against GERP, and is the same quantity as the +0.047 quoted in Notes S10 and S12, on the same 9,529 variants.
Nine tips will not support a phylogenetic mixed model, and seven of the nine are mammals sharing a single ~94-My divergence point, so we cannot give that margin a phylogenetically corrected standard error. We can ask whether it depends on counting close relatives as independent, and it does not. Collapsing the panel to one unit per order leaves the macro advantage over conservation within 0.0002 of its all-nine value of +0.108; it is positive in all five orders; and all eight one-representative-per-order choices fall between +0.095 and +0.124. The panel also shows no grading of discrimination with divergence from human (Spearman rs = −0.365, p = 0.334, n = 9; Figure S7a). That null is uninformative and is not evidence that phylogenetic signal is absent: with seven species tied at one point the test has almost no resolution, and chicken, the deep anchor at 319 My, reads 0.954, as high as the mammals. The regression is also not a like comparison across its range: the point at zero divergence is human, the one species whose negatives are clinically adjudicated rather than catalogued population variation, so the anchor of the abscissa differs from the other eight in construction as well as in distance.
Without goat, whose nine positives occupy two loci, the 1,001-bp margin, the 8,192-bp margin and the paired change are +0.032 (−0.019 to +0.083), +0.100 (+0.045 to +0.154) and +0.068 (+0.033 to +0.103). Averaged within the five taxonomic orders first, the 8,192-bp margin is +0.109 (+0.012 to +0.205), positive with an interval twice as wide. Those are t intervals over species, and over orders for the order-averaged margin. Holding the nine species fixed instead, resampling positive-bearing 100-kb loci whole and negatives individually within each species (B = 2,000, seed 61) and averaging each replicate over species, gives +0.047 (+0.014 to +0.082) at 1,001 bp, +0.108 (+0.079 to +0.139) at 8,192 bp and a paired change of +0.061 (+0.044 to +0.078), and +0.032 (−0.004 to +0.070) at 1,001 bp without goat. Goat's eight co-scorable positives sit in one locus, so its replicates resample negatives alone; its 1,001-bp margin, +0.173, is the largest at that readout, and it is the only species whose omission takes the 1,001-bp interval across zero (section 6c of recompute_atlas_strata.py, Additional file 3).
A windowed comparator. Evo 2's readouts read a window and GERP a single base, so the margins above could reflect the window alone. Averaging GERP uniformly over each variant's own 1,001-bp and 8,192-bp scoring windows, read from the same Ensembl tracks (Table S39), tests that, although the windows are symmetric while the single-token readout reads only the 501 bases upstream of the variant and the window-mean delta only the variant and the positions downstream of it. On 9,528 of the 9,529 co-scorable variants (one cat positive, whose identifier carries an accession outside the cat assembly, has no window value) GERP's species mean falls from 0.831 to 0.586 at 1,001 bp and 0.530 at 8,192 bp, a change of −0.301 (−0.439 to −0.163) at 8,192 bp, with 95% t intervals on eight degrees of freedom here and below. The window average reads below GERP per base in eight of the nine species; the exception is goat, whose eight GERP-scorable positives sit in one locus. Against the windowed averages Evo 2 leads by +0.292 (+0.188 to +0.397) at 1,001 bp and +0.409 (+0.289 to +0.530) at 8,192 bp. A comparator allowed the base and both windows together, the cross-validated logistic model of Note S15 on per-base GERP and the two window averages, reaches a species mean of 0.848, and Evo 2 at 8,192 bp leads it by +0.091 (+0.036 to +0.145) on the same variants. At 8,192 bp the window averages read below 0.5 in five species (chicken 0.371, horse 0.410, pig 0.465, cat 0.468 and dog 0.484), so they describe the tracks around these alleles and are not the comparator; per base, GERP reads 0.725 (chicken) to 0.915 (horse) in the eight OMIA species (Table 2). Regional conservation is a weaker baseline here than the base itself, so against the window averages the per-base margins are the conservative ones (tables/atlas_gerp_windows.parquet and sections 10 and 21 of recompute_atlas_strata.py, Additional file 3).

## Note S12. The 1,001-bp near-tie, its equivalence test, and the per-species split

Whether Evo 2 beats conservation on the variants both can score depends on the scoring readout, a dependence we have not seen reported for DNA foundation models against conservation, and one that changes the conclusion. Scoring-strategy dependence is itself well known on the protein side, where masked-marginal, wild-type-marginal and pseudo-likelihood scoring were compared directly and found to differ materially [113]; what is new here is that the choice decides whether a DNA model beats a conservation score at all. At the 1,001-bp single-token readout used for the reach, calibration and abstention analyses (Notes S14 and S44 to S54), the two are close: pooled, Evo 2 scores 0.8805 against GERP's 0.8780. The unweighted species-mean difference against GERP on the co-scorable variants is +0.047 (n = 9,529; Supplementary Table S7). Subtracting Table 2's two printed columns instead, Evo 2 on the full panel minus GERP on the subset it can score, gives +0.048 from the printed means, or +0.0484 as the species mean of the nine per-species column differences; that is the reach-mismatched comparison Table 2's own caption forbids, so the co-scorable within-set margin is the one we report. The +0.0485 tested in Note S34 is a different quantity, the reach-matched species mean over the 9,532 variants both methods can score; restricting Evo 2 to the co-scorable set moves every species' value, though only seven move at the precision Table 2 prints, pig most of all (0.848 to 0.814), and two small panels carry the mean (goat +0.173, chicken +0.137). Against a 0.02 smallest-effect-size-of-interest (SESOI), a two one-sided tests procedure (TOST) establishes neither superiority nor equivalence (p = 0.28; species-clustered 90% interval [−0.024, +0.056]; sensitivity to δ in Supplementary Table S5). That test is run on the pooled difference (+0.0024) with a species-clustered bootstrap, so the interval [−0.024, +0.056] and the p-value both belong to that pooled estimate resampled by species, as Note S2 states. The species-mean difference (+0.0485) is a different summary of the same comparison, tested in Note S34: its 90% t interval on eight degrees of freedom, [+0.003, +0.094], is not equivalent to zero at δ = 0.02 (TOST p = 0.86) and is one-sided superior (p = 0.041; 0.084 without goat). We name both rather than let one interval be read as belonging to the other. The equivalence verdict is the same under either estimand, since the upper bound exceeds δ = 0.02 in both. The two are different summaries of the comparison (on 9,529 and 9,532 co-scorable variants; the three-variant difference is cat, which contributes 1,034 variants to the head-to-head set and 1,037 to the equivalence set), and each is tested against δ with its own interval. Per species, on locus-resampled one-sided p-values over the eight species that can be estimated, Evo 2 beat GERP with Benjamini–Hochberg significance in cattle alone (+0.075) and was significantly worse in human (−0.048); goat's +0.173 rests on positives in one locus and has no locus-level p-value. The main text reports two-sided 95% intervals on the 9,529 co-scorable variants throughout; the one-sided test and the 9,532-variant set belong to the equivalence analysis of Note S34 and carry no claim of their own.

## Note S13. The conservation-matched control, and the residual it leaves

Because positives preferentially occupy conserved sequence whereas population negatives do not, part of this separation could reflect a conservation confound rather than pathogenicity per se. To isolate the pathogenicity component, we resampled negatives to match the GERP conservation profile of the positives (quantile-binned, 5:1, 40 resampling replicates; goat was excluded for having fewer than 10 eligible positives). Discrimination persisted under this conservation-matched control (negatives resampled with replacement to match the positives' GERP profile), with a mean AUROC of 0.834 across the eight remaining species, a drop of only 0.035 from the unmatched mean over those same eight species, 0.869 (this is the difference of the two printed values; on the unrounded means the drop is 0.034). Per species the two columns are, matched against unmatched on the GERP-scorable subset both are computed on, sheep 0.911 against 0.912, cattle 0.851 against 0.900, dog 0.846 against 0.895 and human 0.761 against 0.825, so the drop is negligible in sheep and reaches 0.064 in human. We give the eight-species unmatched mean (0.869) explicitly because the comparison must be like-for-like: subtracting Table 2's two printed Mean cells (0.878 − 0.834) would give 0.044, but that mixes a nine-species mean with an eight-species one, since goat is absent from the conservation-matched column, and is not the drop. This is consistent with a genuine pathogenicity component rather than solely a conserved-versus-intergenic confound. Two caveats belong with that reading. The first is a reach artefact that is easy to misread as a paradox. Sheep appears to gain from the control, rising from 0.903 in Table 2 to 0.911 matched, which removing a confound should not do. The two figures are not computed on the same variants: the matched value uses only the 451 sheep variants GERP can score, of 616 in the panel, and on that same subset the unmatched AUROC is 0.912, so matching in fact costs 0.001. The apparent gain is the easier scorable subset, not the control. The second caveat is the same defect stated generally: negatives can only be GERP-matched if they carry a GERP value, so the matched pool is drawn from the class-biased scorable subset quantified in Note S14 and Table S2. This control therefore cannot fully escape the defect it is testing for, and its per-species values should be read as within-subset contrasts, not as panel-wide accuracies. Across the eight species the matching itself is stable, varying by 0.002 to 0.008 AUROC over 40 resampling replicates (chicken 0.818 ± 0.008, pig 0.804 ± 0.003, sheep 0.911 ± 0.002, horse 0.846 ± 0.003, cat 0.837 ± 0.003, cattle 0.851 ± 0.006, dog 0.846 ± 0.002, human 0.761 ± 0.003); that spread measures the resampling, not the sampling uncertainty of the AUROC itself. Run at both readouts on the variants that also carry an 8,192-bp score, the same control lowers the eight-species mean AUROC by 0.031 at 8,192 bp and by 0.034 at 1,001 bp, so the readout gain survives it (section 9 of recompute_atlas_strata.py, Additional file 3).

## Note S14. The cause of class-dependent missingness, and what it costs the reach estimates

**The defensible test does not establish why the missingness is class-dependent: a consequence-matched Mantel–Haenszel analysis, which controls for consequence category, finds a positive class gap separable from zero in none of the five testable species, and in dog the matched gap reverses, while a cruder coding/noncoding stratification finds it surviving in four of the eight species in which it is defined (pig, horse, cat and cattle; goat has no stratum holding five variants of each class). We privilege the matched arm because the unmatched one confounds class with consequence.** The obvious explanation is that positives are overwhelmingly coding (85–100% across all nine panels) while negatives are sampled from population variation, so the class gap could be a coding-versus-noncoding artefact rather than a property of the conservation track. Stratifying by consequence category, with the Mantel–Haenszel risk difference [93] and a stratified Miettinen–Nurminen score interval [94,114], no species keeps a positive matched class gap separable from zero: horse falls from +0.489 marginal to +0.161 [−0.055, +0.514], cat moves from +0.194 to +0.239 [−0.153, +0.276] on a single intronic category in which all six positives are reached against 418 of 549 negatives, cattle reads +0.032 [−0.016, +0.112], and human, the only well-powered stratum, is a clean null at −0.003 [−0.028, +0.029]. Dog's +0.009 turns to −0.120 [−0.228, −0.037], but its categories disagree (score test of homogeneity 34.4 on 4 degrees of freedom) and the reversal comes from its intronic category, where 7 of 13 catalogued alleles are reached against 806 of 858 population variants, so it is a property of that category rather than a pooled effect. Only 5 of 9 panels have a matchable stratum at all. A further deposited test constrains the mechanism from the other direction and also returns a null: within class, the GERP-missing variants do not differ from the GERP-scorable ones in Evo 2 score (benign rank-biserial, pooled, +0.012 [−0.021, +0.046], p = 0.47; positive class +0.057, pooled across species, unlike this paper's primary species-mean aggregation, because several panels carry too few GERP-missing positives to support a per-species estimate), so, pooled, the unscorable variants are not a subpopulation the model itself distinguishes. For the benign class the species mean of the same statistic is −0.107, negative in eight of nine species, and the intervals exclude zero in cat (−0.166 [−0.242, −0.085]) and dog (−0.118 [−0.212, −0.022]), so in those two species the benign statistic is not null. Three things follow, and they pull in different directions. First, the framing: reach as we use it is a property of the deposited annotation for a species, not an intrinsic property of the conservation method (a differently built alignment would move it), so the deployment claim is about the tracks a user can actually download, which is the operative constraint but a narrower statement than "a property of the method". Second, the control is close to uninformative rather than reassuring: only 5 of 9 panels have a matchable stratum at all, and horse's interval [−0.055, +0.514] cannot distinguish +0.489 from zero, so "not separable from zero" here reflects power, not evidence of absence. Third, if the mechanism is in fact a coding/noncoding artefact, then the deployment penalty is a restatement of "positives are coding" rather than an independent property of conservation scoring, and we cannot exclude that. The marginal gap is real and its deployment cost is real; its cause is not resolved by these data, and we do not claim otherwise.

## Note S15. The orthogonal-contribution model and its two estimands

A logistic model combining the conservation score with the Evo 2 feature improved discrimination beyond conservation alone by a pooled ΔFM of +0.0202 (95% CI [+0.0132, +0.0279], a variant-level bootstrap matching this pooled variant-level estimand; the species-level estimand instead carries the species-clustered interval quoted below; n = 9,532 variants across nine species, 5-fold cross-validation, within-species standardised), a modest but real orthogonal contribution. The two scores are only weakly correlated (median per-species Spearman between Evo 2 and conservation +0.31) and Evo 2 discriminates where alignment-based conservation is weak (median AUROC 0.829 at low-conservation sites, a median over the eight species with a defined cell, the smallest of which rest on few positives), so the increment is complementary signal rather than a re-expression of constraint. This pooled variant-level value is the estimand we quote throughout; two species-level aggregations of the same quantity are larger, the median per-species ΔFM (+0.048 on the nine-species layer used for the other two values here; the deposited eight-species layer, which omits goat, gives +0.043) and the n-weighted mean (+0.054, species-clustered 95% CI [+0.039, +0.078]; the orthogonal-contribution decomposition, not the readout head-to-head of Table S7), and neither is interchangeable with the pooled value. The capacity ladder is descriptive, and its apparent size depends on the readout: on the matched 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood ladder, whole-atlas macro discrimination rose +0.075 from the 1B to the 40B checkpoint (0.868 to 0.943; the intermediate 7B is 0.928). The ladder is computed on the whole nine-species panel per checkpoint and not on a coding subset, so its 40B rung is the same statistic as the headline macro and not a second, independent measurement of it. All three checkpoints are scored on the same complete 11,109-variant panel, so the panel does not change between rungs. It is still a parameter ratio and not a controlled scaling sweep, as Note S30 says. The 1,001-bp readout shows a larger apparent gain (+0.146 human to +0.263 goat), which we do not treat as a capacity estimate: on that arm the 1B checkpoint was scored under a different aggregation protocol from the 40B, so the comparison mixes a capacity difference with a protocol difference. The matched ladder, where all three checkpoints are scored identically on the complete panel, gives +0.075, and neither of its steps is resolved by a paired species-mean t interval: +0.061 [−0.016, +0.137] from 1B to 7B and +0.015 [−0.003, +0.032] from 7B to 40B. Human alone makes the first of those intervals wide. The 1B-to-7B step is positive in all nine species (exact two-sided sign test, p = 0.004), and its locus-resampled 95% interval excludes zero in cattle, dog, human and sheep. In human it is +0.324, from 0.641 at 1B to 0.964 at 7B, and on the 752 variants of human's missense stratum (Note S23) 1B reads 0.562 against 0.862 at 7B and 0.865 at 40B; in the other eight species it runs from +0.007 (horse) to +0.061 (sheep). Species by species, with positive-bearing loci resampled (B = 2,000), the step is +0.029 in goat, whose two positive loci admit no interval, and +0.008 [−0.024, +0.040] in cat, +0.032 [+0.012, +0.053] in cattle, +0.034 [−0.036, +0.133] in chicken, +0.028 [+0.006, +0.049] in dog, +0.007 [−0.015, +0.023] in horse, +0.324 [+0.302, +0.344] in human, +0.025 [−0.017, +0.080] in pig and +0.061 [+0.018, +0.125] in sheep. Over the eight OMIA species the mean step is +0.028 [+0.014, +0.042], positive in all eight (p = 0.008). The 7B-to-40B step is positive in seven of the nine species, and its interval excludes zero in chicken, dog and human, but over the eight OMIA species it is +0.015 [−0.005, +0.035] and is not resolved (section 2b of recompute_atlas_strata.py, Additional file 3). The human 1B scores are themselves anomalous, and so, with them, are human's step and the width of the first step's interval: the median score of human positives is 0.00019 at 1B, against 0.0031 at 7B and 0.0036 at 40B, and 0.0011 to 0.0023 at 1B in the other eight species; their 1B and 40B scores rank-correlate at 0.06 (Spearman), against 0.33 to 0.92 in the other species; and the 40 human variants of Note S36's subpanel, scored at 1B by the atlas's own scorer, read 0.835 averaged over 1,002-bp windows (Additional file 2, analyses/results/strand_consistency_1b.json) but 0.650 over 8,192-bp windows, a fall of 0.185, while the other seven species with both classes move by less than 0.05. A second 1B implementation, the window-sum scorer of Note S36 (Additional file 3, tables/strand_ladder_evo2_1b_scores.parquet), reads 0.945 on those 40 and 0.8985 on all 327, against 0.848 for the atlas 1B; on the other 287 windows it reads 0.896 against 0.870, so the two implementations also differ outside human. Without human, the eight-species mean is 0.896 at 1B, 0.924 at 7B and 0.939 at 40B, a 1B-to-40B gain of +0.043 against the +0.075 above. Whether the human scores or the windows they were read from are at fault needs a rescoring at 1B; section 6 of recompute_strand.py in Additional file 3 recomputes every value of these three sentences but the 1,002-bp ones.
Three summaries of the increment over conservation appear in this file, and they answer different questions. The pooled ΔFM of +0.0202 above folds all nine species into one cross-validated model; the n-weighted mean of the per-species ΔFM is +0.054 [+0.039, +0.078] (Figure S3c); and the unweighted species mean, the paper's primary aggregation, is +0.071 [+0.029, +0.113] (t on 8 degrees of freedom) at the 1,001-bp readout and +0.108 [+0.051, +0.166] at 8,192 bp. The Analyses quote the species mean.
The same construction measures what Evo 2 adds to annotation. With the four-level snpEff impact ordering as the base, Evo 2 adds +0.032 [+0.016, +0.048] by the species mean at 8,192 bp and +0.027 [+0.013, +0.041] at 1,001 bp; with impact and GERP as the base, on the 9,529 co-scorable variants, it adds +0.008 [+0.002, +0.014] at 8,192 bp and +0.003 [−0.001, +0.008] at 1,001 bp, positive in eight of nine species at 8,192 bp. Holding each species out of its own model instead gives +0.024 [+0.013, +0.036] over impact and +0.005 [+0.003, +0.008] over impact and GERP at 8,192 bp. Once annotation and conservation are known, the likelihood adds little on this panel.

## Note S16. Consequence gradient in score magnitude, and why it is reported as a within-species separation

The gradient was mirrored in the magnitude of the deleteriousness scores the model assigned. Mean Evo 2-40B deleteriousness (n = 524, the same consequence-annotated positives as the AUROC gradient of Note S7) followed the same ordering (nonsense 6.863, missense 5.961, splicing 3.774), and fell to 0.282 for regulatory variants. With n = 18 and an AUROC interval that contains 0.5 we do not claim the model assigns regulatory variants almost no effect; we claim only that this cell is the lowest of the four and that the independent eQTL panel, not this cell, carries the regulatory result. These are raw Evo 2 deleteriousness means over each class, not standardised scores; the deposited artefact also carries a z-scored field, but it is standardised against all annotated positives (mean 5.661, SD 3.860) rather than against each species' negative pool, so neither field supports reading a class mean as a shift from the negatives in their own standard deviations. They are means, and for the coding classes they are outlier-driven, so the coding magnitudes are not on the same interpretive scale as the regulatory one and we do not compare them arithmetically; only the regulatory cell, at 0.282 against an observed AUROC of 0.579, is consistent with normality to the precision that reading would require. The comparison that makes 0.282 interpretable is the negative pool itself. These are raw deleteriousness means and the pool is not standardised: across the seven species carrying consequence annotation it has mean 0.0358 and standard deviation 1.6976. On that scale the regulatory positives sit 0.145 standard deviations above the negatives they are contrasted with, and nonsense 4.02. That is what "approaching neutral" means here, and it approaches the value expected for variants the model treats as neutral. In other words, Evo 2 does not merely rank regulatory variants poorly; it separates them from the negative pool by 0.145 standard deviations, which is the smallest separation of any class we measured. We put it that way rather than as a claim about absolute effect size, because the coding means on the same scale are outlier-driven.

## Note S17. Construction of the eQTL panel, and what the PIP-based control set is and is not

We assembled a 20,000-variant pig cis-eQTL panel from PigGTEx [75] (Sscrofa11.1; 100% reference-matched; genotypes imputed for RNA-sequencing samples), comprising 5,000 fine-mapped candidate causal variants (SuSiE-inf [76] posterior inclusion probability ≥ 0.9) and 15,000 within-eGene non-causal controls (PIP ≤ 0.001), both thresholds applied to the maximum of PIP across tissues for each variant–eGene pair. The control set is not neutral and we should say what it is. Selecting on PIP ≤ 0.001 within the same eGene selects variants that fine-mapping confidently excluded, which are systematically those in low linkage disequilibrium with the candidate causal variant or in regions carrying no association signal. That makes the contrast easier in one respect and harder in another, and it is not the same as a random within-gene control. The panel is not matched on allele frequency, and Evo 2 scores correlate with frequency by our own label-free analysis, but the two classes do not differ in frequency. Over the 5,457 unique RNA-sequencing samples in the genotype files of PigGTEx's 34 tissues (Science Data Bank, doi:10.57760/sciencedb.08089), which carry all 20,000 panel variants, candidate causal variants and controls have indistinguishable minor-allele-frequency distributions (median 0.241 against 0.243; Mann–Whitney p = 0.98), and frequency alone reads AUROC 0.500 (95% CI [0.487, 0.512]; eGenes resampled, B = 2,000). With each candidate causal variant matched to up to three controls within 0.005 in frequency (4,836 of the 5,000 matched), Evo 2 reads 0.487 (95% CI [0.478, 0.497]), and stratified by frequency decile, 0.487. Nor is the panel matched on GC content, repeat context, mappability or distance to the transcription start site. Each of these could move an AUROC by more than the 0.012 deviation we report, in either direction. One objection has to be met before this result can be read at all: the null on this panel is measured at the 1,001-bp single-token readout, while this paper reads its headline atlas values at the 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood readout. At that readout the result also holds: on the 2,000-variant consequence-matched subpanel, where 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood scores do exist, Evo 2-40B reads 0.498 (95% CI [0.473, 0.523]), indistinguishable from chance at the reference-implementation readout as well. The 20,000-variant panel itself is scored at the single-token readout only. It is also why the coding-versus-regulatory contrast is drawn at a matched 1,001-bp readout on both sides (Note S9). On this panel Evo 2 reached an AUROC of 0.488 (95% CI [0.478, 0.497]; n = 20,000; permutation p = 0.00025). Each record's distance to its eGene's transcription start site is deposited for the 19,341 whose eGene the Ensembl gene models carry (analyses/results/eqtl_tss_distance.parquet, Additional file 2). The allele frequencies are not deposited: analyses/scripts/build_piggtex_maf.py in Additional file 2 rebuilds them from PigGTEx's genotype release, which it identifies by MD5, and they agree with the frequencies PigGTEx reports for its significant eQTLs (Science Data Bank, doi:10.57760/sciencedb.09233) to within 3 × 10⁻⁸ in all 144,136 variant–tissue pairs the two share; analyses/scripts/maf_stratified_auroc.py recomputes the values above and checks them against analyses/results/eqtl_maf_matched_auroc.json.

## Note S18. The label-free conditional analysis, and how it differs from the eQTL result

This analysis is exploratory: its frequencies come from imputed genotypes over tissue samples, some of them repeated tissues of one animal (Note S31). Conditioning the cattle frequency–deleteriousness gradient (Note S31) on conservation marks the same competence boundary without using a single label, which is a weaker claim than recovering the eQTL-panel null and is the one the Discussion makes (Supplementary Table S6). Partitioning the same variants into phyloP quintiles (reach 97.2%, 27,234/28,006), the frequency–deleteriousness relationship survives at fixed conservation in 4 of 5 quintiles: at the same phyloP, rarer variants still score as more deleterious, so the gradient is not merely a conservation artefact. But its strength increases monotonically with conservation (ρ = −0.018 [−0.044, +0.011] in the least-conserved quintile, rising to −0.118 [−0.143, −0.093] in the most), and its confidence interval crosses zero in the least-conserved quintile, where conservation itself carries little signal. The model adds information beyond conservation where conservation itself has signal, and goes flat in neutral or unconserved sequence. This is a boundary of the same shape as the one on the eQTL panel, observed in a different species and from population data instead of curated candidate causal variants. Three qualifications keep it honest. The analysis is free of disease labels but not of annotation: it is conditioned on phyloP quintiles, and phyloP is an alignment-derived annotation, so "label-free" here means free of curated pathogenicity calls, not free of external information. The least-conserved quintile is also mostly neutral sequence, not regulatory sequence, so a model that assigns no fitness gradient there may simply be correct; we therefore read the flattening as a limit on where the score is informative and not as a demonstrated regulatory failure. Alignment-based constraint may under-mark fast-turnover regulatory elements, which is a reason to treat phyloP quintiles as a proxy for constraint and not for regulatory status. We are careful about what these two arms jointly establish. They are not independent measurements: both are Evo 2 scores, and the boundary is drawn by phyloP in one case and by SuSiE PIP in the other, so what differs is the label source, not the measurement. They also differ in score definition, readout and label semantics, so this is not a reproduction of the eQTL result in a second species. The relationship between allele frequency and deleteriousness that the label-free arm relies on is the one on which population-genetic inference of the distribution of fitness effects is built [115].

## Note S19. Panel construction, the unfiltered negative set, and the MAF-threshold sensitivity check

We then asked whether Evo 2-40B distinguishes catalogued disease- or trait-associated alleles from putatively benign population variation in species with sparse curated labels. "Putatively benign" carries a real caveat: in the eight non-human species the negatives are population variants, from Ensembl variation in seven and from the PigGTEx v0 genotype panel in pig, with no minor-allele-frequency threshold applied, so those negative sets are contaminated at an unknown and species-dependent rate by low-frequency and genuinely deleterious alleles. That raises a confound this paper is otherwise careful about: disease alleles are overwhelmingly rare, Evo 2's score correlates with allele frequency by our own label-free analysis (Note S18), and a frequency-unmatched negative set could therefore inflate discrimination without any pathogenicity signal. We tested it in cattle, the one species for which a population allele-frequency panel is available here (44.5 M variants over the 7,394 samples of the FarmGTEx cattle imputed-genotype release [105]), by restricting the negatives to progressively rarer strata and recomputing. Against negatives carrying any frequency, discrimination is 0.915 at the 1,001-bp readout and 0.984 at 8,192 bp; restricted to MAF ≤ 0.005, rarer than roughly 55% of the frequency-carrying negatives, it is 0.911 and 0.984. The change is 0.004 and 0.000, well inside the resampling intervals. Discrimination is flat across frequency, so the atlas result is not a frequency effect in the species we can check. Two honest limits: only 723 of cattle's 1,880 negatives carry a frequency in that panel, and only 51 of its 188 positives do, because curated disease alleles are largely absent from a population sample, which is the reason the control has to be run this way round rather than by matching positives to negatives. Because sequencing depth and cohort size differ by species, so does the contamination, and it will act to depress measured discrimination rather than inflate it. For each of nine vertebrates (human, dog, cat, cattle, pig, sheep, goat, horse and chicken), we scored catalogued positives against matched negatives: trinucleotide-matched population variants from Ensembl [71] in seven species and from the PigGTEx v0 genotype panel in pig, and ClinVar Benign/Likely_benign variants in human. Positives come from two sources, which we distinguish throughout: OMIA [53] for the eight non-human species (9–227 positives each, at a 10:1 negative-to-positive ratio), and ClinVar [54] for human (1,500 Pathogenic/Likely_pathogenic variants at 1:1), because OMIA by design excludes human [53]. The atlas totals 11,130 variants (2,240 positives).

## Note S20. The BRCA1 positive control in full, and the start-codon failure mode it exposes

Before applying the model where no ground truth exists, we established that the scoring harness reproduces a known answer where it does. We scored 3,893 BRCA1 single-nucleotide substitutions, assayed by saturation genome editing as cell-survival depletion at the endogenous locus [50], zero-shot with Evo 2-40B, using no BRCA1 labels at any stage (Figure S6). Variant-level discrimination of loss-of-function from functional and intermediate variants reached an area under the receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve (AUROC) of 0.874 (95% confidence interval [CI] 0.858–0.889, genomic-site-clustered bootstrap; this interval is not wider than the Hanley–McNeil analytic interval assuming full independence, [0.858, 0.890], so clustering by genomic site does not materially inflate the uncertainty here and the interval should not be read as accounting for more dependence than an independence assumption would), and per-residue predicted deleteriousness tracked the measured phenotype at Spearman ρ = +0.52 ([+0.43, +0.59], n = 326 residues), concentrating on the RING zinc cage and the BRCT phosphopeptide-binding cleft. The nearest external reference is the Arc Institute notebook for this protocol, whose printed output is a zero-shot BRCA1 AUROC of 0.73 for the 1B checkpoint on the LOF-versus-FUNC/INT split of all 3,893 substitutions. We name that source precisely, because it is a notebook output rather than a figure in reference [19], and because [19] reports BRCA1 under a different decomposition (coding and noncoding SNVs scored separately), which is not compared here. We therefore make no claim about what the published paper does or does not report for the 7B and 40B checkpoints, and our 40B result of 0.874 is not a matched reproduction of that reference, which is for a different checkpoint. What the control checks is that the harness, with its windowing, offset, strand and sign, separates loss-of-function variants at this level zero-shot; read at the single token the same panel gives 0.632 (Note S30). It also exposes one readout-specific failure that recurs throughout this paper. Start-codon loss at residue 1 is invisible to a mean-log-likelihood score, because deleting a start codon barely perturbs the likelihood of the surrounding sequence.

## Note S21. The scope of the three eQTL-panel controls
