Three controls, described in Note S4 (scale across a 40-fold parameter range, window length at fixed readout, and a panel on which every sequence and conservation scorer collapses together), make it hard to attribute the near-chance eQTL result to one model, one panel or one protocol. What they cannot establish is the complement. With the |z| statistic excluded as circular, the only non-circular positive control on this panel is the TSS-distance baseline reported in Note S4 (AUROC 0.651 on n = 19,341), which is informative but modest, so "the model is blind to regulatory variation" and "these labels are not learnable from sequence" are both consistent with this panel. The labels carry recoverable signal, which is why we do not treat the two readings as equally supported, but 0.651 is modest enough that the second cannot be dismissed either. A supervised linear probe of Evo 2-1B embeddings (deposited) separates them by a negligible margin (gene-grouped AUROC 0.52, 95% CI [0.508, 0.531]); the interval excludes 0.5, so this is a detectable effect of negligible size and not a null, and at the 1B checkpoint the weak separation is not confined to the zero-shot likelihood readout; the probe is of the 1B checkpoint and is not extended to 40B. The honest summary is that no scorer we tested separates these labels, not that no scorer could. One study using a different methodology reaches a convergent conclusion: probing the representations of pre-trained genomic language models offers no substantial advantage over one-hot-encoded sequence baselines on cell-type-specific regulatory tasks [17]. The neighbouring references [116,117] address related but distinct questions, supervised expression models and realistic sequence generation, and we cite them as context rather than as convergent evidence. The probing study [17] evaluates learned representations under supervision where we evaluate zero-shot likelihoods, so the agreement is not a shared artefact of protocol; taken together these results locate the gap in what current pre-training extracts from the noncoding genome, a limitation that recent reviews and probing studies also emphasise [18,118,119]. We are careful about the scope of our own version of the claim: it concerns what a zero-shot autoregressive likelihood readout recovers, not what such architectures can be trained to do, and supervised regulatory models do predict eQTL effects.

## Note S22. Scope of the eQTL-panel null across checkpoints and architectures

Against the atlas result sits a sharp and systematic limit. Evo 2's competence falls from nonsense variants (AUROC 0.954) to near-chance discrimination on the panel of fine-mapped pig cis-eQTLs (0.488 on 20,000 variants; at a matched readout the coding effect is larger by at least an order of magnitude, though we do not quote a ratio, because the denominator sits close to zero). We state the scope rather than the class: the same model reads noncoding Mendelian disease and trait alleles at 0.852 at the 8,192-bp readout (the atlas's non-coding stratum, Figure 10b, pooled and also by the mean within the five species with at least eight non-coding positives; sections 4b and 21 of recompute_atlas_strata.py, Additional file 3) and its own paper reports zero-shot caQTLs at 0.58 and DNase QTLs at 0.66, so this is not blindness to noncoding sequence in general, and on this panel a capability gap cannot be separated from labels that may not be learnable from sequence. A peer model shares the limit. Across a confounded 1B-to-40B checkpoint comparison, the point estimate did not improve. Population allele frequencies mark the same boundary with no disease labels at all, though we read that arm as a limit on where the score is informative rather than as independent corroboration of a regulatory failure. Together those three observations point beyond a single checkpoint. We are deliberately careful about how far: the evidence is one autoregressive family across a 40-fold parameter range plus one masked-language comparator (Nucleotide Transformer, the 500M-parameter multispecies checkpoint used throughout and labelled NT-500M in Supplementary Table S4), which is not a survey of a model class. Nucleotide Transformer also falls outside the scope of our stated claim, since that claim is about zero-shot autoregressive likelihood readouts and NT is not autoregressive. What we can say is that the limit is not a defect of one checkpoint and is not closed by scale within this family; whether it is a property of DNA foundation models in general, or of likelihood readouts specifically, or of pre-training corpora that under-represent regulatory grammar, these data cannot separate. Independent evaluations of DNA foundation models, by probing their learned representations and by zero-shot likelihood tests, approach the same question from other directions [120,121,122,123].

## Note S23. What the atlas measures, and the composition controls behind that statement

How the model is read out proved to matter as much as which model was used, so we state the readout with every number in this paper. Positives are catalogued disease- or trait-associated OMIA alleles in the eight non-human species and ClinVar pathogenic variants in human, which are 85–100% coding in all nine species; negatives are population variants, drawn from pools that in sheep, chicken and pig do not reach every chromosome (Note S62), and matched on trinucleotide context (in human, ClinVar benign or likely-benign records). Trinucleotide matching controls mutational context. It does not control genomic region, gene density, coding status or GC content, so a model that had learned only to recognise coding sequence would score highly on this task. Measured, such a model is not outperformed by the headline: a one-bit coding flag reaches 0.944 by the mean of the eight OMIA species, against Evo 2's 0.939 at 8,192 bp, and the four-level snpEff impact ordering 0.958 over all nine species against 0.943 (Table 2). This trinucleotide-only matching is narrower than the frequency-, GC- and mappability-matched negatives the study's own design set as its target: in practice trinucleotide context is matched exactly (per-species χ² p = 1.0, by construction on mutation type) but GC is not matched (positives run about 0.07 higher in GC than negatives in eight of nine species, human excepted and reversed, so a GC-only classifier still separates the classes at a macro AUROC of 0.67; see the composition control below), frequency is checked in cattle, the one species with a population allele-frequency panel, and mappability is not matched. These are shortfalls against that design we state rather than leave implicit, and the residual GC signal is the same coding/composition confound the pooled missense stratum, 0.812, isolates. The headline below is therefore a measure of the separation of catalogued positives from population negatives, which is the construction this literature standardly uses and the one that makes our numbers comparable to published ones, not a measure of pathogenicity discrimination within a matched sequence class. The closest estimate of the latter that we can offer is the pooled missense stratum, 0.812, reported below, and it should be read per species, as below, rather than as one number. Its composition should be read with it: that stratum pools 1,285 missense variants, by the first term of their annotation as the Methods define it, across nine species, 752 of them human and 1,039 of them positives, so the figure is itself pooled and human-dominated. Some 41% of the pairs it ranks are within-species. We report both because they answer different questions; the headline figure should not be quoted without the matched one beside it. Within consequence class the separation annotation supplies is gone. Over the positive–negative pairs that share a species and a first snpEff term, pooled over species by pair count, the non-human panels read 0.751 at 8,192 bp and 0.651 at 1,001 bp against 0.638 for GERP, and human reads 0.851 and 0.811 against 0.838; those pairs are 2.8% of the non-human pairs and 4.9% of the human ones. The missense stratum is small per species: at 8,192 bp, with variants and negatives in parentheses, they read 0.804 in chicken (20, 3), 0.854 in pig (26, 2), 0.757 in sheep (38, 1), 0.812 in horse (56, 8), 0.896 in cat (77, 3), 0.672 in cattle (155, 36), 0.873 in dog (152, 24), 0.865 in human (752, 167) and 0.643 in goat (9, 2), and only cattle and human hold enough negatives to be read as estimates.
Read per species, cattle's missense AUROC reads 0.744 at 1,001 bp and 0.672 at 8,192 bp, and human's 0.832 and 0.865, neither change resolved: −0.072 (−0.177 to +0.034) and +0.033 (−0.002 to +0.066), loci resampled. Human holds 92.3% of the stratum's within-species pairs, and weighted by precision across the species with at least 20 missense negatives, cattle, dog and human, the change is +0.003 (−0.130 to +0.136). On the variants GERP also scores, human's missense row in Table S35 gives a gain of +0.036 (+0.001 to +0.072), whose lower bound clears zero by less than the Monte Carlo error of its 2,000 resamples.
Table S35 pools its within-class pairs over species by pair count, and the eight OMIA species' pairs are mostly dog's (48.9%), cattle's (32.2%) and cat's (13.5%). Read species by species, Evo 2's within-class margin over GERP at 8,192 bp is +0.168 in dog, +0.138 in cattle, −0.053 in cat, −0.015 in horse, −0.146 in chicken, +0.137 in sheep and +0.644 in pig, the last on 160 pairs. Weighted by precision across these seven species with at least 100 pairs, by random-effects meta-analysis on locus-resampled standard errors (between-species variance by restricted maximum likelihood, Hartung–Knapp–Sidik–Jonkman interval with Röver's modification), the margin is +0.045 (−0.113 to +0.204) and the readout gain +0.065 (−0.006 to +0.135), against the pair-weighted +0.121 and +0.095 of Table S35: read per species, the within-class margin is smaller and not resolved. recompute_macro_ci.py prints every species' cells with their locus-resampled intervals.

## Note S24. The head-to-head at both readouts, restated for the Discussion

Held to the same-variant standard, the comparison changes with the tested scoring protocol. At the 1,001-bp single-token readout, pooled Evo 2 and GERP estimates on 9,532 co-scorable variants were 0.8805 and 0.8780; a species-clustered TOST on the pooled difference established neither superiority nor equivalence, and on the species mean the margin, +0.0485, is one-sided superior (p = 0.041) but not equivalent at δ = 0.02. At the 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood readout, the macro point estimate favoured Evo 2 in all nine species and the macro difference was +0.108 [95% CI +0.058, +0.159], t on eight degrees of freedom. This establishes protocol sensitivity in these panels. It does not show that one readout is universally standard or that a named published result is overturned, and it does not remove residual composition, clustering or reach uncertainty.

## Note S25. The readout as a decision variable: the comparisons that reversed

In the panels audited here, changing the scoring readout changed the Evo 2-versus-conservation comparison. The cattle and human phyloP comparisons of Table S8 illustrate the dependence, each reversing sign between the readouts, while chicken does not show the same reversal. We attribute no specific published conclusion to the single-token readout. On the 9,532 co-scorable variants, the pooled 1,001-bp comparison established neither superiority nor equivalence under the species-clustered interval, and the species-mean margin is one-sided superior (p = 0.041) but not equivalent; at 8,192 bp the macro point estimate favoured Evo 2 in all nine species. These results make readout and reach necessary reporting variables for this study's comparisons. They do not establish that the larger readout is universally optimal, that every prior comparison is ill-defined, or that reach accounting alone identifies the better predictor.

## Note S26. The bat pilot, and why its lowest frequency bin is an allele-count floor

This arm is pilot grade, cross-clade and is not a matched replication of the cattle result: the bat panel rests on ten libraries, an allele number of at most 20, so its MAF floor is 0.05, exactly where the cattle result is null, and no additional sequencing depth can fix a limit set by the number of libraries. To test whether the relationship extends beyond the labelled clades, we repeated the analysis in a wild bat (Myotis lucifugus), a lineage in a different laurasiatherian order from cattle, Chiroptera against Cetartiodactyla, and with no curated variant resource, and recovered a monotone frequency–deleteriousness relationship (ρ = −0.125 [−0.138, −0.113], p = 2.0 × 10⁻⁸⁴, though computed over only 32 distinct minor-allele-frequency levels, so this p-value overstates the evidence and the effective sample size is far below the 23,888 windows). Ten libraries — six individually sequenced bats and four DNA pools — bound the allele count at 20, so the lowest frequency observable anywhere in the panel is 0.05, and the effective floor is higher wherever fewer libraries are called (1/6 ≈ 0.167 at the minimum allele number of 6). Because four of the ten libraries are DNA pools, that allele number is an upper bound and this axis is an ordinal rank rather than a frequency. Taking the permissive 0.05 bound: the bat panel contains no rare variants at all, 60.0% of the cattle panel (16,812 of 28,006) lies below that floor, and within the bat's own frequency window the cattle relationship is a clean null (ρ = −0.008, p = 0.38). The two frequency axes are therefore not the same quantity. An allele seen once across ten libraries is biologically rare but numerically scores 0.05, whereas a cattle variant at 0.05 really is at 5%, so the bat's low bin is an allele-count floor rather than a matched frequency. This establishes something narrower than recurrence: a gradient of the same sign in a lineage with no curated resource.

## Note S27. Panel completion at 8,192 bp, and the class-dependence of the 21 exclusions

Of the 21 excluded variants, 18 lie within 4,096 bases of a contig end, where no full 8,192-bp window exists. They are not evenly distributed: horse loses 15 of the 21, cat 3, and goat, cattle and dog one each. Cat's three are not at contig ends: checked against the cat assembly, each carries a reference allele that disagrees with the assembly base, two matching it only on the opposite strand and one with reference and alternate swapped, so the builder's reference check is what dropped them. Nor are they class-random, which is worth flagging because it is a miniature of the reach effect this paper quantifies: all three of cat's excluded variants are positives (against cat's 9% positive rate; exact test of the null that exclusion is independent of class, p = 0.0008, uncorrected for selecting the most extreme of the five species that lost variants), so the very step that builds the 8,192-bp panel drops positives preferentially in that species, an effect negligible in magnitude here (3 variants) but the same class-dependent missingness the reach section shows for conservation scores. They are retained in the 1,001-bp analyses, which is why the full panel is 11,130 and the 8,192-bp panel is 11,109. At 1,001 bp cat reads 0.846 with them and 0.848 without. Charged at one half for every pair that touches the 21 variants it leaves unscored, Evo 2's own must-answer penalty at 8,192 bp is +0.003 by the species mean. Several 8,192-bp figures are deliberately computed on smaller matched panels: the 2,599-variant readout decomposition (Table S9), the consequence-matched strata (Table S14), the 2,000-variant eQTL subpanel, the 9,529-variant conservation head-to-head (Note S11 and Table S7) and Table S8's 5,075. The analyses that follow (the conservation head-to-head, the calibration and abstention layers, and the reach accounting) are reported on the full nine-species panels at the 1,001-bp variant-delta readout, where discrimination is consistent but moderate: mean AUROC 0.878 across species (goat 0.951, sheep 0.903, cattle 0.900, dog 0.888, horse 0.880, chicken 0.861, pig 0.848, cat 0.846, and human 0.825). Every comparison in this paper is internally matched on readout and on panel: the four composition strata of Figure 10b compare the two readouts on the variants of each that carry both (11,109, 3,189, 7,920 and 1,285 of 11,130, 3,191, 7,939 and 1,286), as do the macro and pooled readout effects. No comparison mixes the two readouts, and effect sizes measured under one readout should not be compared against the other.

## Note S28. Comparing predictors within gene, and what that changes

Every head-to-head between two variant-effect predictors in this literature, including ours, faces the same structural problem: variants are not independent within a gene, and two predictors with different reach are therefore compared on different genes. A pooled comparison answers "which predictor ranks this collection of variants better", which is not the question a user asks. The question is closer to "for a variant in this gene, which predictor should I believe", and the two can disagree.
We measured how often they disagree. Across all 1,176 pairs drawn from the 49 dbNSFP predictors, computed on the 202,643 missense variants of the 328,328-variant dbNSFP panel, 115 pairs (9.8%) reverse the sign of their difference between a pooled comparison and one computed within gene and then aggregated with genes weighted by their pair count. Of those 1,176 pairs, 815 (69.3%) shift by at least 0.01, and 724 of them (61.6%) shift by at least 0.01 without reversing, which is larger than many margins reported as improvements. On the whole-panel version of the same comparison the reversal rate is 8.6%, so the effect is not an artefact of restricting to missense variants.
On this panel AlphaMissense against REVEL gives a difference of −0.0100 pooled and +0.0101 computed within the 3,143 genes carrying both classes among the 189,301 variants both can score: the sign flips, and with it the answer to which of the two is better. The same pair behaves the same way on the human ClinVar missense panel, where the matched difference moves from −0.0096 pooled to +0.0124 within gene and the must-answer difference from −0.0357 to −0.0044. Those four ClinVar figures come from a deposited gene-clustering check that uses 300 bootstrap draws rather than the 2,000 used elsewhere here, so they should be read as indicative. We name both panels rather than pooling them, because the two are different variant sets and the point is precisely that the set decides the verdict.
Interval width moves as much as the point estimate. A bootstrap resampling genes rather than variants gives [−0.0421, −0.0298] for the pooled must-answer difference on that pair, against [−0.0368, −0.0347] from the closed-form calculation that treats variants as independent: 5.8 times wider. Any comparison at this panel size will produce intervals that exclude zero if variants are treated as exchangeable, so an interval alone is not evidence that a difference is real.
Three consequences follow, and we apply all three to ourselves. We report no ranking of the 49 predictors. Every interval on the dbNSFP panel in this paper comes from a gene-clustered bootstrap, and the gene key is canonicalised first, because an uncanonicalised key fragments 2.46-fold and the fragmentation correlates with reach, which narrows the intervals it is used to compute by a median of 27%. And where we do report a per-predictor quantity, it is one that does not require comparing two predictors to each other: reach, must-answer accuracy, and the class gap are each properties of a single predictor on a fixed panel. The gene-balanced drop quoted in the Analyses is a fourth such quantity. For each predictor it is the pooled AUROC over every variant that predictor scores, minus the unweighted mean of its within-gene AUROCs, one per gene, over the genes carrying at least five variants of each class. The one-review-star filter that defines the reach panel is applied first, so both terms sit on the same panel: the 328,328 variants, or, for the value the Analyses quote, the 202,643 missense ones. Of the whole panel's 15,606 genes, 2,418 carry five of each class, and the number entering a given predictor's mean runs from 1,611 to 2,418, because the predictors score different genes; of the missense subset's 15,389 genes, 1,203 carry five of each class, and the number runs from 1,052 to 1,203. The two terms are therefore computed on different variant sets and under different aggregation, and their difference is not a bias in a fixed estimand: on the whole panel it is negative for three of the 49 predictors, reaching −0.0399. It carries no interval.
The gene-balanced drop does not measure gene-level circularity, and this panel shows the gap. The within-gene estimator runs only over genes carrying at least five variants of each class, 2,418 of the 15,606 on this panel: the 13,188 it never sees hold 28.3% of the panel's variants and contribute nothing to the drop, and on the missense subset 78.7% of genes are single-class and hold 36.1% of the variants. The median drop is therefore neither an estimate of the gene-level effect nor a bound on one: both the evaluated variant set and the gene weighting change between the two terms, so the difference has no fixed estimand to bound. Nine of the 49 scores are label-free conservation tracks, which cannot be circular with respect to ClinVar gene labels because they never saw them; they carry a larger median drop than the other forty, 0.0438 against 0.0280. A pooled-to-within-gene difference on a score that cannot be retrained confounds circularity with the between-gene component of any constraint signal, and on this panel that component dominates.
The drop bears on the gene-level kind of circularity only, without measuring it; overlap between a predictor's training variants and these labels is not corrected here. Two contrasts can be measured on the panel itself, only the second of which bears on variant-level leakage: with its allele-frequency feature BayesDel leads its frequency-free version by +0.0072 [+0.0067, +0.0077] on the 314,475 variants both score, and VARITY_R and VARITY_ER lead their leave-one-out versions by +0.0017 [+0.0016, +0.0019] and +0.0005 [+0.0004, +0.0006] on 180,062, all with genes resampled.

## Note S29. Table 2's intervals: dependence structures the percentile bootstrap ignores

Table 2's per-species intervals are locus-clustered two-sample bootstraps at both readouts, B = 20,000, resampling the positive-bearing 100-kb loci as whole blocks and the negatives individually. An unstratified paired variant-level percentile bootstrap is deposited alongside as a reproduction control. The operating point a prioritisation would use is a fixed false-positive rate: the lowest score that at most that share of a species' negatives reach. At 1%, the 8,192-bp readout recovers 0.532 [0.371, 0.812] of cattle's positives, 0.543 [0.468, 0.659] of human's, 0.714 [0.548, 0.917] of chicken's, 0.746 [0.316, 0.899] of horse's, 0.750 [0.559, 0.875] of pig's, 0.811 [0.688, 0.920] of cat's, 0.821 [0.640, 0.950] of sheep's and 0.859 [0.787, 0.906] of dog's. At 5% it recovers between 0.786 (chicken) and 0.947 (cattle), and at 1% the 1,001-bp readout recovers between 0.470 (human) and 0.768 (sheep). Goat, with 89 negatives, has no 1% operating point, and its 5% value, 0.778, is descriptive. The intervals are the same two-sample locus-clustered bootstrap (B = 2,000). Beside each of these operating points stands that of a consequence filter, which keeps the variants whose first snpEff term is HIGH or MODERATE. It flags 2 of goat's 89 negatives, 3 of chicken's 280, 2 of pig's 360, 1 of sheep's 560, 9 of horse's 695, 3 of cat's 1,240, 38 of cattle's 1,879 and 25 of dog's 2,269, and at those rates it recovers 1.000, 0.857, 0.833, 0.964, 0.887, 0.877, 0.926 and 0.841 of the positives, against 0.444, 0.750, 0.722, 0.625, 0.859, 0.738, 0.904 and 0.863 for the 8,192-bp readout; the means are 0.898 and 0.738. With loci resampled and no correction over the eight species, the difference excludes zero only in sheep, +0.339 [+0.075, +0.906], and narrowly in cat, +0.139 [+0.009, +0.395], and with 1 to 38 flagged negatives per species these operating points are coarse. In human the filter flags 174 of the 1,500 benign records (11.6%) and recovers 0.973 of the positives, against 0.968 for the 8,192-bp readout.
Positives are catalogued disease- or trait-associated alleles and cluster at the locus scale; the trinucleotide-matched negatives do not. Counting a locus as a 100-kb bin, the positives of the nine panels occupy 2 (goat), 20 (chicken), 28 (pig), 38 (sheep), 36 (horse), 83 (cat), 147 (cattle), 187 (dog) and 944 (human) loci. Kish's effective cluster count is lower still wherever one locus dominates: 1.2 in goat, where eight of nine positives lie within a 570-bp span on chromosome 13 (46,469,788–46,470,358) and the ninth is a single variant on a separate contig, and 6.7 in horse, where 26 of 71 positives share one 100-kb bin. Negatives are effectively unclustered, at most two or three per bin in seven species. Re-binning at 1 Mb changes these counts marginally (goat 1.2, horse 6.6, cat 46.9), so the result does not depend on the bin width. Table 2 therefore reports a locus-clustered two-sample bootstrap at both readouts, resampling the positive-bearing loci as whole blocks and the negatives individually, at B = 20,000. Two other estimators were computed for comparison and are deposited: a variant-level percentile bootstrap, and a cluster-robust DeLong interval whose positive-side variance uses a sandwich over loci and whose multiplier is Student's t on G − 1 degrees of freedom. The three agree closely wherever the effective cluster count is large — cattle, dog and human move by less than 1.11× under any of them — and diverge exactly where it is small. Cat widens by 1.51× at 8,192 bp, to [0.813, 0.964], the largest single correction in the table. At 1,001 bp cat widens 1.25× and horse 1.46×, reproducing under a differently constructed estimator the 1.241× and 1.503× of the 2,000-replicate all-loci block bootstrap deposited in Additional file 2. Where a cluster estimator returns a narrower interval than the variant one, as it does for horse at 8,192 bp and marginally for pig there and for chicken at 1,001 bp, the printed interval takes the wider endpoint of the two: a correction introduced to acknowledge dependence is not a licence to narrow. Even so, where the effective count is small, horse's 6.7 above all, then chicken's 14.0, sheep's 21.5 and pig's 23.1, no resampling estimator here is reliable, and those intervals are likely too narrow. Leaving out each species' most frequent gene shows how far one locus carries a point estimate: at 8,192 bp horse reads 0.959 without its 26 KIT positives, against 0.941 with them, while the impact ordering falls from 0.951 to 0.937, so the two swap order; cat reads 0.922 without its 9 scored CMAH positives, against 0.890, and chicken 0.949 without its 5 MC1R positives, against 0.954. Without its 8 PRNP positives goat keeps one, so it has no such value.
Goat is not estimable and we decline a cluster-aware interval rather than print one (Table S1 prints its unclustered interval for completeness only). Its nine positives sit in two loci, so the positive class admits three distinct resamples; across 20,000 replicates the locus bootstrap returns 99 distinct AUROC values, and an all-loci block bootstrap discards 13.3% of replicates as containing no positive, which is why that scheme returns an interval narrower than the uncorrected one rather than a wider one. The cluster-robust interval, the only estimator that makes the shortage visible, rests on one degree of freedom (t₁ = 12.706) and spans [0.770, 1.000] at 1,001 bp and [0.758, 1.000] at 8,192 bp. Those are statements that goat's discrimination is unquantified, not intervals. We report goat's AUROC as a descriptive point estimate throughout, which matches the em dash its conservation-matched cell already carries.
The GERP and conservation-matched columns of Table 2 carry no intervals, and that is deliberate rather than an omission. The GERP column is computed on a different variant set in each species, for the reason the table notes give, so a paired interval taken across the two accuracy columns would not be meaningful.
Two provenance points complete the table. The point estimates throughout are the recompute-layer values, and each 1,001-bp AUROC is computed directly on its own panel rather than derived by subtracting the readout delta from the 8,192-bp figure, so where Table 2 and Table S3 score the same panel they print the same value: sheep reads 0.903 on its 616 variants in both. And pooled across species the model adds signal beyond conservation, the increase in AUROC when the Evo 2 feature joins a cross-validated logistic model already carrying the conservation score being +0.0202 (95% CI [+0.0132, +0.0279]; n = 9,532); the per-species increments are positive in all nine species, from +0.024 in pig to +0.180 in goat, and the deposit carries no per-species interval or p-value for them, so no significance statement is available for this quantity. The pattern of two significant species and a significantly negative human belongs to the head-to-head Evo 2-versus-conservation comparison, not to this one.

## Note S30. Scoring provenance: checkpoints, window extraction and per-species FASTA handling

Notes S30 to S35 hold extended Methods, grouped by the Methods subsection each one extends. Notes S41 and S42 also hold extended Methods; a few paragraphs appear in both sets, and where they overlap the two copies agree.
The protocol is identical across checkpoints on the 8,192-bp arm, which holds the scoring protocol fixed; on the 1,001-bp arm the 1B checkpoint was scored under a different aggregation from the 40B, so that comparison mixes capacity with protocol and we do not read it as a capacity estimate (Note S15). They differ in training data as well as in parameter count, and [19] describes the 1B release as short-context, so the ladder is a parameter ratio rather than a controlled scaling sweep. The scores were produced on cloud GPUs rented from RunPod, the 8,192-bp atlas on NVIDIA H200s, in a container built from nvcr.io/nvidia/clara/bionemo-framework:2.6.2 (image digest sha256:10b6f398295b399047a6a328e05ab4aa2bf09da839a865da877f19d32213a24b) with the evo2 package installed; the archived build of that container carries evo2 0.6.0, vortex (vtx) 1.1.0, PyTorch 2.7.0a0 (NVIDIA release 25.04), TransformerEngine 2.2, CUDA 12.9, cuDNN 9.9 and Python 3.12, and Additional file 2 deposits its recipe under container/. The 8,192-bp mean log-likelihoods come from src/ccs/score_evo2_meanll.py, which for the atlas loads each checkpoint whole on the GPU and calls evo2's score_sequences. The 1,001-bp single-token 40B scores, for the atlas and for the splicing and ClinVar panels, come from src/ccs/score_evo2_40b_local.py, which streams the model's blocks through the GPU and reads the next-token log-probability at the variant; the 1B and 7B panel ladders of Note S39 use the same scorer. The 1B and 7B window sums of the splicing and ClinVar consequence panels, and the strand subpanel's 1B rung of Note S36, come from analyses/scripts/score_mfass_evo2.py in the BioNeMo runtime, which calls BioNeMo's predict_evo2 in FP8 and falls back to bf16 at batch size 1 without recording which ran, on a NeMo checkpoint passed in its CKPT variable whose revision was not recorded. The code of the 40B passes that wrote the strand subpanel's and the readout decomposition's scores (Notes S36, S37 and S42) is not deposited.
**The nine-species atlas is gated separately, on its built windows.** Its deposited window files carry no ref_ok column, and the concordance matters because OMIA deposits coordinates against several assembly versions and a reference/alternate swap would invert the sign of logP(ref) − logP(alt) for the affected variants. We therefore verified it directly, without needing the reference FASTAs: each window file carries ref_seq, alt_seq and the variant offset, and each variant identifier encodes chromosome, position, reference and alternate allele, so the three conditions that matter can be checked against each other. For all 11,109 variants in all nine species, the base at the variant offset in ref_seq matches the reference allele in the record: 100.0%. One window is nonetheless degenerate in a different sense: in one of the 11,130 records the alternate allele equals the reference base (n_window_ref_eq_alt = 1), which the build-match gate does test for, since its third condition requires exactly one differing position between the reference and alternate windows and a ref-equals-alt record produces none. That figure is guaranteed by construction rather than a cross-check: the builder discards any window whose central base disagrees with the record's reference allele, and the check then reads the builder's output, so it cannot fail. It records that the filter ran. Concretely, the record's reference allele is parsed from the per-species OMIA source records at panel construction (which reads the *_omia_pos files), whereas ref_seq is the unmodified FASTA window, so neither is derived from the other; the gate therefore catches an assembly or coordinate mismatch and any reference/alternate swap that disagrees with the genome, since for a single-nucleotide variant a swapped reference no longer matches the FASTA base. The one failure it cannot exclude from the deposited window files alone is a source record whose reference allele had itself been rewritten from the genome upstream, a circularity ruled out by that construction script, not by the deposited windows, and the raw source records sit outside the deposit only for size (Additional file 2). The other two conditions we verified, that alt_seq carries the alternate allele at that offset and that the two windows differ nowhere else, also pass at 100.0% but are true by construction: alt_seq is built as ref_seq with the alternate allele substituted at the offset. We report them for completeness and do not present them as independent evidence. The check is a deposited check whose output is also deposited; it exits non-zero if any species falls below 100%, so it can gate a rebuild. Windows were extracted by random access into a .fai-indexed FASTA, avoiding any dependence on samtools in that step. The trinucleotide matcher that draws the negatives does call samtools faidx, and it runs in a separate conda environment from the pip analysis layer, so samtools is a load-bearing external dependency of panel construction even though the window reader has none.
For each variant we extracted a 1,002-bp genomic window from the appropriate reference assembly, 501 bases upstream of the variant, the variant base itself, and 500 downstream (W = 1002, HALF = 501), and computed the next-token log-softmax at the variant position given the reference prefix. The prefix is therefore 501 bases, not 500. We name this arm the 1,001-bp readout throughout, which is the nominal centred window and one base short of what is extracted (the splicing and ClinVar consequence panels of Note S35 are the exception, with genuinely 1,001-bp windows that put the variant at index 500 after 500 bases of left context); because the single-token score is read from the last position of the prefix, the trailing base is never seen by the model and the extra upstream base is the only difference. Only the left half of that window enters this readout. The model is autoregressive, so the probability at the variant position is conditioned on the preceding 501 bases and the 500 downstream bases are never read. The window is therefore centred as constructed but effectively 501 bp of left context as scored, and the same holds at the other single-token window sizes: the 2,049-bp rung supplies 1,024 bases of left context. This is why the window-length comparisons in the Analyses and Note S4 should be read as a left-context ladder rather than as a symmetric context ladder, and it is a weaker claim than the phrase "window size" suggests. The 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood readout is not affected: it averages over every position in the window, so the bases downstream of the variant enter it, each conditioned on the variant allele, while the positions upstream of the variant are identical in the two windows and add nothing to the difference. The variant-effect score was defined as the reference-versus-alternate log-likelihood difference, score = logP(ref_base) − logP(alt_base) = −(log P(alt) − log P(ref)), so that higher scores denote a more disfavoured alternate allele and greater predicted deleteriousness. The signed score was used throughout, including the regulatory eQTL analysis, oriented so that higher values indicate greater predicted deleteriousness. Reference alleles were verified base-for-base against the source assembly before scoring (the ref_ok build-match gate), which reached 100% concordance on both the cattle selection panel and the pig eQTL panel. That gate is enforced at panel-construction time for the 8,192-bp panels and for the pig negative sets, which filter on it explicitly. For the 1,001-bp panels it is computed and written as a column but not used as a filter, and two builders set it unconditionally, so a reader should treat it as a recorded diagnostic on those panels and as an enforced gate only where we have just said it is enforced. The 100% figure above therefore records that the surviving 8,192-bp panel is build-consistent; it is not a pass rate over all candidate variants. Across the eight OMIA-dense species the curated candidate positives numbered 828 (dog 283, cattle 193, cat 126, horse 72, sheep 69, pig 39, chicken 30, goat 16, from the OMIA source records; the 824 of Note S31 is the same set counted after four records that carry no single-nucleotide coordinate are dropped) and 740 survived to the final panel (dog 227, cattle 188, cat 125, horse 71, sheep 56, pig 36, chicken 28, goat 9), an overall attrition of 88 (10.6%). The dominant exclusion reasons were failure of the reference-allele build gate, non-SNV records, and the absence of a symmetric scoring window at a contig end. The attrition is load-bearing in one place: goat falls from 16 to 9 positives, below the ten-positive threshold the conservation-matched control requires, which is why goat is excluded from that control.
**Which variants carry an 8,192-bp score, and why.** Every atlas figure at 8,192 bp in this paper is computed on the completed 11,109-variant panel or on a subset of it, and the 1B and 7B checkpoints are scored on the same panel (Note S27). The 1,001-bp single-token variant-delta readout covers the full nine-species panels and is used for the reach, calibration, abstention and selection analyses; it and the 8,192-bp readout are different scores of the same variants, and differ by +0.065 AUROC by the species mean. As a positive control, the human BRCA1 (GRCh37) saturation-genome-editing set was scored at 8,192-bp mean log-likelihood and reached AUROC 0.874 (Analyses). The Arc Institute reference notebook prints 0.73 for the 1B checkpoint under the same protocol (a notebook output, not a published figure); that is a different checkpoint, so it sets a scale rather than testing the harness, and on our own ladder the whole-atlas macro runs 0.868 at 1B, 0.928 at 7B and 0.943 at 40B, and 0.896, 0.924 and 0.939 without human, whose 1B scores are anomalous (Note S15), so a 40B-versus-1B gap should not be read as a gap over 7B. Additional file 3 carries the per-variant 1B and 7B scores, so each rung recomputes. A single-token sweep of the same set gives AUROC 0.632 at 1,001 bp and 0.655 at 2,049 bp, correct in sign but below the mean-log-likelihood protocol, reported here only to document the readout sensitivity, not as the control. The human population and ClinVar variants are on GRCh38; the BRCA1 assembly differs because the saturation-editing coordinates are published on GRCh37.
Case and sign. Both scorers upper-case every window before tokenising, so soft-masked repeats are read as ordinary bases. The atlas table's evo2_meanll_8192 and the BRCA1 table's evo2_meanll_delta are mean_LL(alt) − mean_LL(ref) and must be negated to give logP(ref) − logP(alt), while the eQTL table's evo2_40b_score is already oriented to deleteriousness and is used as stored; negating the wrong one gives 1 − AUROC.

## Note S31. Panel provenance: assemblies, normalisation, the provenance ledger and the reference-concordance audit

**Cross-species disease atlas.** The atlas comprises 11,130 variants (2,240 positives, 8,890 negatives) across nine species, assembled from two different positive sources, which we state explicitly because the distinction matters for how the panel should be read. For the eight non-human species (goat, chicken, pig, sheep, horse, cat, cattle, dog), positives were catalogued disease- or trait-associated alleles from OMIA [53] (9, 28, 36, 56, 71, 125, 188 and 227 positives respectively, 740 in total). OMIA is not equivalent to ClinVar in evidence standard, and this matters because the paper sets an OMIA panel and a human ClinVar benchmark side by side. OMIA is literature-curated, many entries rest on a single small study, and its evidence field is a Pathogenicity Classification rather than a review-status or assertion-criteria rating, so it is populated but not directly comparable to ClinVar's stars, and the OMIA panels are not stratified by evidence level. OMIA now ships a per-variant AVCG pathogenicity classification in the same download, the veterinary analogue of the American College of Medical Genetics and Genomics/Association for Molecular Pathology (ACMG/AMP) system, but across the 824 panel-species records it is populated for 86: 45 Pathogenic, 20 Likely pathogenic/Pathogenic, 9 Likely pathogenic and 12 of uncertain significance or outside the classification's scope, with 738 (89.6%) not currently evaluated. A P/LP-only rerun would leave 74 positives across eight species and is not a panel this design can carry, so the field records the confound without removing it. The difference in label quality between the two is a confound we can name but not remove, and it runs in the direction of making the OMIA panels noisier, not cleaner. Positives were matched against trinucleotide-matched population negatives at a 10:1 negative-to-positive ratio, drawn from Ensembl variation [71] in seven of the eight non-human species (release 110 for goat, cattle and horse, 109 for sheep, 104 for chicken and dog, and 114 for cat) and, for pig, from the PigGTEx v0 genotype panel on Sscrofa11.1, which supplies real common segregating SNVs rather than Ensembl-catalogued ones and which we state separately because a reader following the Ensembl route could not reconstruct the pig negatives (exactly 10:1 in seven species; cat is 1,240 negatives against 125 positives, i.e. 9.92:1, because ten trinucleotide-matched negatives could not be drawn). OMIA by design excludes human, covering animal species other than human, mouse, rat, zebrafish and western clawed frog [53], so the human arm could not come from OMIA and was built separately: positives are 1,500 ClinVar [54] Pathogenic/Likely_pathogenic single-nucleotide variants on GRCh38, matched 1:1 against 1,500 ClinVar Benign/Likely_benign variants rather than 10:1. Human is therefore the one species whose negatives are clinically adjudicated rather than catalogued population variation, which matters when the human arm is compared with the other eight. We re-verified this provenance directly against the ClinVar GRCh38 variant call format (VCF) file: all 1,500 human positives are present in ClinVar, 1,495 of them classified Pathogenic, Likely_pathogenic or Pathogenic/Likely_pathogenic (the remaining five are one Uncertain_significance, one Conflicting_classifications, one Pathogenic/Pathogenic-low-penetrance, one Pathogenic|other and one Pathogenic|drug_response). By review status, 138 of the 1,500 human positives carry no review star and 1,357 one or more; the five mixed or uncertain records are kept because the panel is deposited as drawn, and at 0.3% of the class they cannot move its AUROC by more than 0.003. The human panel therefore differs from the other eight in both label source and class balance. Because AUROC is rank-based, the 1:1 balance does not by itself inflate human discrimination; it does affect every prevalence-sensitive quantity (calibration error, conformal coverage, pooled aggregation), which is why species-mean (macro) aggregation is the primary summary throughout this paper and why pooled statistics are reported alongside it and not in place of it. Reference genomes were those against which the source variant coordinates are deposited, which is not the same as the newest assembly available. Four are superseded in Ensembl: chicken GRCg6a has been succeeded by GRCg7b, dog CanFam3.1 by ROS_Cfam_1.0 and UU_Cfam_GSD_1.0, sheep Oar_rambouillet_v1.0 by ARS-UI_Ramb_v2.0, and cattle ARS-UCD1.2 by ARS-UCD1.3 and then ARS-UCD2.0. We used the builds matching the OMIA and Ensembl records rather than lifting coordinates over, since a lift-over would introduce its own error into a panel whose build-match is already the weakest link. For dog, where the deposit also contains a lift-over route and a second assembly, we verified which was used rather than assert it: 355 of 355 sampled dog windows reproduce base-for-base from CanFam3.1 and none of them from ROS_Cfam_1.0, so the published dog panel is CanFam3.1 throughout and the lift-over script contributed nothing to it. The assemblies were: cattle ARS-UCD1.2 [124], pig Sscrofa11.1 [72], sheep Oar_rambouillet_v1.0 [125], goat ARS1 [126], horse EquCab3.0 [127], cat F.catus_Fca126_mat1.0, chicken GRCg6a (GCA_000002315.5) [128] and dog CanFam3.1 [129,130]. All were stored locally and .fai-indexed. Two of those citations are approximate and we say so rather than let the bracket imply otherwise: no dedicated assembly publication is available for the cat build, and the assembly described in [131] is Felis_catus_9.0, a different one; and [128] is the original chicken genome publication rather than a description of GRCg6a specifically. The same holds for the dbNSFP and PLINK references, which are cited in the main-text Methods rather than in this note: they describe v4 and the second-generation release, while we use 5.3.1a and PLINK2. We cite the originating publications throughout because no release note exists for the specific versions used.
**Human ClinVar benchmark.** Generalisation of the reliability layer to established frozen scores was assessed on ~1.6 M ClinVar variants using CADD, REVEL and AlphaMissense, with isotonic calibration fit under 4-fold cross-validation on ~150,000 variants and a coding/noncoding region split. A circular Evo 2-embedding probe present in the source data (EVEE; near-perfect by construction) was identified and excluded from every model-agnostic claim. The benchmark file is the EVEE Zenodo shard (10.5281/zenodo.19701997). Having found one leaked probe we checked every other scored column in that release against the labels; none reached a comparable correlation. Two further caveats on this stratum. The coding/noncoding split follows the region annotation carried in that source file rather than a VEP consequence call against a named transcript set, so the boundary is inherited and not defined here. And REVEL and AlphaMissense are missense-only predictors, so the 391 and 397 variants they carry in the noncoding stratum are annotation inconsistencies in the source file rather than genuine noncoding predictions; that is a further reason not to build any claim on this stratum.
**Independent regulatory panel (PigGTEx cis-eQTLs).** The panel draws on pig cis-eQTLs from PigGTEx [75] v0 SuSiE-inf [76] fine-mapping (SuSiE-inf extends the SuSiE framework [77] and its summary-data form [78]) (Sscrofa11.1; 32 tissues), using the posterior inclusion probability (PIP) as the candidate causal label. The fine-mapping was taken from the PigGTEx v0 release file PigGTEx_v0.finemapped_eQTL. The 32 tissues are those carrying SuSiE-inf fine-mapping in that file, one .susieinf shard per tissue and no DAP-G shard among them, which is fewer than the tissue set the resource paper describes. Fine-mapping quality varies with per-tissue sample size and we pooled across tissues without a per-tissue breakdown, so a tissue-level heterogeneity in label quality is possible and unmeasured here. The panel comprised 20,000 variant records: 5,000 high-confidence candidate causal variants (PIP ≥ 0.9) and 15,000 within-eGene non-causal control variants (posterior inclusion probability ≤ 0.001). Both thresholds are applied to the maximum of PIP across the 32 tissues for each variant–eGene pair, so the two classes are selected from opposite tails of one statistic and are not symmetric: a positive needs to reach 0.9 in any one tissue, which is a maximum over correlated tests and admits a winner's-curse false positive, while a control must stay at or below 0.001 in every tissue. Contamination of the positive class pushes discrimination towards chance, which is the direction of our own headline null, so this construction works in favour of the result we report and we flag it as such. The controls are drawn from the eGenes that carry the positives, so that candidate causal and control variants share genomic context. The 5,000 candidate causal variants fall in 3,160 eGenes, 1.58 per eGene, and the 15,000 controls in 1,430 of those, 10.49 per eGene. Every control is drawn from an eGene that also carries a candidate causal variant; the converse does not hold, so 1,730 eGenes carry a candidate causal variant and no control, and 2,217 of the 5,000 candidate causal variants (44.3%) are matched only at the panel level. Within-eGene matching is therefore complete for 55.7% of positives and absent for the rest, which we state because the panel's difficulty rests on that construction. The within-eGene precision@1 and rank-percentile statistics are computed on the 1,430 two-class eGenes only and are unaffected. We checked whether that reflects per-tissue duplication of the same variant–gene pair, and it does not: the panel contains 20,000 distinct variant identifiers and 20,000 distinct variant–gene pairs, so no record is repeated. The repeats within an eGene, 1.58 candidate causal variants and 10.49 controls on average, are distinct variants rather than duplicated records, and variants within one eGene may be correlated through linkage disequilibrium. We measured what that correlation costs instead of assuming it: resampling eGenes instead of variants, the correct unit under within-gene linkage, gives a 95% interval of [0.479, 0.497] against [0.478, 0.497] at the variant level, a width ratio of 0.99; the score is oriented so that higher values are more deleterious, so an AUROC below 0.5 means candidate causal variants scored below their controls, and the deposited artefact records this comparison in that convention. Clustering does not materially widen the interval here, because the panel spans 3,160 eGenes in total (more than the 1,430 that also carry a control, because 1,730 carry a candidate causal variant and no control), far too many clusters for the small-cluster problem to bite. That the candidate causal variants are therefore only partly matched to controls from their own eGenes is a residual confound of gene identity with label, which we note here and cannot remove. The effective sample size is below 20,000 in principle and the interval is unaffected in practice. We give both figures, not only the convenient one. These 15,000 controls are not linkage-disequilibrium partners of the candidate causal variant: no LD statistic was computed, and selecting on low PIP if anything excludes the candidate causal variant's credible-set neighbours, making the near-chance result a conservative test. Discrimination was evaluated as AUROC of the signed, deleteriousness-oriented score (candidate causal versus non-causal), within-eGene precision@1 against the per-eGene candidate causal base rate, and the mean candidate causal rank-percentile, with a coding contrast at the identical 1,001-bp single-token readout and score column to distinguish a genuine regulatory failure from a scoring artefact. The conclusion does not depend on this readout choice: ranking instead on the unsigned magnitude |score| gives AUROC 0.496, also indistinguishable from chance. A separate consequence-matched 2,000-variant subpanel was built for the matched contrast in Figure S2, matching candidate causal variants and controls on consequence category; its discrimination at the 8,192-bp readout is reported in Note S17.
**Label-free selection panels.** The cattle panel comprised 44.5 M variants over 7,394 samples on ARS-UCD1.2 (equivalent to bosTau9), read from PLINK2 allele-frequency tables [100]. It is the FarmGTEx cattle imputed-genotype release [105]: genotypes imputed for samples that are mostly RNA-sequencing libraries, some of them repeated tissues of one animal, so its frequencies are over samples rather than individuals and are least accurate in the rarest bin, and we treat the analysis as exploratory. It comprises 7,394 samples (geno_pgen/*.psam) whose identifiers span four sequence archives, 6,298 distinct accessions in the NCBI Sequence Read Archive (SRA) (SRS…; SRS2408617 appears only as its six suffixed technical replicates, SRS2408617_1 to _6, with no bare row, giving 6,303 SRS-prefixed rows), 817 ENA (ERS…), 97 CNGB (CRS…) and 17 DDBJ (DRS…), with a remainder of 160 tissue-coded laboratory identifiers carrying no archive accession, yielding 44.5 M biallelic variants on ARS-UCD1.2 (3,065,707 on chromosome 1 alone; both counts are taken from the cohort VCF). This panel does not match the Daetwyler et al. 2014 resource [132] (234 bulls, 28.3 M variants). The sample identifiers are in the deposited sample table (built from the PLINK2 .psam files) for anyone who wishes to reconstruct it; 7,234 of the 7,394 are public SRA, ENA, CNGB or DDBJ sample accessions and the remaining 160 are the aggregating project's own library identifiers, which we state as such. Variants were stratified-sampled across five minor-allele-frequency bins (ultra-rare, rare, low, common, major) to 28,006 windows, with purifying selection serving as the label-free ground truth. Two controls that the atlas has are absent here. The selection panel is not trinucleotide- or mutational-context-matched, even though the atlas is matched precisely because the score depends on context, and rare variants differ systematically from common ones in mutational spectrum (CpG fraction, transition/transversion ratio); a context effect alone could produce a frequency–deleteriousness gradient. And the rarest alleles are the most genotyping-error-enriched class in any short-read panel, with errors falling preferentially at sites a sequence model finds surprising (repeats, low-complexity and paralogous regions), which would generate the reported gradient with no selection involved. Our lowest bin is ultra-rare rather than singleton, admitting any variant below a frequency of 0.002 and averaging 0.00062 against 6.8 × 10⁻⁵ for a true singleton, so this concern reaches it in weakened form. The data allow one of these alternatives to be tested directly. On mutational spectrum: collapsing to the six pyrimidine-referenced mutation classes, composition does shift with frequency, C>T falls from 41.4% of the rarest quintile to 34.7% of the commonest while T>C rises from 25.3% to 32.5%, the largest swing being 8.6 percentage points (T>C, maximum minus minimum across the five quintiles), so the concern is real. But the relationship survives within every class, with Spearman ρ from −0.077 (C>T, n = 10,795) to −0.159 (C>G, n = 2,297), all six of the same sign as the pooled −0.099 and all at p < 10⁻⁶, and five of the six within-class coefficients are larger than the pooled one. Composition shifts; controlling for it does not remove the gradient. The exception, C>T, holds the hypermutable CpG transitions, and its weakness is concentrated there. Read from the ARS-UCD1.2 reference, whose base matches the deposited reference allele at all 28,006 sites, 4,351 variants lie at CpG sites and 3,762 of them are CpG transitions. Within the CpG transitions ρ is −0.055 [−0.087, −0.023] (p = 7 × 10⁻⁴), and for C>T outside CpG it is −0.088 [−0.113, −0.065]. Evo 2 scores CpG transitions as far less deleterious than other C>T changes (mean −0.605 against +0.484), and their share falls from 13.9% of the rarest quintile to 11.5% of the commonest, so if anything they dilute the gradient: without them the pooled coefficient is −0.113 [−0.126, −0.100]. Across full trinucleotide context, the 96 pyrimidine-referenced classes, each holding at least 43 variants, ρ is negative in 91 of the 96, with an n-weighted mean of −0.102 against the pooled −0.099. On genotype quality: the allele-frequency tables record an observed allele count of 14,788 (two for each of the 7,394 samples, as imputed genotypes with no missing calls give) at essentially every site in this panel. The correct reading of a constant observed count is that this column carries no information about call rate, not that call rate is uniform, so a filter built on it is a no-op and cannot certify genotype quality either way, leaving ρ unchanged to four decimal places at any threshold. One limit of the first control: it stratifies an unmatched panel rather than matching one, so it shows that context composition does not produce the gradient, not that context plays no part in the score. With no genotype-quality filter applied, the genotyping-error alternative is not excluded, and because the genotypes are imputed, imputation error, largest for the rarest alleles and dependent on local sequence context, is a further alternative these tables cannot test. The cross-clade recurrence check used a wild little brown bat (Myotis lucifugus, Myoluc2.0, GCA_000147115.1; the assembly has no dedicated publication and was produced by the Broad Institute mammalian sequencing effort whose constraint analysis is [133]): whole-genome sequencing reads from BioProject PRJNA624023 (10 libraries) were mapped and joint-called. Those ten libraries are not ten individuals, and this bounds the arm further: the BioProject comprises six individually sequenced bats (two each from Pennsylvania, New York and Upper Peninsula Michigan, ~7.5x) and four equimolar DNA pools of multiple individuals (~45x). A joint call treats every library as one diploid genotype, so for the four pooled libraries the resulting allele counts are not counts of chromosomes in the sense the allele-number threshold assumes, and the allele number of 20 that we describe as ten called diploids is an upper bound on a quantity the design does not actually deliver. The frequency axis for this arm should therefore be read as an ordinal rank rather than an allele frequency, which is the direction the arm was already qualified in, and biallelic segregating SNVs with allele number ≥ 6 were exported to an allele-frequency table, of which 23,888 windows were scored under an SFS-lite construction. No depth or quality filter is applied beyond the allele-number threshold, which matters for a ten-sample joint call against a 2010 short-read assembly: allele-frequency estimates from ten libraries carry large sampling error, and reference or mapping bias against an old assembly is an alternative explanation for the effect we report. The resulting minor-allele-frequency floor is variable, not fixed at 0.05: at the minimum allele number of 6 the smallest observable frequency is 1/6 ≈ 0.167, and 0.05 is reached only where all ten libraries are called (allele number 20), which is not the same as ten individuals. The bat panel therefore contains no rare alleles in the population-genetic sense, and its low-frequency bin is an allele-count floor rather than a frequency-matched one. No bat species is represented in the OMIA variant catalogue [53], so this arm is necessarily label-free and pilot-grade.
**The BRCA1 saturation-genome-editing function scores are those of Findlay et al.** [50], taken from that paper's supplementary data file 41586_2018_461_MOESM3_ESM.xlsx (3,893 chromosome 17 single-nucleotide substitutions on GRCh37), as redistributed with the Arc Institute Evo 2 reference notebook (https://github.com/ArcInstitute/evo2), together with the GRCh37.p13 chromosome 17 sequence (NC_000017.10) from the same source; both are retrieved for the BRCA1 panel.
Cat is stated as F.catus_Fca126_mat1.0 because that is the assembly every cat variant carrying an 8,192-bp window was ref-verified against (1,362 of 1,362), and cat GERP was read from a per-base Fca126 track, so no cross-assembly projection enters the cat comparison. Chicken's GERP track is Ensembl release 106's 27-sauropsid file and dog's is release 104's 90-mammal file on CanFam3.1. The conservation comparison for cat is reported on the co-scorable subset, as it is in every species.
Annotation was taken from Ensembl release 114 for cat, release 112 for goat, pig, horse and human, release 106 for sheep, release 105 for chicken, release 104 for dog and release 99 for cattle, which used a pre-existing local ARS-UCD1.2.99 build. The releases differ because each is matched to the assembly that species' panel is on. For four species a later release would have silently changed the build, and those are the binding cases: chicken moved to GRCg7b after release 106 (release 106 still serves Gallus_gallus.GRCg6a; 107 is the first GRCg7b), dog to ROS_Cfam_1.0 after 104, and sheep to ARS-UI_Ramb_v2.0 and cattle to ARS-UCD1.3 after 110. For goat, pig, horse and human the assembly we used is still served by later releases, so no such constraint applied. Matching the build was the binding constraint, and we state it because an unexplained mix of releases is exactly the kind of unstated choice this paper is about; positives are annotated at the coordinates of the OMIA table each panel was built from, including the 203 dog positives whose identifiers carry a RefSeq chromosome accession; the annotation covers every variant of all nine panels.
Under the coding rule of the Methods, 3,191 atlas variants count as coding, including the 370 splice_donor_variant&intron_variant and splice_acceptor_variant&intron_variant sites, while the 24 splice_region_variant&synonymous_variant sites do not.
Assembly accessions (GenBank): human GRCh38 GCA_000001405.15, cattle ARS-UCD1.2 GCA_002263795.2, pig Sscrofa11.1 GCA_000003025.6, sheep Oar_rambouillet_v1.0 GCA_002742125.1, goat ARS1 GCA_001704415.1, horse EquCab3.0 GCA_002863925.1, cat F.catus_Fca126_mat1.0 GCA_018350175.1, chicken GRCg6a GCA_000002315.5, dog CanFam3.1 GCA_000002285.2, and little brown bat Myoluc2.0 GCA_000147115.1. The cat assembly is the one the panel was built and ref-verified on. Ensembl has served it since release 114, which supplies both the cat negatives' variation file and the gene models of cat's snpEff database. Its BioProject, PRJNA684600, describes it as the maternal, Felis catus haplotype of a Felis catus × Leopardus geoffroyi F1 hybrid, so the cat reference is a domestic-cat haplotype assembled from a hybrid individual; cat contributes 12% of the atlas. The Evo 2 model weights are distributed by the Arc Institute.

## Note S32. dbNSFP panel: gene-resampled intervals and pairwise comparisons

Intervals on this panel come from a bootstrap that resamples genes rather than variants, since variants within a gene share sequence context and annotation history. The per-pair analysis compares each pair of predictors pooled and then within gene, weighting genes by their pair count. Where a paired bootstrap is impractical we use DeLong's method in the O(n log n) form of Sun and Xu [98,99], extended to the must-answer statistic by noting that an unreachable variant has a placement value of exactly one half, so the same covariance machinery applies unchanged and returns a closed-form interval on a must-answer difference. Closed-form and bootstrap standard errors are compared on panels where both can be run, and they disagree: a bootstrap resampling genes rather than variants is 5.8 times wider than the closed-form calculation that treats variants as independent, which is why we do not treat a closed-form interval as evidence that a difference is real. The same placements give confidence sets for the bounds themselves. Each bound endpoint is a Mann–Whitney statistic in which a pair touching an unscored variant counts 0 in the lower bound and 1 in the upper, so summing its placements within genes gives a gene-clustered variance for every endpoint and every difference between two. A comparison counts as identified at 95% when the gap between its facing endpoints exceeds c standard errors, with c chosen so that the set covers a partially identified difference with 95% probability [34,35]; c lies between 1.645 and 1.960 in principle, 1.960 being its limit for a point-identified difference, and over the identified comparisons between 1.645 and 1.958, the realised maximum. The sets are per-comparison, not simultaneous over the 1,176 comparisons. On that standard 74 of the 83 identified comparisons stay identified, and 376 of the 429 on the missense subset; treating variants as independent, all 83 and 418 would. The nine that drop out all clear the bar by less than 0.012 AUROC, CADD against MPC and against PrimateAI by 0.0009 and 0.0011, and for the bound endpoints the gene-clustered standard errors are a median 6.2 times the variant-level ones.
Holm's step-down procedure on the p-values dual to these confidence sets, which holds the family-wise error rate over the 1,176 comparisons at 5%, keeps 67 of the 83 and 316 of the 429, and a step-down max-T bootstrap over the 2,352 one-sided endpoint statistics, with each gene's summed placements multiplied by one standard normal draw (B = 10,000, seed 0), keeps 68 and 323. Of the 83, 16 involve bStatistic, and all 16 survive each of these standards, so that without it the count falls from 83 to 67, from 74 to 58 at 95% and from 67 to 51 under Holm's procedure, and on the missense subset from 429 to 382, from 376 to 330 and from 316 to 271.

## Note S33. Supervised baselines and the embedding probe: architectures, schedules and cross-validation

The two supervised baselines are a floor, not competitors, and both are trained on one-hot sequence in a 500-bp window spanning 250 bases upstream of the variant and 249 downstream, which is not exactly centred for the same reason the 8,192-bp window is not. The 1D convolutional network takes an 8-channel input, the reference and alternate windows stacked, through two Conv1d blocks of 64 channels and kernel width 9 with batch normalisation and ReLU, a global max-pool, and a dropout-0.4 head of 64 to 32 to 1. It is trained with Adam at learning rate 1e-3 and weight decay 1e-3 under a class-weighted binary cross-entropy, batch size 64, 40 epochs, seed 0. The second is not a frequency classifier but gradient boosting on k-mer features: k = 4, the feature vector concatenating the normalised reference k-mer spectrum with the alternate-minus-reference difference, fitted with HistGradientBoostingClassifier at 300 iterations, learning rate 0.06, maximum depth 3, L2 penalty 1.0, seed 0. Both run over eight species, excluding goat, whose 99 variants are too few to fit; the leave-one-species-out protocol therefore trains on seven and tests on the eighth, and the within-species protocol is 5-fold stratified cross-validation.
The embedding probe of Evo 2-1B reads the blocks.24.mlp.l3 hidden state at the variant offset from the evo2_1b_base checkpoint, separately for the reference and alternate windows, and fits LogisticRegression (C = 0.5, 3,000 iterations) on standardised features under GroupKFold by gene, so no gene is split across folds (embeddings from the deposited extraction step). Two feature sets were run on the same 10,000-variant balanced subset of the eQTL panel, and we report both rather than the better one: the alternate-minus-reference difference reaches a gene-grouped AUROC of 0.504 (95% CI [0.493, 0.516]), and concatenating reference, alternate and difference reaches 0.519 ([0.508, 0.531]). The 0.52 quoted in Note S21 is the higher of the two. The interval is a 2,000-resample percentile bootstrap over variants rather than over eGenes, which might be expected to make it narrower than the panel's dependence structure warrants. The variant-level interval excludes 0.5, so what the probe establishes is a detectable effect of negligible size rather than a null, and the narrowness of the interval works against us in saying so.
The five cattle allele-frequency bins are fixed by minor allele frequency at [0, 0.002), [0.002, 0.01), [0.01, 0.05), [0.05, 0.30) and [0.30, 0.5]; the code writes the last edge as 0.999 to exclude fixed sites, but a folded minor-allele frequency cannot exceed 0.5 and the realised mean of that bin is 0.39. Bins were sampled to a target of 6,000 windows each. We call the lowest bin ultra-rare rather than singleton: at the 14,788 alleles observed here a true singleton sits near a frequency of 6.8 × 10⁻⁵, whereas this bin admits up to about 29 alternate copies and has an observed mean frequency of 0.00062. The distinction matters where Note S31 weighs a genotyping-error explanation, because that argument is about literal singletons and this bin contains few of them.

## Note S34. Bootstrap resampling units, permutation schemes and the TOST configuration

The Evo 2 versus GERP readout head-to-head scores Evo 2 at both readouts against the same fixed GERP baseline on the co-scorable variants of the 8,192-bp subset, per species, with a paired species-clustered bootstrap (B = 4,000) on the macro difference. To attribute the readout effect itself to its two causes, the decomposition reads the deposited per-variant readouts for a 2,599-variant subset and forms the two terms from four quantities. It does not itself re-score: the subset and its per-species cap of 400 were fixed by the upstream 8,192-bp scoring job, and the resulting counts are recorded per species in the deposited artefact (goat 98 to dog and human 400 each, summing to 2,599), so that context length and aggregation are varied one at a time. Three of the four are read from one 8,192-bp forward pass per sequence: the variant-position log-likelihood delta, the mean over the central 1,001 bp and the mean over the full window. The fourth is the variant-position delta from the separate 1,001-bp pass. The left-context-and-scorer term holds aggregation fixed at a single position and compares the 8,192-bp pass with the 1,001-bp pass (pooled 0.9168 against 0.8792, +0.038); the aggregation term holds the conditioning context fixed at 8,192 bp and compares the full-window mean with that same single-token delta (pooled 0.9542 against 0.9168, +0.037). The mean over the central 1,001 bp (pooled 0.9571) enters neither term as reported, but it is the cell that makes the split path-dependent, and we give the other ordering here rather than only in the Analyses: taking aggregation first gives, on the species-mean scale, aggregation +0.071 and context −0.004, and the two orderings differ by −0.040 on that scale, a path dependence rather than a factorial interaction, because the fourth cell of a complete two-by-two — a window mean at 1,001-bp conditioning context — is not part of the design; pooled, the same ordering gives +0.078 and −0.003. In that ordering the first step also moves from the streamed 1,001-bp pass to the 8,192-bp pass, and so changes left context and scorer as well as aggregation; only the context-first aggregation term, +0.037 of a pooled +0.075, is measured within one pass. What is robust across both orderings is that extending the averaged window past 1 kb adds nothing; the roughly equal halves reading holds only under the first ordering, and which of the two terms is larger changes with it. The full-window mean reproduces the deposited 8,192-bp score to within 0.0003 pooled AUROC, validating the intermediate readouts (Supplementary Table S9). A percentile interval on nine clusters undercovers regardless of resample count, as noted below, so we also computed a bias-corrected and accelerated (BCa) interval on the species-level differences, resampling the nine species 20,000 times rather than the 4,000 used for the species-clustered intervals elsewhere: [+0.073, +0.157] against the percentile [+0.071, +0.153] for the same nine species-level values (a coarser resampling of nine summary differences than the variant-level paired species-clustered bootstrap, which yields [+0.060, +0.162]; the Analyses report the t interval on eight degrees of freedom, [+0.058, +0.159]) (bias correction z0 = +0.029, acceleration a = +0.034). The correction shifts the interval marginally upward and widens it by 1.8%, and does not change any conclusion; we report it because claiming a small-sample problem and then not applying the available correction would be the same failure this paper documents elsewhere. BCa is not a remedy for the underlying deficiency: it corrects median bias and skewness, not the shortage of degrees of freedom that causes nine-cluster intervals to undercover, and a t-interval on eight degrees of freedom is roughly a quarter wider than the percentile interval on these data. Both still exclude zero for the headline margins. Three descriptive nulls are reported as bare non-significance, and we name them here, so that the sentence above does not overstate the discipline: the within-window cattle correlation (ρ = −0.008, p = 0.38), the divergence-grading test (ρ = −0.365, p = 0.334, n = 9) and the unsigned-magnitude ranking (AUROC 0.496). None of the three carries a conclusion on its own. The increment from which the equivalence bound is set has a larger species-level estimand as well (the +0.054 n-weighted species mean of the orthogonal-contribution decomposition, not the readout head-to-head; Note S15) and we set the bound from the smaller pooled value deliberately; but the choice does not carry the verdict, because the species-clustered equivalence interval reaches +0.056 and so exceeds both candidate bounds (0.020 and 0.054), leaving equivalence unestablished under either. One caveat travels with it, and we state it because Note S2 is emphatic that units of analysis are not interchangeable: ΔFM = +0.0202 is a pooled, variant-level statistic, while the equivalence test it bounds is between-species and species-clustered. We use it because it is the only internally derived scale available, not because the two estimands are equivalent; sensitivity at δ = 0.01 and 0.05 is reported in the deposited output. The equivalence interval for this between-species comparison uses a species-clustered bootstrap (B = 4,000), consistent with the species-mean aggregation taken as primary; within-species intervals elsewhere (the readout effect, ΔFM, ΔECE) are variant-level or within-species bootstraps appropriate to those estimands. That choice is consequential: on the Evo 2 versus GERP co-scorable comparison a variant-level bootstrap would yield [−0.010, +0.015] and declare equivalence, whereas the species-clustered interval is [−0.024, +0.056] and does not. We report the latter. On the species-mean difference, the primary estimand, the test gives a third answer. The 1,001-bp species-mean margin over GERP on the 9,532 co-scorable variants is +0.0485, with a 90% t interval on eight degrees of freedom of [+0.003, +0.094]: it is not equivalent to zero at δ = 0.02 (TOST p = 0.86), nor at 0.01 (p = 0.92) or 0.05 (p = 0.48), and it is one-sided superior at p = 0.041. Equivalence would need a margin of at least 0.094. Without goat the margin is +0.033, with a 90% t interval on seven degrees of freedom of [−0.008, +0.073] and a one-sided p of 0.084. The same t construction gives the intervals quoted for the 8,192-bp margin, [+0.058, +0.159], the 1,001-bp margin on the 9,529, [−0.009, +0.104], and the readout shift on the 11,109, [+0.032, +0.097]. The main text reports these two-sided intervals; the one-sided test and the 9,532-variant set belong to this equivalence analysis and carry no claim of their own.
Per species, the advantage of Evo 2 over GERP was assessed at 1,001 bp by the one-sided bootstrap tail mass p = 1 − P(Δ > 0), the fraction of paired bootstrap replicates in which the difference failed to favour Evo 2, and at 8,192 bp by one-sided DeLong tests on the co-scorable variants. Where a permutation p-value is reported it is two-sided, P(max_family |Δ_permuted| ≥ |Δ_observed|). The ClinVar rungs permute labels within a rung with rung sizes fixed; the splicing classes hold labels fixed and swap the two predictors' within-class rank scores at each variant, the null being that the two predictors are exchangeable on a given variant. Ranks are used there because swapping raw scores on incommensurable scales, an Evo 2 log-likelihood ratio against a bounded probability, inflates the null.
**Ties at the refusal boundary.** The abstention rule refuses the least-confident fixed fraction within each species, which is a rank rule, and a rank rule has to say what it does with ties. Coarse posteriors make that decide the answer rather than decorate it, and this affects the three arms very differently. The 1,001-bp Platt posterior as refit here takes 8,842 distinct values, nine variants sit on the 15% boundary, and captured error is identical to sixteen decimal places across 399 permutations of the row order, so the 2.35× lift is not exposed. The 1,001-bp isotonic posterior takes 248 distinct values with 899 variants on the boundary, and its lift moves between 2.23 and 2.34 under permutation alone. The 8,192-bp arm, which is the one the package ships and the one the reproduction benchmark checks, is the most exposed of the three: it is isotonic, takes 218 distinct values, 597 variants sit on the boundary, and permuting the rows moves pooled capture over a range of 0.043 and the pooled lift between 3.95 and 4.24 against a published 4.07. That range is twice the tolerance a single ordering would allow, so the benchmark instead checks that the published capture lies inside the range 20 row-order permutations produce (Methods). We report the published figures under that rule and name it: it is the rank rule with a positional tie-break, exposed as tie_policy="rank". A positional secondary key cannot fix this, because the position is the row order. The package therefore defaults to refusing whole tie blocks and never part of one, which is deterministic under any row order, reports the realised coverage that results, and gives a pooled capture of 0.542 on this panel rather than 0.610 because it refuses fewer calls; tie_policy="rank" reproduces the published figures.
All resampling in the analysis scripts is seeded. Every draw that builds a panel reported here takes an explicit seed and defaults it to zero: the three negative-set builders, and the bootstrap and permutation routines throughout. One deposited script uses an unseeded DuckDB reservoir sample, and it builds no panel reported in this paper; it is a CattleGTEx eQTL builder.
