The panel it produced is deposited and fixed, and every analysis downstream of it is seeded, so the published numbers are reproducible from the deposited panel rather than from a re-draw. The seed is recorded in each recompute layer rather than in every figure legend: the pipeline uses seed 0 by default and, among others, 3, 5, 7 and 11 for the independently seeded replicate arms, and seed 20260719 for Table 2's per-species intervals, the Figure S2 eQTL and the trust-layer bootstraps, 20260923 for the identification verdicts of Note S55, 20260721/20260722 for the equivalence and head-to-head bootstraps, 20260718 for the eQTL-panel bootstraps of the capacity ladder and the window-by-readout control, 61 for most other locus-clustered bootstraps (Table S38 and Figure 10), 20250211 for the TraitGym resamples (Note S75), 20260720 for the reach statistics, 20260724 for the selective-robustness audit, and 20260723 for the atlas and readout rebuilds, the eGene-clustered eQTL interval, the consequence-matched Mantel–Haenszel reach test and the eQTL embedding probe (the divergence-grading test takes no seed: it is a deterministic Spearman correlation with a closed-form p-value); every value in this paper is reproducible from the deposited artefacts without a further random draw; what regenerates from the archive alone, and what needs the undeposited data/ tree, is set out under Data Availability.
Uncertainty on AUROC, ECE and the calibration-transfer deltas was quantified by nonparametric percentile bootstraps over variants, and the resampling unit differs by estimand. The macro Brier score is deposited as a point estimate with no interval and is reported as one: 0.0517 under the isotonic leave-one-species-out transfer, 0.0536 under the Platt transfer and under the trivial sigmoid, which are bit-identical, and 0.0488 under the in-species oracle. It is bin-free, so unlike ECE it does not move with the binning rule. The paired calibration-transfer deltas are stratified within species. The per-species intervals in Table 2 are locus-clustered resamples at B = 20,000 in both columns. Unstratified paired variant-level resamples, 50,000 replicates at 1,001 bp and 20,000 at 8,192, are deposited as a control and reproduce every printed endpoint to within 0.002. The unstratified draw shows in the printed table and we say where: because a replicate can contain few positives, some of goat's replicates return an AUROC of exactly 1.0, which is one of the reasons goat carries no interval, and replicates containing only one class are undefined and dropped. Goat is the smallest panel here, 9 positives in 99 variants; the variant-level control interval is [0.864, 1.000], and Table 2 prints no interval for it. The deposit does carry a second script, build_hardening_cis.py, which recomputes the 1,001-bp column under a within-class stratified draw at 2,000 resamples as a hardening check. It returns the same nine point estimates and a goat interval of [0.870, 1.000], so the upper endpoint of the variant-level control does not depend on the stratification choice. Its intervals differ slightly from that control in all nine species; Table 2 reports the locus-clustered estimator throughout. The upper endpoint of the smallest panel is therefore a property of the resampling unit and not a claim of perfect separation. The frozen-score ClinVar calibration arm used B = 300 stratified resamples, each a full 4-fold isotonic cross-validation, and the ClinVar reach-panel audit used B = 200 (the ClinVar consequence rungs of Table S19 use B = 200,000). Per-predictor intervals on the dbNSFP panel come from a bootstrap that resamples genes (B = 2,000), because variants within a gene share sequence context and annotation history. Between-species estimands instead use a species-clustered bootstrap over the nine species, and an unweighted mean over the nine carries a t interval on eight degrees of freedom as its headline. The Table 2 per-species intervals are locus-clustered at both readouts, and the variant-level percentile bootstrap described above serves only as the reproduction control reported in Note S29. Two limitations of that choice should be read with the intervals. B = 200 estimates each 95% percentile from roughly five order statistics per tail, and B = 300 from seven or eight, so the ClinVar intervals should be treated as indicative. For the reach-panel audit a deposited simulation puts an endpoint's Monte Carlo standard deviation at about 0.19 of the statistic's standard error, so an endpoint can move by about a fifth of a standard error between resampling runs, while the point estimates are exact. The strong claim of the calibration arm (calibrated error orders of magnitude below raw error) is a separation so large that resampling noise does not decide it, but the interval endpoints are not precise. Separately, the species-clustered bootstraps resample nine clusters, which gives about eight effective degrees of freedom; a nine-cluster percentile interval undercovers regardless of how many resamples are drawn, so the species-clustered intervals in this paper are, if anything, narrower than they should be, which is why each nine-species mean is headlined by its t interval and its percentile interval kept as a sensitivity. We report the others because they are still wider and more honest than the variant-level alternative, not because nine clusters is adequate.
The selection analyses used Spearman rank correlation between minor-allele frequency and predicted deleteriousness (expected negative and monotone towards rare alleles). The two quantities are not oriented to the same allele. Minor-allele frequency is folded, min(AF, 1 − AF), while the deleteriousness score is fixed in the reference-to-alternate direction and is never polarised against an outgroup. At any site where the alternate allele is the major one, the frequency therefore describes the reference allele while the score describes the change away from it, which inverts the sign of that site's contribution. This dilutes the correlation rather than creating it, so it is a reason the measured effect is small and not a reason it exists, but it means the magnitude should not be interpreted and we do not interpret it. AUROC was computed with scikit-learn roc_auc_score.

## Note S35. The splicing benchmark and the ClinVar consequence panel

**Source and provenance.** The splicing panel is Table S1 of Smith and Kitzman [10], which assembles six splicing assays with a measured splice-disruptive call for each variant and the scores of eight published splice predictors. Five arms are saturation mutagenesis. The sixth is 296 MLH1 variants, 7.6% of the panel, which Smith and Kitzman curated from clinical variant databases and literature reports rather than assaying de novo. They report that this arm covers 1.6% of the possible single-nucleotide variants in MLH1 exons and their 100-bp flanks, that 59.5% of it is splice-proximal, that eight conflicting calls were resolved by majority vote with ties taken as pathogenic, and that essential-splice dinucleotides were admitted without molecular evidence. Apart from those dinucleotides the calls rest on published RT-PCR or minigene results, so they remain cell measurements, but they are measurements Smith and Kitzman collected rather than made. We used the six assays carrying a binary label: BRCA1, FAS exon 6, MLH1, POU1F1 exon 2, RON exon 11 and WT1 exon 9, giving 3,912 variants of which 1,060 (27.1%) are disruptive. The 1,386-variant BRCA1 arm is the intronic and synonymous slice of the saturation-genome-editing set of Findlay et al. [50] that supplies this paper's positive control, on the same build. No independence between the two is claimed. They read different measurements at different readouts: a splice-disruptive call at 1,001 bp on the 1B checkpoint here, against cell-survival depletion at 8,192 bp on the 40B checkpoint there. Nothing is fitted on either. BRCA1 is also the assay Evo 2 reads best, at AUROC 0.808, so the overlap narrows the deficit we report rather than inflating it: dropping the BRCA1 arm moves the n-weighted mean deficit against SpliceAI from −0.2626 to −0.3092. The same table supplies 500,000 simulated SNVs drawn at random from internal protein-coding exons and their 100-bp flanks across 14,577 genes, with no labels, which we use only for reach.
**Window extraction.** The panel is on GRCh37 and our reference tree carries GRCh38, so rather than download a genome for six genes we fetched the six loci as slabs from the UCSC REST API and cached them. Windows are 1,001 bp centred on the variant. Every window was reference-verified: the base at the variant offset must equal the table's reference allele or the variant is dropped, because a mismatch inverts the sign of the score. All 3,912 windows passed; none was dropped for a reference mismatch or a truncated window.
**The ClinVar consequence panel.** Sampled from the deposited ClinVar panel, balanced by consequence and label with a cap of 250 per cell, giving 3,714 variants across 16 cells. Windows are 1,001 bp and were reference-verified by the same rule; zero variants were dropped for a reference mismatch. The regulatory rungs carry 54 to 250 positives, against the 18 available in the OMIA consequence gradient of Note S7.
**Composition control on the reach comparison.** The benchmark and the background do not share a variant-class mix, and reach depends on class, so a raw comparison confounds curation with composition. We standardised the background to the benchmark's class mix by reweighting per-class reach. The gap is unchanged to three decimals for SpliceAI, ConSpliceML and SQUIRLS, so composition explains none of it for those three. For S-CAP the raw gap of 0.251 falls to 0.175, so composition accounts for about 30% and the remainder survives.
**Reproduction of published coverage.** Before using the background we reproduced the coverage figures the source paper reports in its methods. Across all seven predictors with a reported figure, our largest discrepancy is 0.05 percentage points. Our own recomputed call rates are deposited, which covers all eight predictors; the eighth, ConSpliceML, has no published call rate to compare against.
**Interval construction on the Essential Splice class.** That class carries 238 disruptive calls in 246 variants, so it rests on eight negatives and its bootstrap distribution is skewed. Table S34 prints its percentile interval, [0.7878, 0.9112] before rounding; the bias-corrected and accelerated construction has z0 = −0.047 and acceleration = −0.016 and gives [0.7820, 0.9076]. The endpoints differ by at most 0.0058, but with eight negatives neither construction is informative, and we report the class descriptively.
One published conclusion changes under the accounting. Smith and Kitzman's Figure 3C tallies, for each dataset and variant class, the tool with the highest area under the precision–recall curve, each tool scored on the variants it answers [10]. On their MLH1 dataset that tool is S-CAP, at 0.988, computed on the 138 of 296 variants S-CAP scores: 116 of the 160 splice-disrupting variants and 22 of the 136 neutral ones, so 84.1% of its scored subset is disruptive against 54.1% of the dataset. On those same 138 variants four tools read higher, Pangolin, SpliceAI, ConSpliceML and MMSplice (0.999, 0.998, 0.998 and 0.995). Charged for what it declines, S-CAP's must-answer AUROC on the dataset is 0.551, last of the eight, and its bound [0.109, 0.992] overlaps every other tool's, so the printed win is not identified. HAL's tally win on the RON exon 11 dataset is not identified either, its bound being [0.274, 0.891], although HAL still leads on the 378 of 598 variants it scores. The paper's headline, that SpliceAI and Pangolin perform best overall, survives: they remain the most frequent winners under every convention, although their lead over HAL and S-CAP is not identified in most datasets.

## Note S36. Strand consistency at matched capacity

A genome has no preferred strand, so scoring a window or its reverse complement is a free protocol choice. A capacity comparison needs the panel and window length held fixed, since either can move the result on its own.
The 1B model was therefore scored on the identical 327-variant, 8,192-bp panel the 40B run used, in both orientations (Additional file 3, tables/strand_ladder_evo2_1b_scores.parquet), so that panel and window are held fixed. The two rungs were scored by different implementations (below), so capacity is not the only variable; the ladder below has two rungs, 1B and 40B. The 327-variant subpanel is 51.7% positive and is stratified at about 40 variants per species; pig contributes 20, all of them positives.
**Strand agreement and the strand-choice shift at 1B and 40B**

|  | 1B | 40B |
|---|---|---|
| Pearson, forward against reverse complement | 0.7732 | 0.7123 |
| Spearman, forward against reverse complement | 0.8837 | 0.8992 |
| strand-choice AUROC shift | 0.0081 | 0.0079 |
| as a percentage of the DeLong-based pooled 8,192-bp margin of Note S64, +0.096 | 8.4% | 8.2% |
| gain from strand-averaging | +0.0073 | +0.0031 |
| forward-strand AUROC | 0.8985 | 0.9238 |

By Pearson, strand agreement is slightly worse at the larger model, though by Spearman it is slightly better, and the shift is flat, at about 8% of the 8,192-bp margin at both scales. Capacity does not repair strand inconsistency. Strand-averaging improves discrimination at both scales, and none of the audited protocols specifies which strand to score. The table is the full-window readout, the only one the two rungs share: the 1B rung was scored by the window-sum scorer, which emits a window-summed delta and nothing else. On the same 327 windows the 40B run also gives the mean over the central 1,001 bases, where the strand-choice shift is 0.0046. Averaging over a window attenuates the sensitivity without removing it, and an architecture that is reverse-complement equivariant by construction [66] cannot exhibit it at all. The reach, calibration, abstention, eQTL and selection arms are scored on the forward strand at the single-token readout, so they carry a strand sensitivity measured here at the window readouts and not at their own.
Two deposited runs at the 1,002-bp window mean, on a 389-variant subset at Evo 2-1B and at Evo 2-7B, move the other way. That subset is not an atlas subset: beside the 327 variants of the table it holds 40 variants of a superseded dog panel, 20 pig negatives from a negative set that the PigGTEx genotype sites replaced, and one goat and one horse variant without an 8,192-bp window, and its per-variant scores are not deposited. On it forward minus reverse complement reads −0.0111 at 1B and −0.0121 at 7B, forward minus the strand average −0.0183 and −0.0282, with forward-against-reverse Pearson 0.5771 and 0.4422 (Additional file 2, analyses/results/strand_consistency_1b.json and strand_consistency_7b.json). Resampling the 327 variants of the table above, the 40B strand-choice shift at the full-window readout is +0.008 (−0.007 to +0.024), an interval of the order of the difference between two passes: the strand run and the atlas run are two 40B passes over the same windows, differing by at most 0.000125 per variant, and they move per-species AUROC by up to 0.012 in goat, the smallest stratum with both classes.

## Note S37. The reference-likelihood confound across three panels

A variant delta is the difference between two window likelihoods, so a panel on which the reference window's likelihood alone separates the classes would produce a delta that inherits that separation. The deposited scorer discards the reference likelihood from its own output, but tables/strand40b_scores.parquet in Additional file 3 retains it at all three aggregations, so this is testable from the deposit.
**Discrimination from the reference likelihood alone, on three panels**

| panel | readout | MLL(reference) alone |
|---|---|---|
| nine-species animal panel | 8,192 bp, 40B | 0.5713 |
| nine-species animal panel | central 1,001 bp of the 8,192-bp window, 40B | 0.6908 [0.634, 0.749] |
| human ClinVar consequence panel | 1,001 bp, 1B | 0.4878 [0.4693, 0.5064] |
| splicing benchmark | 1,001 bp, 1B and 7B | 0.5122 [0.4921, 0.5326] at 1B; 0.5736 [0.5509, 0.5966] at 7B |

The confound is real on the animal panel and modest, and it is checkpoint-dependent on both human panels, in opposite directions. At 1B the reference likelihood alone is indistinguishable from chance on each of them. At 7B the ClinVar quantity reads 0.4610 [0.4426, 0.4802], an interval that excludes chance on the far side, so the reference window is mildly anti-predictive there rather than uninformative; while the splicing quantity reads 0.5736 [0.5509, 0.5966], which excludes chance on the near side and is the largest of the human-panel readings, slightly above the 0.5713 of the animal panel's full window. The animal panel's central kilobase reads higher, 0.6908 [0.634, 0.749], and 0.682 on same-species pairs: of the window readings it is the one that counts most against us, at the aggregation that already carries the readout gain (Table S9), and only the single-position reading below exceeds it. We report all six rather than the ones that suit us. The marginal figure is not the whole test. On the 389-variant strand subset of Note S36, 62 of whose variants are not among the 327, scored at Evo 2-1B in 1,002-bp windows, the reference likelihood alone reads 0.5820 [0.5266, 0.6370], and discrimination rises across quartiles of that likelihood: 0.7005, 0.8402, 0.8581 and 0.8792 on 97, 97, 97 and 98 variants. The spread across quartiles is 0.1787, against an asymptotic standard error of 0.069 for the highest-minus-lowest difference, and it is carried by the lowest quartile alone. The delta and the reference likelihood correlate at −0.180 on the same variants. Discrimination is therefore weakest where the reference window is least likely under the model. Two things bound what this shows. The readout is the window mean log-likelihood, and the reach, calibration, abstention, eQTL and selection arms use the single-token readout, and it carries to them with greater force: there, on the 327-variant subpanel of Note S36, the reference base's log-probability at the variant alone reads 0.8766 [0.833, 0.916] against 0.8987 for the full variant delta, and the two rank variants almost identically (|ρ| = 0.94), so at that readout the score is close to rank-equivalent to a statistic that never reads the alternate allele. These single-token values are read from the 8,192-bp windows, with 4,096 bases of left context, not from the 1,001-bp arm's own pass. Within species the two statistics rank variants as closely (median |Spearman| 0.94 over the eight species with both classes), and on same-species pairs alone the full delta reads 0.906 against 0.882 for the reference, so the near-equivalence is not an effect of pooling species, although 88.9% of the pooled pairs cross species. The strata are too small for per-species intervals. At the 8,192-bp readout the same single-token delta is one of the window delta's 8,191 terms and supplies a median 53% of it; the window delta ranks variants less like the reference base alone (|Spearman| 0.81 against 0.94), and its downstream terms alone read 0.865 against 0.9238 for the whole delta (section 2 of recompute_strand.py, Additional file 3).

## Note S38. Gene-structured missingness, and the union-ensemble limit

Reach failure among splice predictors is organised by gene rather than scattered across variants. On the 500,000-variant background, restricted to genes carrying at least 50 variants, SpliceAI's per-gene reach is 1.000 at both the tenth and the ninetieth percentile and zero for 100 genes. SQUIRLS has 296 such genes and ConSpliceML 78. A predictor with this shape does not degrade gracefully as a panel grows: it answers a gene completely or not at all.
Joint availability follows. Of the 500,000 variants, 7.9% are scored by all eight predictors and the mean variant is scored by 6.63 of them. For essential-splice, intron-near-junction and proximal-intron variants the proportion scored by all eight is zero.
**The limit this places on our own claim.** The genes each predictor cannot see are largely not the same genes. Pairwise Jaccard overlap between zero-reach gene sets runs from 0.000 to 0.134, the largest being SpliceAI against ConSpliceML at 0.134. Across the eight, 432 genes are invisible to at least one predictor and none is invisible to every predictor that has holes at all. That is the falsifiable form and we use it deliberately: HAL, MMSplice and Pangolin each have n_genes_zero_coverage = 0, so a claim that no gene is invisible to all eight could not have come out any other way and would be decoration. A union across those five would therefore repair gene-level reach almost completely.
This result bounds the strongest interpretation of reach failure. The individual tools do not implement that union, and combining them would require a prespecified aggregation rule and renewed evaluation of class-dependent selection. Reach failure is therefore addressable in principle but is not repaired by the single-predictor comparisons reported here.

## Note S39. A capacity ladder measured at one readout

Table S34 and Note S65 read Evo 2 at 1B and 7B on the splicing panel, and Note S36 reports 40B on a strand subset. Those numbers come from two different scorers. The 1B and 7B panel scores collapse the 1,001-bp window by summing its log-likelihood; the 40B scores are a single-token next-token delta, the readout the 40B scorer implements. Reading one against the other moves the readout and the capacity together, so Note S36 compares the two checkpoints only on one panel, at the readout they share.
We therefore rescored both panels at 1B and 7B through the streaming scorer, so that the panel, the window length, the readout, the scorer and the variant offset are identical across rungs and only the checkpoint changes.
**The capacity ladder at 1B, 7B and 40B, on the splicing and ClinVar panels**

| panel | 1B | 7B | 40B |
|---|---|---|---|
| Splicing, n = 3,912 | 0.5351 [0.5133, 0.5576] | 0.5304 [0.5091, 0.5529] | 0.5959 [0.5766, 0.6165] |
| ClinVar, n = 3,714 | 0.6029 [0.5848, 0.6220] | 0.6364 [0.6191, 0.6544] | 0.6857 [0.6682, 0.7033] |

Those intervals overlap between rungs and settle nothing on their own. Every rung scores the same variants, so the comparison is paired, and we resample variants once per replicate and recompute both AUROCs on that replicate (B = 2,000). The sevenfold step from 1B to 7B is −0.0047 [−0.0214, +0.0121] on the splicing panel and +0.0334 [+0.0198, +0.0469] on ClinVar.
The splicing result is a null rather than an absence of power: the interval excludes any gain above +0.012, so a sevenfold parameter increase is not merely unproven on cell-measured splicing but bounded to +0.012, about a third of the 1B-to-7B step on ClinVar. On ClinVar the same step is worth a real if modest amount. Capacity buys discrimination on one panel and not on the other.
**What the 40B column does not license.** Evo 2 ships an 8k-trained line and a context-extended one, and 1B exists only as evo2_1b_base. The ladder therefore sits on the 8k line at 1B and 7B, while the 40B rung is evo2_40b, the checkpoint reported everywhere else in this paper and a context-extended one. Any comparison reaching that column spans the context-extension training as well as capacity, and the deposited artefact flags each such contrast. Only the 1B-to-7B step compares two base checkpoints, and only it is quoted as a checkpoint contrast; those two base checkpoints differ in training data as well as in parameter count (Note S4).
Every value in these tables is read from a deposited artefact; none is typed by hand.

## Note S40. Reproducibility status of the submission archive

All ten main-text figures rebuild from deposited artefacts, together with Figures S1, S2, S4, S7 and S8; Figures S3, S5 and S6 are built from the primary score and label tree, which is not deposited (Note S58 sets out what regenerates without it). Additional file 2 ships a single requirements file that pins 18 packages, including torch and transformers; evo2 and vortex are installed on the GPU scoring host, in the container whose recipe is deposited under container/ (Note S30). One verification tool, tools/verify_bh_seed_stability.py, seeds its generator from Python's process-randomized hash(), so its per-seed streams are reproducible between runs when PYTHONHASHSEED is fixed; the published count that tool checks is unaffected.

## Note S41. Extended Methods: panel and scoring provenance

Notes S41 and S42 hold extended Methods, as do Notes S30 to S35, which are grouped by the Methods subsection each one extends. A few paragraphs appear in both; where they overlap the two copies agree, and neither supersedes the other.
Of 828 curated candidate positives, 740 survived to the final panel, an attrition of 88 (10.6%) whose dominant causes were failure of the reference-allele build gate, non-SNV records and the absence of a symmetric scoring window at a contig end. The attrition is load-bearing in one place: goat falls from 16 positives to 9, below the ten-positive threshold the conservation-matched control requires, which is why goat is excluded from it.
Reference genomes are those against which the source coordinates are deposited, which is not always the newest assembly: in Ensembl, chicken GRCg6a has been succeeded by GRCg7b, dog CanFam3.1 by ROS_Cfam_1.0, sheep Oar_rambouillet_v1.0 by ARS-UI_Ramb_v2.0 and cattle ARS-UCD1.2 by ARS-UCD1.3 and then ARS-UCD2.0. We used the builds matching the OMIA and Ensembl records without lifting over, since a lift-over would add its own error to a panel whose build-match is already the weakest link. For dog, where a lift-over route was also available, we verified which was used: 355 of 355 sampled dog windows reproduce base-for-base from CanFam3.1 and none from ROS_Cfam_1.0. The assemblies are cattle ARS-UCD1.2 [124], pig Sscrofa11.1 [72], sheep Oar_rambouillet_v1.0 [125], goat ARS1 [126], horse EquCab3.0 [127], cat F.catus_Fca126_mat1.0 (no dedicated assembly publication exists; [131] describes Felis_catus_9.0, a different one), chicken GRCg6a [128] and dog CanFam3.1 [129,130]; those references are the originating genome publications, since no release note exists for the specific builds. Versions and accessions are in Note S31 and under Data Availability.
Consequence classes for the atlas positives are OMIA's own curated Variant Effect field, joined on OMIA variant identifier. Human, whose positives come from ClinVar, carries no class, and cattle drops out with it for this SNV set, so the consequence gradient rests on seven species and 5,510 negatives, down from nine and 8,890. The four classes analysed are nonsense, missense, splicing and regulatory (Supplementary Note S7).
The 5,000 candidate causal variants fall in 3,160 eGenes and the 15,000 controls in 1,430 of those. Every control comes from an eGene that carries a candidate causal variant; the converse does not hold, so 1,730 eGenes carry a candidate causal variant and no control, and 2,217 of the 5,000 positives (44.3%) are matched only at panel level. The within-eGene precision@1 and rank-percentile statistics are computed on the 1,430 two-class eGenes only. No record is repeated: the panel holds 20,000 distinct variant identifiers and 20,000 distinct variant–gene pairs, and the repeats within an eGene are distinct variants, which may be correlated through linkage disequilibrium. Resampling eGenes rather than variants, the correct unit under within-gene linkage, gives [0.479, 0.497] against [0.478, 0.497] at variant level, a width ratio of 0.99. The controls are not linkage-disequilibrium partners of the candidate causal variants: selecting on low PIP if anything excludes the credible-set neighbours. Discrimination was evaluated as AUROC of the signed score, within-eGene precision@1 against the per-eGene candidate causal base rate, and mean candidate causal rank-percentile, with a coding contrast at the identical readout. Ranking on the unsigned magnitude gives AUROC 0.496, also indistinguishable from chance. A separate consequence-matched 2,000-variant subpanel supports the matched contrast in Figure S2.
**Label-free selection panels.** The cattle panel is 44.5 M biallelic variants on ARS-UCD1.2 over the 7,394 samples of the FarmGTEx cattle imputed-genotype release [105], read from PLINK2 allele-frequency tables [100]; its genotypes are imputed for samples that are mostly RNA-sequencing libraries, some repeated tissues of one animal, so the analysis is exploratory. It does not match the Daetwyler et al. resource [132] (234 bulls, 28.3 M variants); 7,234 of the 7,394 identifiers are public SRA, ENA, CNGB or DDBJ sample accessions. Variants were stratified-sampled across five minor-allele-frequency bins to 28,006 windows, purifying selection serving as the label-free ground truth: deleterious alleles are held at low frequency, which is the relationship the site-frequency spectrum encodes [134,135]. Two controls the atlas has are absent here: the panel is not trinucleotide-matched, and nothing controls genotyping error, to which the lowest bin is most exposed; that bin is ultra-rare rather than singleton, admitting anything below a frequency of 0.002 and averaging 0.00062 against 6.8 × 10⁻⁵ for a true singleton. Of the two alternative explanations for the frequency gradient, mutational composition can be tested directly. Composition does shift with frequency, C>T falling from 41.4% of the rarest quintile to 34.7% of the commonest and T>C rising from 25.3% to 32.5%, but the relationship survives within every mutation class, Spearman ρ from −0.077 to −0.159, all six of the same sign as the pooled −0.099 and all at p < 10⁻⁶, and it holds within CpG transitions (ρ = −0.055) and in 91 of the 96 trinucleotide contexts (Note S31). Genotype quality is not testable from these tables: they record an observed allele count of 14,788 at essentially every site, as imputed genotypes give, so that column carries no information about call rate and a filter built on it is a no-op. The cross-clade recurrence check used wild little brown bats (Myotis lucifugus, Myoluc2.0, an assembly with no dedicated publication, produced by the mammalian sequencing effort whose constraint analysis is [133]), whose ten sequencing libraries comprise six individually sequenced animals and four DNA pools; a joint call treats each library as one diploid genotype, so its allele counts are not counts of chromosomes and its frequency axis should be read as an ordinal rank. No bat species appears in OMIA, so this arm is necessarily label-free and pilot-grade. Note S31 gives assembly versions and accessions, the provenance ledger for every panel and the reference-concordance audit.
For all 11,109 atlas variants in all nine species the base at the variant offset in the stored reference window matches the record's reference allele: 100.0%. That check is not independent evidence of panel quality: at the 8,192-bp readout the builder discards any window whose central base disagrees with the record, so the check reads its own filter's output and records that the filter ran. The 1,001-bp readout has no such gate. extract_windows_local.py records ref_ok and does not enforce it, writing every window whatever its value, and check_atlas_ref_ok.py reads only the 8,192-bp panel. A 1,001-bp record whose reference and alternate are swapped against the assembly therefore survives with an inverted score, and the reach, calibration, abstention, eQTL and selection arms all read that panel. The exposure is bounded. The 8,192-bp builder keeps a variant only where the window's central base matches the record's reference allele, and the 11,109 identifiers it keeps are a strict subset of the 1,001-bp panel's 11,130, with no variant scored at 8,192 bp and absent at 1,001 bp. Both windows are drawn from the same assembly by the same routine and centred on the same position, so the 1,001-bp window of any variant that passed the 8,192-bp reference check reads the same verified base. Reference-allele failures in the 1,001-bp panel are therefore confined to the 21 variants carrying no 8,192-bp score, 0.19% of the panel, 18 of them negatives and 3 positives. One record's reference and alternate windows are identical, so its delta is exactly zero by construction: the assembly base at that position is the record's alternate allele rather than its reference, which is a reference mismatch of exactly the kind just bounded and the one instance the deposit observes directly. A further 12 atlas variants also score exactly zero at the 1,001-bp readout, 13 of 11,130 in all (0.117%), and those 12 have distinct windows and arise from numerical ties: the two alleles received equal logits under reduced-precision inference. A zero delta is an absence of a prediction, not a neutral one, and it falls at the centre of the score distribution, so these variants sit inside a tie block under the rank-based abstention rule of Note S34. Note S30 gives the checkpoint provenance, the full window-extraction procedure and the per-species FASTA handling.
Of the 11,130 atlas variants, 11,109 carry an 8,192-bp score; of the 21 exceptions, 18 lie within 4,096 bases of a contig end and cat's three fail the reference check (Note S27). At 1,001 bp, where the check is recorded but not enforced, those three stay in; without them cat reads 0.848 against 0.846. As a positive control, the human BRCA1 (GRCh37) saturation-genome-editing set scored at 8,192-bp mean log-likelihood reaches AUROC 0.874; the Arc Institute reference notebook prints 0.73 for the 1B checkpoint under the same protocol, a notebook output rather than a published figure and a different checkpoint, so it sets a scale rather than testing the harness. On our own ladder the nine-species mean at the 8,192-bp readout runs 0.868 at 1B, 0.928 at 7B and 0.943 at 40B (0.896, 0.924 and 0.939 without human, whose 1B scores are anomalous; Note S15), the top rung being the headline atlas macro itself rather than a separate coding-only quantity, so a 40B-versus-1B gap should not be read as a gap over 7B. Additional file 3 carries the per-variant 1B and 7B scores, so each rung recomputes. A single-token sweep of the same set gives 0.632 at 1,001 bp and 0.655 at 2,049 bp, correct in sign but below the 8,192-bp readout.
Human clinical panels. The ClinVar consequence panel has no review-status filter, so 353 of its 3,714 variants (9.5%) carry no review star; excluding them, which leaves 3,361, moves the 1B-to-7B capacity step at the single-token readout from +0.033 to +0.036. The dbNSFP cut keeps 97.3% of the reach panel's pathogenic and 12.8% of its benign variants, moving the class ratio from 1:7.40 to 1:0.98. Membership is by presence in dbNSFP, but the deposited consequence column, and the missense subset cut from it, carry ClinVar's term on ClinVar's chosen transcript, so 16,549 rows (5.0%) show a term outside dbNSFP's scope; 99.2% of them are benign, and without them consequence class alone separates the classes at 0.819 rather than 0.837 (recompute_labelfree_baseline.py). The later weekly ClinVar release that supplies ClinVar's own gene annotation and allele frequencies lacks 81 of the reach panel's variants, which therefore take no ClinVar gene.

## Note S42. Extended Methods: comparators and statistical machinery

We tested whether GERP's no-calls on the atlas are spatially clustered. A runs test on the no-call pattern gives z = −1.28 in pig at a 57.6% no-call rate and −1.83 in horse at 51.5%, close to unclustered, against −7.31 in cattle, −10.86 in dog and −13.87 in human at 4–9% rates. However, the test has no power at the spatial scale of whole-genome alignment gaps. Median spacing between adjacent panel variants within a chromosome is 2.42 Mb in pig and 2.43 Mb in horse, against 0.41 Mb in human, 0.58 Mb in dog and 0.97 Mb in cattle, while alignment blocks and the gaps between them typically run from hundreds of bases to tens of kilobases. At that sampling density a z near zero is what a contiguous alignment gap predicts, and it is not evidence against one. The z values are also not comparable across panels that differ in size, in spacing and in no-call rate. Pig and horse carry the largest must-answer penalties. We report the reach numbers as measured; the cause of the missingness is unresolved. Evo 2 returns a score for every variant of every panel at the single-token readout.
GERP is the appropriate baseline here only on a uniformity argument, and not a strength argument. It is the only per-base conservation score available for all nine assemblies; a genuine UCSC phyloP track exists only for chicken (GRCg6a) and human (GRCh38), the cattle (bosTau9) track used here having been obtained from Zenodo record 13332541 (Roslin) [101] and computed over a different alignment and species set. Where both exist, phyloP is the stronger discriminator in all three: by 0.103 in cattle, 0.068 in chicken and 0.051 in human (Table S8). Every Evo 2-versus-GERP margin we report is therefore an upper bound on the margin over the stronger track, not a conservative one; Note S11 calls the per-base margins conservative only against GERP averaged over the scoring windows. Genuine phyloP [42] was used in two places. It is one of the scores compared in the three-species cross-check above, and it enters the label-free cattle analysis, where per-chromosome bigWigs span the 44.5 M-variant panel (43.4 M non-NaN). Conservation tracks were queried with pyBigWig, and with pybigtools for the phyloP cross-check, under a chromosome-naming normalisation, and the readers differ in what they do when no name matches. The GERP reader, which supplies every GERP reach figure reported here, returns NaN, as do the two phyloP cross-check readers. The two cattle phyloP readers instead open the per-chromosome bigWig named for the variant's chromosome and, where the contig name inside it does not match, read that file's first contig.
Two model comparators were run alongside. Nucleotide Transformer v2 500M multispecies [60] was scored as a masked-marginal reference-versus-alternate log-likelihood ratio over the tokens covering the variant, on the same windows used for the single-token Evo 2 readout; it is a masked rather than an autoregressive model, so it sits outside the scope of our claim about autoregressive likelihood readouts. ESM-2 [109] (esm2_t33_650M_UR50D) was run on the protein-coding subset only, as a masked-marginal ratio between wild-type and mutant residue. It reaches only three of the nine panels, because in the others one class has fewer than ten missense variants, and only human at usable size, 912 variants. The ProteinGym [103] arm used the smaller esm2_t30_150M_UR50D checkpoint. The masked-marginal readout is the one Meier et al. [113] establish for zero-shot variant-effect prediction. Neither is a like-for-like competitor: both bound what the atlas comparison means and do not rank models.
Two supervised baselines are a floor, not competitors (Supplementary Note S5). Both train on raw sequence in a 500-bp window, 250 bases upstream and 249 downstream. The 1D convolutional network takes the stacked reference and alternate windows through two Conv1d blocks of 64 channels and kernel width 9, a global max-pool and a dropout-0.4 head. The second is gradient boosting on k-mer features at k = 4, concatenating the normalised reference spectrum with the alternate-minus-reference difference. Both run over eight species, excluding goat, whose 99 variants are too few to fit; the leave-one-species-out protocol trains on seven and tests on the eighth. The embedding probe of Evo 2-1B reads the blocks.24.mlp.l3 hidden state at the variant offset and fits logistic regression under GroupKFold by gene. Two feature sets were run on a 10,000-variant balanced subset of the eQTL panel and both are reported: the alternate-minus-reference difference reaches a gene-grouped AUROC of 0.504 (95% CI [0.493, 0.516]) and the concatenation of reference, alternate and difference reaches 0.519 ([0.508, 0.531]). The second interval excludes 0.5, so the probe establishes a detectable effect of negligible size; it does not establish a null, and the narrowness of the interval works against us in saying so. Architectures, training schedules and the probe's cross-validation scheme are in Note S33; the dbNSFP field used for each predictor is listed in Additional file 3 (tables/dbnsfp_predictors.csv), and the panel's resampling and pairwise machinery is in Note S32.
To attribute the readout effect to its two causes, we took the per-variant readouts for a 2,599-variant subset and formed two terms from four quantities without re-scoring. The left-context-and-scorer term holds aggregation fixed at a single position and compares the 8,192-bp pass with the 1,001-bp pass (pooled 0.9168 against 0.8792, +0.038); the aggregation term holds context fixed at 8,192 bp and compares the full-window mean with that same single-token delta (pooled 0.9542 against 0.9168, +0.037). The split is path-dependent, so we give both orderings. The two terms above are pooled; taking aggregation first gives, on the macro scale, aggregation +0.071 and context −0.004, and the two orderings differ by −0.040 on that scale, a path dependence rather than a factorial interaction, because the fourth cell of a complete two-by-two — a window mean at 1,001-bp conditioning context — is not part of the design (pooled, the same ordering gives +0.078 and −0.003). In that ordering the first step also moves from the streamed 1,001-bp pass to the 8,192-bp pass, so it changes left context and scorer as well as aggregation; only the context-first aggregation term is measured within one pass. The scales are stated because the equal-halves reading is a property of the pooled context-first split and of no other cell. Under the aggregation-first ordering local aggregation carries the gain, and extending the averaged window past 1 kb adds nothing. Under the context-first ordering the context term is the larger of the two, so that reading is not robust across the orderings. The full-window mean reproduces the deposited 8,192-bp score to within 0.0003 pooled AUROC (Table S9).
Because a percentile interval on nine clusters undercovers, we also computed a bias-corrected and accelerated (BCa) interval on the species-level differences, resampling the nine per-species deltas alone 20,000 times: [+0.073, +0.157] against the percentile [+0.071, +0.153] for the same nine values. This is a different scheme from the paired species-clustered bootstrap given beside the +0.108 head-to-head in Note S11, which resamples species and variants jointly at B = 4,000; resampling the nine species-level values alone is a sensitivity check on clustering and is necessarily narrower (z₀ = +0.029, a = +0.034). The correction shifts the interval marginally upward, widens it by 2% and changes no conclusion. BCa corrects median bias and skewness, not the shortage of degrees of freedom that causes the undercoverage, so it is not a remedy for it. Two methods that do address small k are the t interval on eight degrees of freedom and the bootstrap-t; on these nine deltas the former gives +0.108 [+0.058, +0.159]. We report the t interval as the headline for every nine-species mean, as for Table 2's macro row and for the readout shift (+0.065 [+0.032, +0.097]), and keep the percentile intervals as sensitivities; a reader should treat each of those as somewhat narrower than its nominal coverage, by about five percentage points, since a nominal 95% percentile interval on a mean of nine covers P(|t₈| < 1.96√(8/9)) ≈ 0.90. The Hartung–Knapp intervals of the random-effects pools are the paper's other small-k-valid intervals: the pool the Analyses quote, on locus-resampled standard errors without goat, gives +0.092 [+0.052, +0.132] at 8,192 bp, beside the DeLong-based +0.096 [+0.054, +0.138] kept for comparison (Note S64), and Note S23 pools the within-class margins the same way.
The selection analyses used Spearman rank correlation between minor-allele frequency and predicted deleteriousness. The two quantities are not oriented to the same allele: minor-allele frequency is folded while the deleteriousness score is fixed in the reference-to-alternate direction and is never polarised against an outgroup, so at any site where the alternate allele is the major one the frequency describes the reference allele while the score describes the change away from it. This dilutes the correlation rather than creating it, so it is a reason the measured effect is small and not a reason it exists, and the magnitude should not be interpreted. AUROC in the analysis layer was computed with scikit-learn roc_auc_score. Two deposited components implement it independently and deliberately: tools/verify_from_data.py computes it via the Mann–Whitney U statistic so that the re-derivation imports nothing it is checking, and glmtrust/src/glmtrust/delong.py carries its own midrank routine for the DeLong variance. All three use midranks for ties and agree on every value reported here. Bootstrap resampling units, permutation schemes and the TOST configuration are given in full in Note S34.
Score orientation. The analysis helpers that flip an AUROC below 0.5 upward never fire on the GERP and phyloP columns: unflipped, GERP's AUROC exceeds 0.5 in all nine species.

## Note S43. Per-panel statistics and caveats beyond the printed legends

This note carries the per-panel statistics, intervals and caveats that do not fit the main-text figure legends.
Figure 5. Panel b counts, at each λ, the comparisons whose λ-contaminated intervals are disjoint, computed from the deposited covered AUROCs and pair coverages without resampling, so its curves carry no interval, and neither do a's funnels; at λ = 1 they are the identified 83 and 429. In panel c the seven whole-panel comparisons and the one missense comparison with λ* below 0.001 fall outside the axis and are counted at its left end. Panel d counts dominance at the point estimates; the part the gene-clustered 95% sets of Note S32 keep is in Note S67, and the two open bars are the predictors those sets do not keep.
Figure 9. (a) Pooled AUROC 0.973 [0.968, 0.978] (variant-level bootstrap), drawn as the band of Figure S7a; the unweighted species mean, our primary aggregation, is 0.943 (from 0.974 in cattle and human down to 0.863 in pig). These are separations from population negatives matched only on trinucleotide context (in human, ClinVar benign or likely-benign records), not measures of pathogenicity within a matched sequence class; matched towards missense, the nested missense subset of the 11,109-variant atlas reads 0.812 on 1,285 variants (Fig. 10b); the two are different panels and not one number correcting another. Sensitivity at 90% specificity runs from 0.79 (chicken) to 0.96 (human and cattle). The coding flag reads 0.682 in human, whose negatives are 61.1% coding, and 0.909 to 0.989 in the other eight species; snpEff impact reads 0.924 to 0.991. (b) On the score itself human has the lowest effect size of any species (Cohen's d = 1.09, against 2.35–3.65 in every other species) although its AUROC, 0.974, is among the highest, because its positives, half its panel, bring their long right tail into the pooled standard deviation; on the inverse hyperbolic sine scale the panel draws, every other species' d lies between 2.26 and 3.16 and human's is 2.64. Effect sizes in the four species with fewer than 60 positives (goat, chicken, pig, sheep) are upward-biased by their small positive-class n, so the spread across species is wider than the underlying separation.
Figure 10. (a) Every species gains: on the 11,109 variants carrying both readouts the gain runs from +0.009 in goat to +0.149 in human (Table S1), and by the species mean the whole-atlas effect is +0.065 (Note S27). (b) The interval marks are locus-clustered 95% intervals, two-sample bootstraps within species (B = 2,000, seed 61). From coding to missense the pooled AUROC falls by 0.129, and from all variants, pooled 0.973, by 0.161; AUROC is rank-based, so neither drop depends on prevalence. On the variants of each stratum, all of which carry both readouts, moving from the 1,001-bp single-token to the 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood readout lifts every stratum, missense least: non-coding +0.172, coding +0.132 and all variants +0.092 [+0.083, +0.101] by a variant-level bootstrap, the pooled whole-atlas effect, against +0.013 for missense, whose locus-resampled interval, −0.017 to +0.042, includes zero. (c) At 8,192 bp the margin is positive in all nine species, from +0.023 (horse) to +0.231 (chicken), and its locus-clustered interval excludes zero in chicken, human, dog, cattle and sheep, five of the eight species where it can be estimated. At 1,001 bp human's margin is −0.048 [−0.069, −0.027], and the interval excludes zero only in human and cattle.
Figure S2. (a) Each variant's distance from its own eGene's lead credible-set variant against its Evo 2 score, on the 16,840 of the 20,000 records at a non-zero distance across 1,806 eGenes; the omitted records are the leads themselves, at distance zero. What the panel explains: candidate causal eQTLs read 0.488 (n = 20,000) at 1,001 bp and 0.498 at the matched 8,192-bp readout, against Mendelian strata of 0.941 all-coding, 0.852 non-coding and 0.812 missense (Fig. 10b). The 0.324 drop is against the lowest stratum and the 1,001-bp reading; the matched-readout contrast, both at 8,192 bp, is 0.314. (b) The matched design drawn as matched pairs: 2,783 of the 5,000 candidate causal variants acquired an in-gene comparator and 2,217 did not, and the candidate scores above its own gene's control median in 46.4% of the pairs. Panels a and b are the 20,000-variant panel; panels c and d's sequence and conservation rows are the consequence-matched 2,000-variant subpanel, on which the candidate causal and non-causal |Δ log-likelihood| are also one distribution (median ratio 0.99). Only the subpanel carries an 8,192-bp score, so a's two values differ in panel and readout. (c) Scale does not close the gap: over a 40-fold range (1B→40B) the whole-atlas macro gains +0.075, or +0.043 without human, whose 1B scores are anomalous (Note S15); the eQTL effect size |AUROC − 0.5| reads 0.008 at 1B, 0.012 at 7B and 0.002 at 40B, non-monotone and with overlapping intervals. (d) Every sequence and conservation scorer collapses there: Evo 2-40B 0.498, Nucleotide Transformer [60] 0.486, three GERP flavours 0.473, 0.494 and 0.532, a span of point estimates, not an interval (Supplementary Table S4). The scorers do not share variants: GERP is defined for only 884–1,015, the reach problem inside our own control. The |z| statistic reaches 0.594 but is circular (PIP computed from |z|). The labels are not empty: a positional baseline using no sequence reads 0.651 (Note S4). Deviation from chance is resolvable at this n (permutation p = 0.00025, 20,000 permutations) but negligible: an effect size, not a failed test (Note S9). Supervised models (Enformer [136], Borzoi [137]) predict eQTLs; neither was run here. Bootstrap 2,000 resamples, seed 20260719.

Whether the class shape is a property of the conservation track or of how these panels are built is not settled here, but the deployment cost quantified in this note does not depend on which it is.
