## Note S44. Calibration estimators, and the effect of the binning rule

Expected calibration error is not a single quantity. It is a family of estimators indexed by a binning rule, and on our data the choice of rule changes the sign of the comparison we care about.
We evaluated twelve rules (equal-width and equal-mass, at 5/10/15/20/30/50 bins; Table S22). The formal paired test (stratified bootstrap, macro aggregation) was run on four (equal-width 10 and 15, equal-mass 10 and 15). Three of the four cannot distinguish transferred isotonic calibration from a trivial two-parameter global sigmoid; the fourth (equal-mass 15) favours transfer. We report that split rather than a majority verdict, and we bound the disagreement instead of resolving it: any advantage of the isotonic transfer over the two-parameter sigmoid is at most ≈0.014 ECE under macro aggregation; pooled, the width-10 rule gives 0.039. Neither arm is species-aware (neither conditions on the target species) and calling the isotonic one "species-aware" would overstate what that bound bounds.
**The mechanism of the disagreement is identifiable and worth stating.** The transferred isotonic posterior is a step function taking only a few dozen distinct values per species. An equal-mass rule asked for 15 bins therefore cannot deliver 15: it achieves 6 to 11, depending on species (Table S22, middle panel). The equal-mass estimators are thus not measuring what their name implies. The dissent of mass15 follows from that degeneracy, because the degeneracy is one-sided. The transferred isotonic posterior takes 248 distinct values on this panel against the two-parameter sigmoid's 8,842, so an equal-mass rule collapses for the isotonic arm alone: at 50 requested bins it realises 14 while the sigmoid realises 50. A binned calibration error is upward-biased in the number of bins actually realised, so the sigmoid's bias keeps growing with the requested count while the isotonic arm's saturates, and the difference between the two grows monotonically whether or not either calibrator is the better one. Drawing labels from each arm's own posterior, which makes both calibrated by construction and their true error zero, reproduces that monotone difference in full: it runs from +0.004 at five bins to +0.026 at fifty, larger at every count than the advantage we actually observe. Subtracting each arm's own bias leaves the isotonic advantage negative at every rule, from −0.003 at fifteen bins to −0.015 at fifty. The dissent is an artefact of the estimator, and the null holds.
A corollary that we flag for anyone reusing this code: a binned calibration statistic computed on an isotonic posterior should be treated as suspect by default, and any conclusion resting on one should be repeated under at least one equal-width and one equal-mass rule.

## Note S45. Mondrian conformal prediction, and the scope of the reported arm

Split-conformal prediction was calibrated on the label-rich species and transported to the zero-label targets. We use the Mondrian (class-conditional) construction throughout, which computes a separate conformal quantile within each class. Throughout the trust layer the positive class is a ClinVar pathogenic variant in human and a catalogued disease- or trait-associated OMIA allele in the eight non-human species, and the negative class is the matched population or ClinVar benign set. The isotonic map was fitted on one half of the pooled label-rich variants and the conformal quantiles on the other half, so the layer is held out in goat, chicken and pig only. Table S25 reports all nine species. The six label-rich rows are scored on the variants that fitted the map and set the quantiles, so they are in-sample and are not evidence of transfer. On the calibration half coverage at or above the nominal level follows by construction.
The alternative marginal construction is more attractive on paper, it abstains far less, and it is the arm a reader might assume we used, so Table S25 reports both. Marginally it retains 0.937 coverage by the species mean (0.916 pooled). Its positive-class coverage, however, falls to 0.222 in goat, 0.444 in pig and 0.571 in chicken, and never exceeds 0.732 in any species. A layer that covers the negative class well and misses more than half of the positives is worse than useless in this application, because its headline coverage number conceals exactly the failure a clinician cares about. The conformal layer reported in Notes S10, S49, S63 and S66 is therefore the Mondrian arm, and it is named at each occurrence.
**On validity.** Calibration and test distributions differ across species, so the exchangeability condition required for distribution-free conformal validity does not hold. We therefore report observed cross-species coverage as an empirical result, not as a finite-sample theorem or validated guarantee. Note S47 tests the stricter transported risk-control claim.

## Note S46. Prevalence correction

The prevalence a calibration map is fitted at and the prevalence it is applied at differ, because the donor pool and the held-out panel are not identically composed. Calibrated probabilities were mapped from the donor-pool prevalence to the held-out panel's own construction ratio by Elkan–Saerens prior correction (Table S24). We name the target precisely, because it is easily taken for a deployment prevalence and it is not one: the target column is 0.091 in seven of the nine rows (human is 0.500 and cat 0.092), that is 1/11, the 10:1 ratio the panels were built at, and no screening decision is made at that prevalence. The correction is a leave-one-species-out prior shift that requires only the panel's known construction ratio, as Note S63 states; no deployment prevalence enters.
It reduces macro ECE over the eight non-human species to ≈0.022, and to ≈0.032 over all nine, and is a deterministic transformation of the calibrated score, so it adds no fitting and cannot overfit. It is not uniformly beneficial: chicken worsens slightly (0.020 to 0.025), and human is unchanged at 0.117, and not because the correction is small there. Human carries the largest prior shift in the panel, from a donor-pool prevalence of 0.091 to its own 1:1 construction ratio of 0.500, which is a log-odds move of +2.3 against a mean of about −1.05 for the other eight (range −0.98 to −1.19) and in the opposite direction. The correction moves human's probabilities substantially and leaves its calibration error where it was, which locates that error in the shape of the posterior rather than in its location.

## Note S47. The transported risk-control certificate, and the condition it requires

We attempted to strengthen the trust layer from a coverage statement into a guarantee on the quantity that matters clinically, the missed-positive rate. Using conformal risk control, we calibrated a decision threshold λ on the label-rich species to control the expected positive-class false-negative rate (FNR) at α, then transported λ to each held-out species.
At α = 0.05 the transported threshold failed in every tested source and held-out species: the achieved positive-class FNR is 0.111 in goat, 0.321 in chicken, 0.194 in pig, and 0.075–0.225 across the source species, every one of them a violation, several by more than fourfold. Human is the low end of that range and is the cell the deposited log marks non-informative, because it reaches its 0.075 by calling only 12% of variants benign. The failure is specific to this regime in two respects, and we state both rather than generalise past the evidence. First, it is α-dependent: at α = 0.2 the guarantee holds (achieved FNR ≤ α) in goat (0.111), sheep (0.107), cattle (0.186) and human (0.199). Second, it is a failure of transport, not of the certificate object: on the label-rich ClinVar benchmark the same construction holds in-distribution in 9 of 9 α × score cells under the tolerance that script applies (achieved FNR ≤ α + 0.02 in distribution, ≤ α + 0.03 under shift) and in 7 of 9 under the strict FNR ≤ α rule the cross-species arm uses, the two misses being CADD at α = 0.05 and α = 0.2 by 0.002 and 0.001; the coding→noncoding shift is survived in 9 of 9 strictly (three alphas × three scores, with Evo 2-EVEE probe n = 1,599,200, AlphaMissense n = 204,631, CADD n = 334,153). Two qualifications belong with that count. Three of the nine cells are the Evo 2-EVEE probe, which is circular against these labels (AUROC 0.997) and therefore holds trivially; the non-circular reassurance is 6 of 6 cells, on AlphaMissense and CADD. And the coding→noncoding shift is a shift inside a panel that is 99.7% coding whose noncoding stratum is 81 negatives, so this human benchmark cannot corroborate the eQTL-panel null in either direction and we draw no regulatory conclusion from it. The procedure also remains informative even where it violates, still calling 12–87% of variants benign, 70–87% in eight species and 12% in human, which is the one cell where the procedure is close to degenerate and which the deposited log flags as non-informative, so the non-degeneracy claim holds for the eight and not for human; the transported threshold is simply not valid off its calibration distribution at a strict α.
We report this result because it bounds the conformal coverage interpretation. Cross-species transport of a risk-control target at strict α did not meet its target in these data. A certificate calibrated in distribution, or successful only at a more permissive α, does not establish control for a label-poor species at a strict operating point.

## Note S48. Why Platt transfer and a two-parameter sigmoid are the same operation, and the estimator-dependent per-species split

We caution against reading this as a novel cross-species phenomenon. Transferring a Platt map [138] across species is, by construction, the same operation as fitting a single two-parameter global sigmoid: a Platt map has no species structure, so it is fitted once on the pooled donor species and applied unchanged. The two are therefore identical by definition, not as an empirical finding, and we report the comparison once. The substantive comparison is therefore whether a flexible nonparametric calibrator (isotonic) beats two global parameters for this cross-species transfer, both fitted on the same pooled donor species; it is a null. (Neither arm is species-aware; a genuinely clade-aware calibrator is a separate question we do not resolve here.) Under a paired stratified bootstrap with macro aggregation, the ΔECE interval crosses zero under three of the four binning estimators we tested: equal-width 10 bins, −0.0019 (95% CI [−0.0086, +0.0003]); equal-width 15 bins, −0.0004 ([−0.0076, +0.0021]); equal-mass 10 bins, +0.0007 ([−0.0073, +0.0042]). The fourth, equal-mass 15 bins, clears zero in transfer's favour (−0.0067, [−0.0140, −0.0019]). The defensible summary is quantitative, not dichotomous: any advantage of the transferred isotonic map over two globally fitted parameters is bounded above by approximately 0.014 ECE (the lower confidence limit of the single transfer-favouring estimator, equal-mass 15 bins), and under the other three estimators cannot be distinguished from zero at all. That macro null conceals a per-species split we report rather than average over: on the operating-point ECE estimator (Supplementary Table S33) the transferred map is worse than the trivial sigmoid in six of the nine species (goat, chicken, pig, horse, cat, dog) and better in the other three (sheep, cattle, human), which are not simply the three largest panels, since dog and cat are both larger than sheep. Under the paper's primary equal-width-10 estimator (Table S23) the same comparison is worse in only four of nine (goat, pig, cat, dog) and its macro slightly favours transfer (−0.0019), so even the direction of the per-species split is estimator-dependent, and we name the estimator with each count rather than report one as canonical. Because we argue elsewhere that a binned statistic is the wrong instrument at this scale, we also adjudicate the same comparison with the proper scoring rule: against that same leave-one-species-out sigmoid the macro Brier difference is −0.0019 in transfer's favour and isotonic transfer beats it in seven of nine species, against five of nine on equal-width-10 ECE. The per-variant table's global_sigmoid column is a pooled in-sample fit rather than the leave-one-species-out comparator, and is not the column read here. The macro conclusion is unchanged either way (the advantage is of order 0.001), but the per-species direction is a property of the estimator rather than of the calibration maps, which is the point. An in-species oracle ranges from marginally worse than transfer to 10.8× better where labels exist, 0.9×, i.e. slightly worse, in goat, rising to 6.8× in cattle and 10.8× in human (Supplementary Table S33). The honest reading is that the cross-species transferred map recovers only a fraction of what target-species labels would buy and does not reliably beat two global parameters in any single species, which is why we present it as a bounded, not a positive, result.

## Note S49. The cost matrix behind the always-abstain result, and the break-even prices

A cost-sensitive decision analysis exposed a further, uncomfortable limit that we report in full. Under an asymmetric cost matrix penalising missed positives, a trivial always-abstain policy incurred a pooled cost of 0.500 and beat every learned decision cell, including the best Evo 2-based rule (0.540) and its Nucleotide Transformer [60] counterpart (0.540). At this cost structure, pooled, calibration plus selective abstention added no decision value over simply deferring every case. That verdict is aggregation-specific and we state it rather than let the pooled figure stand alone: expected cost is prevalence-sensitive, human is the only 1:1 panel and carries roughly 5.5 times the miss-cost exposure of a 10:1 panel, and under the unweighted species mean this paper takes as primary the same Evo 2 policies read 0.447 (raw threshold), 0.505 (reject-option) and 0.489 (Mondrian class-conditional conformal) against the same 0.500 floor, so two of the three come in under blanket abstention macro while none does pooled. The Nucleotide Transformer cells do not beat the floor under either aggregation (0.514 and 0.546 macro). Both aggregations are deposited. The cost structure has to be stated for that sentence to mean anything. Missing a positive is charged 10, a false alarm 1, an abstention 0.5 and a correct call 0. The always-abstain floor is therefore exactly 0.500 by construction, and pooled, no learned policy for either backbone reaches it: the best Evo 2 cell is 0.540 at an abstain rate of 0.70, i.e. 30% coverage, and the best Nucleotide Transformer cell is also 0.540, but it gets there by abstaining on roughly 97% of variants, approaching the zero-information floor from above without reaching it. Macro, that changes for Evo 2 and not for its comparator, as above. The identical figure is a coincidence of two very different operating points, not a shared result. Two consequences follow, and the second is the one that matters. Raising the miss penalty further does not rescue answering; it entrenches abstention. Answering only becomes worthwhile once deferral is made expensive relative to errors (the two-action break-even is an abstention price of about 0.56 for Evo 2; the corresponding figure for Nucleotide Transformer exceeds 1, which is outside the range in which the two-action comparison is meaningful, since an abstention costing more than an error is never worth taking; we therefore report it as outside the meaningful range rather than as a comparable price, though the deposited value is about 1.13), or once the miss penalty is softened: at a 2:1 miss-to-false-alarm ratio both backbones beat blanket abstention (Evo 2 about 0.14, NT about 0.42). The self-refuting result is therefore specific to a cost matrix that prices misses ten times a false alarm, which is a defensible choice for clinical screening and a pessimistic one for triage.

## Note S50. Whether abstention flags regulatory variants without labels

The eQTL-panel null would be of limited practical use if it could only be detected with the very labels non-model species lack. A class-conditional (Mondrian) conformal abstention layer [139], trained only on coding variants and applied unchanged, abstained on 72% of regulatory variants versus 63% of held-out coding variants. Those rates are computed on 20,000 regulatory and 803 held-out coding variants, and on those denominators the elevation is resolvable (two-proportion z = 5.2). We nonetheless decline to read it as a warning signal, because the two panels differ in composition as well as in class, not because the elevation is unresolved: at n = 20,000 the Wilson interval on 0.72 is [0.714, 0.726] and excludes the coding rate. What label-free warning survives rests on the direction being right, not on this difference being real. This label-free signal is only partial, however: on the independent eQTL panel the calibrated pathogenicity estimates were low and confident in their neutrality (mean calibrated probability 0.069; 96% below 0.10). They were confident, not uncertain. Whether they were wrong depends on what the probability is taken to mean, and this deserves stating plainly because it bounds the claim. Fine-mapped cis-eQTLs are common, segregating regulatory variants under weak or no purifying selection; they are not Mendelian disease alleles. A low pathogenicity probability is therefore the defensible answer for most of them, and the calibration map was in any case fitted where "positive" means "OMIA disease variant" and applied where "positive" means "candidate causal for expression", different labels, so the transferred probability has no calibrated meaning on this panel. The near-chance AUROC is a discrimination result and stands independently of that calibration point: it means the model cannot rank fine-mapped candidate causal variants above non-causal ones drawn from the same eGenes, a failure of regulatory discrimination that is well-posed whether or not these common, weakly selected variants would ever be called pathogenic. It is therefore not dissolved by observing that a low pathogenicity probability is the appropriate answer for most of them. What the panel does show is that the model's confidence carries no warning signal here: it is equally confident on candidate causal and non-causal regulatory variants, so uncertainty cannot be used to flag the region. Abstention therefore provides at best a weak, partial warning that a regulatory prediction should be distrusted, not a reliable self-detection of the variants the model reads near chance.

## Note S51. The full risk-coverage curve and the random-refusal control

The gain comes from genuine selectivity and not simply from answering less: a random-refusal control stays essentially flat across the entire coverage range, varying only between 0.051 and 0.059 (Supplementary Table S26) against the selective curve's fall from 0.058 to 0.038. Macro selective error fell from 0.058 at full coverage to 0.038 at 85% coverage and then flattened (full risk-coverage curve in Supplementary Table S26 and plotted in Figure S4c). The 85% figure is where the curve flattens, not a resolved optimum: macro selective error reads 0.0376, 0.0377 and 0.0378 at 85%, 80% and 75% coverage, which this panel cannot separate. The operating point was located in sample, though relocating it on eight species and re-measuring on the ninth returns 85% coverage every time (Note S10). Refusing beyond that point buys nothing and eventually costs, and the macro and pooled curves diverge there in a way that is itself diagnostic. The macro (unweighted-species) error shows a local bump at 45% coverage (0.046) and then falls again at 40% and below (0.044, 0.043, 0.041 at 40%, 35% and 30%), because at low coverage the small panels (goat, chicken and pig) can reach zero error and pull the unweighted mean down. The pooled (variant-weighted) error does the opposite: it rises monotonically from its 85% minimum (0.059) to 0.103 at 30% coverage and crosses the do-nothing baseline of 0.077 at 50% coverage, all four figures on the isotonic arm this table tabulates rather than on the Platt arm that is the paper's per-variant deliverable, whose baseline is 0.080, minimum 0.058 at 80% and crossing 35%, so below roughly half coverage the layer is worse than answering every variant. The retained set becoming small and unrepresentative is exactly this mechanism. The retained-set sensitivity falls in step (from 0.67 at full coverage to 0.43 at 50%, 0.24 at 30% and 0.07 at 20% coverage, while specificity climbs towards 1.00) so pushed hard the layer answers "benign" to almost everything (Supplementary Table S26). We therefore confine the recommended operating range to 85–100% coverage and warn against deployment below 50% coverage. The operating point is therefore an empirically located knee at approximately 85% coverage, not a free parameter to be pushed.

## Note S52. The trust layer rebuilt at the 8,192-bp readout

**We also rebuilt the whole layer at the 8,192-bp readout, and report it because the objection is obvious.** Every component of this section (the calibration transfer, the confidence ordering, the selective refusal and the confusion matrix) is computed at the 1,001-bp readout, while the paper argues elsewhere that the 8,192-bp readout is the right one. It survives, and improves on every axis (Supplementary Table S30): with the score changed from the 1,001-bp to the 8,192-bp readout, the error count of the deployable leave-one-species-out arm falls from 893 to 415 (8.02% of the 11,130 calls at 1,001 bp to 3.74% of the 11,109 the 8,192-bp panel scores; no in-species oracle is involved), the same 15% refusal captures 61.0% of them rather than 35.4%, the pooled lift rises from 2.35 to 4.07, and macro calibration error falls from 0.053 to 0.021. The two arms are not calibrated identically, and we name the calibrators rather than imply a single construction: the deployed 1,001-bp arm is a Platt map (8,792 distinct posteriors) and the 8,192-bp arm is isotonic (218 distinct). The 218 is deposited per variant as Additional file 2: reports/fig4_pervariant_8192.parquet (11,109 rows, 218 distinct isotonic posteriors), beside the 1,001-bp panel and its 248 distinct isotonic posteriors over 11,130 rows, so both arms can be checked independently. The like-for-like isotonic 1,001-bp error count is 861 (the leave-one-species-out isotonic arm, as at Table S26), so the isotonic-to-isotonic comparison is 861 → 415 and about 32 of the drop is the calibrator rather than the readout. Human's error-detection AUROC rises from 0.474 to 0.828, consistent with the composition control of Note S66. We keep the 1,001-bp layer as the headline because the reach and calibration analyses were built there and cover all 11,130 variants rather than the 11,109 carrying an 8,192-bp score; the rebuild is the evidence that the choice does not flatter the result.

## Note S53. Calibration-set size, the estimator sweep, and what the twelve estimators agree on

First, transfer quality is insensitive to how much calibration data the map is fitted on: across calibration sets spanning 25 to 10,327 variants the transferred adaptive ECE is flat within noise (0.030–0.058, Supplementary Table S23). That cuts two ways, and we report both. It means the smallest panels are not overfitting (goat's 99 variants are not the reason transfer underperforms), but it does not show that additional target-representative data could never close the gap to the in-species fit. Second, an in-species oracle calibrated on target labels is roughly 3.4-fold better than transfer on macro ECE (0.0157 against 0.0533, equal-width 10-bin; the deposited artefact). The lower in-species ECE shows that target-label calibration can fit these panels in sample; it does not establish prospective performance or a universal transport failure. Calibration is doing genuine work in absolute terms, since both routes clear a base-rate-constant floor in all nine species (the constant-predictor ECE is 0.12–0.16 across the eight non-human species, and 0.409 for human, whose donor pool sits at a prevalence of 0.091 against a 1:1 panel, so human's transferred 0.117 clears its own floor by the widest margin of any species) but it is work that a single two-parameter global sigmoid does about as well as the transferred isotonic map (Note S48). The full twelve-estimator sweep is in Supplementary Table S22, with per-species values in Table S23; across those twelve, ten lean towards transfer and two towards the sigmoid, all by margins under 0.012 ECE, so the four we bootstrap-tested (equal-width and equal-mass binning at 10 and 15 bins) are, if anything, the conservative subset rather than a favourable selection. The result was also method-agnostic: isotonic [140], Platt and beta calibration [141] gave near-identical adaptive ECE (median 0.054, 0.047 and 0.049 respectively; total spread 0.007), so the lack of a large observed advantage was not unique to isotonic regression in this estimator sweep.

## Note S54. Donor pools, the leave-one-species-out folds and the tie problem

Note S63 keeps the donor-pool definitions, the tie problem that decides which posterior the selective layer uses, and the estimator bake-off result; the per-fold detail is below.
The donor pool is defined by construction and therefore not tabulated fold by fold, but the two arms of this paper define it differently, so we state both instead of describing one by the other's rule. For the 1,001-bp calibration analysis behind Table S23 and Figure S4b, a donor must carry at least 50 positives, which admits six species (sheep, horse, cat, cattle, dog, human); a held-out donor species therefore fits on five and a held-out label-poor species on six, and goat, chicken and pig are targets only, never entering any training pool. Transfer into them is measured, and Table S23 is exactly that held-out test; what is untested is the reciprocal direction, a map donated by a label-poor species, which we treat as a limit of that arm. glmtrust.transfer.leave_one_group_out implements the all-other-groups rule with no positive-count threshold, so it reproduces the 8,192-bp layer and not Table S23; it does skip a fold in which either side carries a single class, which cannot arise on these nine species. Per-fold transfer results are in Supplementary Table S23. The probability calibration assessed for expected calibration error is the transferred isotonic map (Tables S22 to S24 and S33). The per-variant selective-prediction results at 1,001 bp (Tables S27 to S29 and S32, the Platt panel of Table S26, the 1,001-bp selective values of Tables S30 and S31, and the per-species operating points behind Figure S4a), however, are computed on the two-parameter Platt posterior fit on the same LOSO pool, and the transferred isotonic posterior takes only 248 distinct values across the 11,130 variants (14–43 per species, 99.63% of variants inside tie blocks), so the least-confident-15% boundary falls inside a tie block in every species and the refusal set would be resolved by sort order rather than by confidence. The Platt posterior as plotted in Figure S4a is the p column of reports/fig4_pervariant.parquet and has 8,792 distinct values; the deposited refit is the platt_LOSO column of reports/_recon_pervariant_trust.parquet and has 8,842. They are two objects, not two names for one, and either resolves the ordering. The two posteriors are close but not interchangeable at the 0.5 decision threshold, and we separate the two quantities that are easy to conflate: their decisions differ on 64 of the 11,130 variants, while their error counts differ by 30 (861 is the isotonic_LOSO column and 891 the platt_LOSO column of the same deposited refit, reports/_recon_pervariant_trust.parquet; 893 is the p column of reports/fig4_pervariant.parquet, the posterior as plotted, and the denominator used throughout this file. The two Platt columns differ by at most 0.0103 in probability and flip six calls across the 0.5 threshold, five in human and one in cattle; four of those flips create an error and two remove one, so the net of two is what separates 891 from 893, and it lands as one further error in each of cattle and human), a net difference between two totals, not the number of variants on which the two maps disagree. The selective layer's deliverable therefore rests on the two-parameter map, whose ranking against isotonic and beta depends on which estimator is used (median adaptive ECE Platt 0.047, isotonic 0.054, beta 0.049; but pooled L1 isotonic 0.0513 against Platt 0.0563, and debiased L2 isotonic 0.0041 against Platt 0.0050), so the choice rests on tie resolution rather than on accuracy, and "trivial" throughout names its parameter count, not its accuracy. The scheme is a leave-one-species-out (LOSO) protocol, and the transferred maps were compared against an in-species oracle (StratifiedKFold). A nearest-relative transfer on a clade map (ruminant, carnivore, bird, suid, perissodactyl, primate) is not reported: chicken and pig have no same-clade donor among the six label-rich species, and in goat it rests on nine positives. Calibration quality was quantified by expected calibration error (ECE), with debiased/adaptive ECE and a Kolmogorov–Smirnov check as supporting diagnostics (the KS check could not reject perfect calibration of the transferred map in two of nine species). To test whether cross-species transfer is a distinctive phenomenon, isotonic transfer was benchmarked against two deliberately trivial baselines fit on the same sources: a pooled two-parameter global sigmoid (Platt) map and a base-rate-constant predictor; a calibrator bake-off additionally compared isotonic, Platt and beta calibration. Prevalence-corrected probabilities were obtained by Elkan–Saerens prior correction [142,143], mapping the donor-pool prevalence to the held-out panel's own construction ratio rather than to any deployment prevalence. Table S24's source column is the leave-one-species-out donor-pool prevalence under the same fifty-positive floor this arm uses, admitting six donors, and it reconciles with the panel counts in all nine species to three decimal places. Computing it instead over all eight other species, with no positive-count floor, gives values 0.005 to 0.015 lower in the eight non-human species and an indistinguishable value for human, and is the wrong rule for this arm.

## Note S55. Bootstrap units and the smallest-panel endpoint

Paired calibration-transfer deltas are bootstrapped within species, whereas between-species estimands resample the nine species. The per-species intervals in the main Table 2 are locus-clustered resamples over positive-bearing loci. In the smallest panel, some goat replicates contain few positives and return AUROC 1.0, which is one of the reasons goat carries no interval and is reported as a descriptive point estimate. A deposited within-class resample of the same data also closes at 1.000; the endpoint is therefore a property of the small panel rather than a claim of perfect separation. Percentile intervals with nine species clusters can under-cover regardless of the number of resamples, and the manuscript treats them as descriptive bounds rather than universal guarantees.
The atlas identification verdicts were resampled in the scheme of Table 2's intervals, a two-sample locus-clustered bootstrap (B = 20,000, seed 20260923) that draws positive-bearing 100-kb loci whole and negatives individually and recomputes both predictors' bounds on every replicate, Evo 2's charging the 21 variants it leaves unscored at 8,192 bp (Note S27). Loci are keyed on the physical chromosome, because seven species carry some positives under a RefSeq accession and others under a bare chromosome name. The verdict at the point estimates, six of nine species, holds in 100.0% of replicates in cattle, human and dog, in 99.8% in chicken and in 93.5% in sheep, whose margin over the bound runs from −0.010 to +0.076 between the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. Goat's nine positives occupy two loci, so the resample admits three distinct positive sets and in about a quarter of replicates leaves GERP with no scorable positive; goat is therefore not estimable, as Table 2 reports no interval for it. Counted over the eight species that can be estimated, the verdict holds at the point estimates in five, and in four of them in at least 95% of replicates, the count the Analyses report. The three unidentified species are identified in 25.4% (cat), 5.6% (horse) and 7.8% (pig) of replicates. The script is recompute_endpoint_fragility.py in Additional file 3.

Together the fifteen arms above and the interval script reproduce 1,391 reported values. Of those, 311 are checkable only with licensed score values, a dbNSFP copy, the CADD, REVEL and AlphaMissense releases or the CADD scores TraitGym distributes, because the deposit withholds the values they compare against; the five scripts concerned check their other values first and then exit 77 without them. The remaining 1,080 reproduce from the deposited additional files alone. A licensed rebuild is checked before the four of those scripts that read a rebuilt panel use it: regenerate_full_panels.py in Additional file 3 compares its keys and the missingness of every score column with the deposit, and each withheld score column with a SHA-256 digest of the authors' values (tables/licensed_value_digests.json), which confirms the values without disclosing them.
Declared not recomputable from the deposit, with the reason printed by the relevant script: the sixteen convolutional-baseline cells of Figure S5c (recorded as per-species AUROCs only); the two Note S35 claims that no variant was dropped for a reference mismatch (they need the 1,001-bp window sequences, which are not deposited; the tables hold only post-gate variants); the Note S31 call-rate threshold sweep (it read PLINK2 allele-frequency files not in the deposit; the table establishes the constant-denominator premise but does not re-run the sweep); and the interval-width ratio, the gene-key fragmentation and the median interval narrowing of Note S28 (they need the gene string before canonicalisation, which no deposited table carries). verify_published_literals.py in Additional file 3 checks every value each script asserts as published against the text of this manuscript, this supplement and the figure canvases; with Additional files 4 to 6 placed in tables/ as their READMEs direct, it locates all 1,096 of them, 582 graded strong or moderate by the tool and 514 weak, and 711 of 711 from Additional file 3 alone. Without licensed score values the five scripts that need them register only their licence-free literals, and from Additional file 3 alone the six that need Additional files 4 to 6 register none, as the tool's own NOT COVERED block reports; with the licensed values in place it locates all 1,407. A literal is located only where it does not sit inside a longer number, such as a DOI, and a literal of two or more numbers only where the paper prints it as one phrase, verbatim or in a printed form the tool registers, which must carry exactly the literal's numbers. Its literals are not the same set as the reproduced values counted above: they also include the sixteen convolutional-baseline cells and the two Note S35 claims, which the scripts declare not recomputable, and they omit two provenance statements of recompute_consequence.py that the Methods make in words, without a literal.

## Note S57. Table 2 intervals: the locus-clustered estimator in full

The per-species intervals in Table 2 are locus-clustered two-sample bootstraps at both readouts, B = 20,000: catalogued disease alleles cluster by locus while the trinucleotide-matched negatives do not, so positives are resampled as whole 100-kb loci and negatives individually. Relative to the variant-level intervals this widens cat by 1.51× at 8,192 bp and 1.25× at 1,001 bp, horse by 1.46× at 1,001 bp and chicken by 1.16× at 8,192 bp. Every other cell moves by less than 1.10×. Goat carries no interval: its nine positives occupy two loci, eight within a 570-bp span, giving 1.2 effective independent positives, so the positive class admits three distinct resamples and a cluster-robust interval on one degree of freedom spans more than 0.23 AUROC units. Its AUROC is a descriptive point estimate. The macro row carries a species-clustered t interval on eight degrees of freedom, which is a different construction from the locus-clustered percentile intervals in the per-species rows, and the reason is that nine clusters cannot support a percentile interval that covers. Resampling nine values from nine shrinks their spread by a further square root of eight ninths and applies a normal quantile where Student's t on eight degrees of freedom is required, so the percentile reads 0.048 and 0.046 wide against 0.061 and 0.058 for the t at the 8,192-bp and 1,001-bp readouts. Only this row changes estimator; the per-species rows and the head-to-head comparisons keep the percentile construction they are quoted with throughout, and the t interval is the same small-sample remedy as the Hartung–Knapp interval already reported for the pooled meta-analysis. The observed species range is 0.863 to 0.974 at 8,192 bp and 0.825 to 0.951 at 1,001 bp. All three estimators and the per-species cluster counts are in Supplementary Note S29, and the script that reproduces every printed endpoint from the deposited nine-species table is in Additional file 3.

## Note S58. The regeneration scope of the deposited archives

From Additional file 2 alone the deposit's own tables report (reports/tables.md, tables D1 to D4) and Figures S1, S2 and S4 regenerate. Figs. 9 and 10 and Figure S7 regenerate from Additional file 2 together with three tables from Additional file 3 (fig1_atlas_pervariant.parquet, table2_cluster_aware_ci.tsv and refseq_chromosome_map.csv; Fig. 10 also reads atlas_gerp_windows.parquet), and Figs. 1 to 8 and Figure S8 are built by Additional file 3's own scripts from the inputs in its figures/ directory, which its prep scripts regenerate from its tables and Additional files 4 to 6, four of those prep scripts only with the licensed score values; all fifteen were checked by rebuilding them from clean extractions of the deposited archives and comparing every text span against the submitted PDF. The builders for Figures S3, S5 and S6, and the scripts that write the recompute-layer files, additionally read the primary score and label tree, which is not deposited, so they exit with a missing-path error naming the file they wanted.
Additional file 3 holds fifteen recompute scripts, nine of which run from Additional file 3 alone (recompute_dbnsfp.py and recompute_labelfree_baseline.py additionally need Additional file 4, recompute_pp3bp4.py Additional file 5, recompute_clinvar_reach.py and recompute_identification.py both, and recompute_fig1cde.py Additional file 6), an interval script, a data dictionary for every table, three per-table manifests carrying byte sizes and digests, one of them with per-column missing-value counts, a MANIFEST.sha256 with the SHA-256 digest of every file, a run_all.sh that runs every script and reports each exit code, the builder scripts that produced the tables from the undeposited tree, the builders of Figures 1 to 8 and Figure S8, a 90-variant set of scoring windows with their expected scores, and the smaller tables themselves; Additional files 4 to 6 hold the three tables too large to travel with them. Together they cover nine analysis arms: the BRCA1 saturation-genome-editing positive control (3,893 substitutions); the consequence-stratified ClinVar panel behind Table S19 (3,714 variants); the massively parallel splicing benchmark behind Table S34 and Tables S16, S17 and S21 (3,912 variants across six assays); the 49-predictor dbNSFP reach and partial-identification analysis (328,328 variants, with a companion 352,907-row key file so that the review-status filter can be executed rather than assumed, the breakdown points of all 1,176 comparisons behind Note S67, and the per-gene bounds behind Note S70); the human ClinVar reach panel behind Table S11, in which CADD, REVEL, AlphaMissense, phyloP and phastCons are named (1,484,836 rows, from which the paper's 1,434,335-variant panel is cut by the deposited filter), with the calibrated-evidence and label-circularity analyses of Tables S36, S37 and S42 (7,479 ClinGen expert-panel classifications, 2,097,787 counted variants of uncertain significance) and the identification analyses of Note S61; the nine-species amniote panel behind Figures 9, 10, S5 and S7, Table 2 and Tables S3, S13 and S35, the orthology transfer of Note S68, GPN-Star's scores for its chicken and human variants and SIFT's scores wherever SIFT answers, with the 500,000-variant simulated deployment background behind Table 1; the cattle, bat and cross-species panels behind Figure S3 (28,006, 23,888 and 11,130 variants); the strand panel of Notes S36 and S37 (327 variants), with TraitGym's Mendelian readouts (3,380 variants); and the values audited from 16 benchmark articles (Note S74). Each script reads only the deposited tables by relative path, imports only numpy, pandas, pyarrow, scipy and the standard library, prints each reported value beside the value it recomputes, and exits non-zero if any disagrees. Run from a directory containing nothing but the tables and the scripts, with the analysis tree unreachable, eight of the fifteen complete and exit zero. With Additional files 4 to 6 placed in tables/ as their READMEs direct, ten exit zero and five exit 77 after checking every value they can without licensed input, the deposit's code for a licensed input being absent rather than a value disagreeing. Together the fifteen recompute scripts and the interval script reproduce 1,391 reported values, 311 of which need licensed score values: a dbNSFP copy for the dbNSFP arm and the identification analyses, the CADD, REVEL and AlphaMissense releases for the human ClinVar arms, and the CADD scores TraitGym distributes for nine values of the strand arm. Missingness is preserved rather than cleaned: predictor non-response is the subject of this article, and each README states whether its table carries non-response as a Parquet null or as a NaN inside a float column, because the two require different tests and the deposit uses both conventions.
What these tables do not close. They are analysis-ready, not score-regenerating: the Evo 2 scores are inputs to them. Re-deriving those scores needs a GPU and the Evo 2 scoring stack, so the deposit supports re-analysing the scores as deposited rather than rescoring the panels from sequence. Three further limits are declared rather than left to be discovered. The sixteen convolutional-baseline cells of Figure S5c cannot be recomputed from any table, because the baseline records them as per-species AUROCs only. The claims in Note S35 that no variant was dropped for a reference mismatch, for the ClinVar and the splicing panels alike, are panel-construction claims needing the 1,001-bp window sequences, which are not deposited; the deposited tables contain only variants that already passed those gates, so they are consistent with the claims but are not tests of them. And the call-rate threshold sweep supporting Note S31 read PLINK2 allele-frequency files that are not part of the deposit; the deposited table establishes the premise on which that sweep rests — a constant allele number of 14,788, recovered at 100.0% of sites — but does not re-run the sweep. Each of these is printed by the relevant script in an explicit "not recomputable" block, with its reason, rather than being dropped from the count. Three analysed inputs lie outside the deposit and so outside that count: the ESM-2 scores the authors computed for the 644,387 ProteinGym single mutants of the calibration arm of Note S5, the frozen scores of the EVEE shard behind the human ClinVar benchmark of Notes S31 and S47, and the pig eQTL panel's allele frequencies of Note S17, which Additional file 2 rebuilds from PigGTEx's genotype release. The values they yield are reported from Additional file 2 (logs/proteingym_grounding.md, logs/clinvar_calibration.md, logs/clinvar_ci.md, logs/crc_clinvar.md and analyses/results/eqtl_maf_matched_auroc.json), whose builders are deposited with them, and no script in Additional file 3 recomputes them. The per-variant scores of Nucleotide Transformer v2 (Notes S4 and S42) and of the eQTL window ablation (Note S4) are not deposited either; only their summaries are, in Additional file 2's reports/nt_vs_evo2.parquet and logs/eqtl_ablation.md.

## Note S59. Extended figure legends

The ten main-text figure legends and the legends of Figures S2 to S5 are given here in full; Figures S1, S6, S7 and S8 carry their full legends with the figures. The legends printed with the main-text figures give the panel construction a reader needs to interpret each plate; every value and every interpretive remark is in the full legends below.
Figure 1. What a comparison is worth when predictors decline to answer. The toy panel is constructed, not sampled: eight pathogenic and eight benign variants, three predictors, and scores chosen so that each verdict of the accounting appears once. Each predictor is named by its pair-coverage regime glyph beside its letter, the glyph's shape and colour marking the regime as in every later figure: a navy circle for ρ ≥ 0.999 (A), a steel square for ½ ≤ ρ < 0.999 (C) and a crimson triangle for ρ < ½ (B), keyed at the top right. Each panel is titled by its step, and the panels read left to right, then down. (a) Rows are predictors and columns variants; tile shade is the score, empty tiles are declined variants, and the dotted outlines mark B's declared scope, four pathogenic and two benign variants, set slightly apart. Beside each row are the pathogenic–benign pairs the predictor ranks, 64, 8 and 49 of 64, and its AUROC on those pairs, 0.97, 1.00 and 0.80. (b) Each 8 × 8 grid is one predictor's pair space, pathogenic rows by benign columns: a pair is ranked right, ranked wrong, or not ranked because the predictor declined at least one of its variants. No toy pair is tied; the key's tied state serves panel c. The share ranked is ρ, the pair coverage of the Analyses: 1.00, 0.125 and 0.77. (c) How the sharp bound is made, for B. Its eight ranked pairs (dotted outline) are all right; counting its 56 unranked pairs all wrong gives the lower bound ρAcov = 0.125, counting them as ties gives the must-answer AUROC 0.5 + (Acov − 0.5)ρ = 0.56, and counting them all right gives the upper bound ρAcov + 1 − ρ = 1.00; the arrow carries the three values to B's row in d. (d) The AUROC each predictor would reach had it scored all 16 variants lies in its sharp bound (band with end caps); the covered AUROC is the regime marker, the must-answer AUROC the diamond at the bound's midpoint, and the dashed line marks chance. A answers every variant, so its three values coincide at 0.97 and only its marker is drawn; B's bound runs from 0.125 to 1.00 and C's from 0.61 to 0.84. (e) A comparison lives in the plane of the two predictors' AUROCs on the whole panel. Each predictor's bound is an interval on its own axis (bands along the axes, as in d), so the pair's uncertainty is the rectangle of the two, centred on the two must-answer values (diamond), and the difference is identified exactly when the rectangle lies on one side of the dotted diagonal of equal accuracy. Each plane is titled by its verdict and draws the rectangle, or the line it becomes where one bound is a point, in the verdict's colour: identified (ink), not identified (grey) or never identifiable (crimson). A and C are identified: A's bound is a point and C's lies below it. A and B are not: B's bound reaches above A's value, so the segment crosses the diagonal, although their pair coverages sum to 1.125, above one. B and C can never be identified: their pair coverages sum to 0.89, so the rectangle's sides, 0.88 and 0.23, sum to more than one and it crosses the diagonal wherever it is placed in the unit square (the dotted rectangles are the same rectangle in the two extreme corners). A and C's pair coverages sum to 1.77. The ink point labelled 'Pairs both rank' is B against A on the six variants both answer, where B ranks every pair right and A three in four: B leads, 1.00 against 0.75, with no assumption. The Analyses apply the same quantities to 49 published predictors (Figures 2 and 3).
Figure 2. Reach of 49 published predictors, as distributed by dbNSFP 5.3.1a, across 328,328 ClinVar substitutions. The nonsynonymous and splice-site substitutions dbNSFP 5.3.1a annotates: 328,328 variants across 15,606 genes, 166,149 pathogenic and 162,179 benign. Predictors are read from their rank-score columns and named as in Table S40, the six phyloP and phastCons tracks without their species-set suffix (phyloP100way for phyloP100way_vertebrate) and the two Eigen scores without theirs (Eigen-raw for Eigen-raw_coding); the rows are ordered by whole-panel reach and grouped by pair-coverage regime, 5 with ρ ≥ 0.999, 13 with ½ ≤ ρ < 0.999 and 31 with ρ < ½, set apart by white space under a header naming the regime and its count, and each name carries its regime's glyph (navy circle, steel square, crimson triangle), as in Figure 3. Which variants each predictor declines is drawn rather than summarised: the 328,328 variants are ordered by how many of the 49 answer them and binned into 700 columns, and each cell is the share of that slice the predictor declines, drawn in blue, darkest where it declines all of it, so a row's blue is its silence. The panel does not degrade smoothly; it has a wall. The strips above give each slice's answer depth, its pathogenic share and its variant types, read from ClinVar's own consequence term (missense, nonsense or canonical splice-site, and the rest, left white, 5.7% of the panel: initiator-codon and stop-lost variants, and the 16,549 whose ClinVar term lies outside dbNSFP's scope but which dbNSFP includes by its own annotation, Note S41); the dotted rule marks where a variant stops being answered by more than 20 of the 49: to its right lies 36.7% of the panel, holding 63.6% of all the pathogenic variants. The wall is where the missense predictors' declared scope ends: of the variants beyond it only 1.0% are missense, and 105,204 of their 105,589 pathogenic variants (99.6%) are nonsense or canonical splice-site variants. The glyphs left of the raster give each predictor's training labels (clinical assertions, population data, evolutionary proxy labels, unsupervised, or a conservation track), whether ClinVar is among its training data (filled square), used only for testing, calibration or evaluation (open square) or not at all (dot) (Table S41), and its declared scope on four nested levels, genome-wide, coding (MutationTaster's gene-internal scope drawn there, though every panel variant lies inside a gene and Note S69 judges MutationTaster on the whole panel), missense or rare missense (Table S40). At right, each predictor's reach on the pathogenic (orange) and the benign variants (grey, drawn larger so that equal reach reads as a ring), joined by a hairline: each of the 30 missense predictors with ρ below one half answers about a third of the pathogenic variants and more than twice that share of the benign, which is the class-dependent silence a covered AUROC does not see. Per-predictor reach by class is in Table S40 and its summary by reach band in Table S12, and each predictor's provenance in Tables S40 and S41.
Figure 3. Sharp bounds and pairwise identification of 49 published predictors. The 49 predictors and the dbNSFP panel of Figure 2. (a) The bound and the must-answer AUROC are both closed functions of two numbers, so one plane carries them: ρ, the share of pathogenic–benign pairs a predictor scores both of, against AUROC. Each predictor's sharp bound [ρAcov, ρAcov + 1 − ρ] is a band at its ρ in its regime's tint, its covered AUROC the regime marker and its must-answer AUROC the diamond (omitted where ρ exceeds 0.97 and the diamond sits under the marker); a band shorter than its marker, as for the nine predictors above ρ = 0.97, is omitted too, the marker then standing for the bound. No lower bound can lie above the dotted line y = ρ nor any upper bound below y = 1 − ρ, which covered AUROCs of one and zero reach; the lines cross at one half, so every predictor with ρ below one half, 31 of the 49, has a bound that contains chance whatever it scores, as the bracket states; 30 of them are the missense predictors named under their bands. The ticks right of ρ = 1 are the sequence-blind baselines of the Analyses, fitted out of fold on the panel's own labels and answering every variant: gene identity alone (0.880), consequence class alone (0.837) and the two together (0.974), each an out-of-fold AUROC averaged over eight fold seeds; the dotted level at 0.974 lies above the whole bound of 23 predictors. (b) Two predictors are identified exactly when the separation in their must-answer AUROCs exceeds the mean of their two bound widths, which is the dotted diagonal; 83 of the 1,176 comparisons clear it (black points). The grey dotted line is the most separation any pair can have, (ρi + ρj)/2, which meets the diagonal at one half, so the 536 pairs beyond one half (crimson points, labelled with their count), whose pair coverages sum to one or less, can never be identified; the 557 others are not identified (grey points). The undecided points are translucent, so their overlap shows where pairs crowd. These are the verdicts of the bounds themselves: with gene-clustered 95% confidence sets 74 of the 83 stay identified (Figure 4b), and CADD_raw against MPC, ringed and identified by 0.0009, is one of the nine that do not. The other two rings are ESM1b against AlphaMissense, not identified, and AlphaMissense against REVEL, never identifiable. The plane shows how far from decided every undecided pair is.
Figure 4. Which comparisons among the 49 predictors can be decided, and what moves the count. The dbNSFP 5.3.1a panel of Figure 2, 328,328 substitutions, and its 202,643 missense substitutions. A pair is identified when its two bounds are disjoint, never identifiable when its two pair coverages sum to one or less, so that the bounds overlap whatever either predictor scores, and not identified otherwise. (a) The 49 predictors lie along one axis in Figure 2's order, whole-panel reach descending, each name in ink with its pair-coverage regime glyph on the whole panel (the row above the names) and on the missense subset (the row below). Each of the 1,176 pairs is one diamond, set where the diagonals leaving its two predictors' names meet, so that a predictor's comparisons lie along the two diagonals from its name; the whole panel's triangle stands above the names and the missense subset's hangs below, and a diamond is filled where the pair is identified, black on the whole panel and blue on the missense subset, pale where it is not and empty where it is never identifiable. On the whole panel 83 pairs are identified, 557 are not and 536 are never identifiable; a dotted crimson outline encloses the 465 of those 536 whose two predictors are both among the 31 below ρ = ½, and most of the other 71 pair VEST4 or fathmm-XF_coding with a predictor below ρ = ½. 76 of the 83 join two of the 18 predictors with ρ ≥ ½, and the other seven join CADD_raw, BayesDel_addAF or BayesDel_noAF to a predictor below ρ = ½. On the missense subset 429 are identified, 747 are not and none is never identifiable: 48 of the 49 predictors reach ρ ≥ ½ there, and the 49th, GERP_92_mammals (ρ = 0.447), pairs only with predictors above ρ = 0.629. (b) Comparisons decided on each panel, whole panel dark grey and missense subset blue, each count printed after its bar, with the bound's own counts as dotted lines through every row. By the bound alone, 83 and 429; with gene-clustered 95% confidence sets of the Imbens–Manski type, per comparison and not simultaneous, 74 and 376, so 9 of the 83 whole-panel pairs identified in a do not survive them; at two review stars or more, 100 and 566; on labels newer than ClinVar's release of fileDate 2021-01-02, which postdates every ClinVar snapshot the predictors' stated training data name (Newer ClinVar labels), 77 and 396; on rare variants, with allele frequency below 0.001 or absent from the cohorts ClinVar carries, 414 on the missense subset; within gene, 110 and 544; and ordered whatever common score a no-call rule gives every declined variant, 202 and 676 (Note S61). The grey end ticks, each ending a hairline track, are the ceiling each accounting's pair coverages set, the pairs with ρi + ρj > 1 on that accounting's variants: 640 on the whole panel and all 1,176 on the missense subset for the bound and its confidence sets; 693 and 1,175 at two review stars; 616 and 1,176 on newer labels; 1,176 on rare missense variants; 573 and 1,176 within gene, from the within-gene coverages of figures/dbnsfp_49_provenance.csv; and every pair for the constant no-call scores, each of which point-identifies every AUROC. prep_fig_decide.py takes each count from the output of the deposited script that reproduces it (recompute_dbnsfp.py and recompute_identification.py) and computes the ceilings from the deposited reach indicators. (c) The pairs between four classes of predictor, as two lower-triangular 4 × 4 matrices, the whole panel's and the missense subset's: nine label-free conservation tracks, bStatistic among them, seven unsupervised predictors, 18 trained on other labels (11 on clinical assertions other than ClinVar, five on population data and two on evolutionary proxies) and 15 trained or tuned on ClinVar (Table S41's ClinVar-in-training column, parameter tuning counted as training). A cell is one pair of classes and a diagonal cell the pairs within one class; the class names between the matrices serve both. Each cell's shade is the share of its possible pairs identified, printed as a percentage. Identified pairs comparing two conservation tracks number 23 on the whole panel and 22 on the missense subset, one track 47 and 227, and none 13 and 180; of those with none, the pairs in which at least one predictor lists ClinVar among its training data number 6 and 131. A covered AUROC measured on labels a predictor was trained on can carry that overlap, so these are the pairs whose order could owe something to circularity.
Figure 5. How far each comparison survives, and which predictors trained without clinical labels stay behind another. The dbNSFP 5.3.1a panel of Figure 2 and its missense subset: 1,176 comparisons among the 49 predictors on each. Let a share λ of the pathogenic–benign pairs a predictor does not rank resolve arbitrarily and the rest like the pairs it ranks (Note S67): its AUROC on the whole panel then lies in [A − (1 − ρ)λA, A + (1 − ρ)λ(1 − A)], with A its covered AUROC and ρ its pair coverage, the covered AUROC at λ = 0 and the sharp bound [ρA, ρA + 1 − ρ] at λ = 1. Two predictors are ordered while their intervals are disjoint, and the least λ at which they meet is the comparison's breakdown point λ*, which exceeds 1 exactly when the bound identifies the comparison. Panels a to c share one logarithmic λ axis from 0.001 to 1. (a) REVEL against AlphaMissense on the whole panel, where each ranks fewer than half of its pairs (ρ = 0.299 and 0.320), hence the crimson triangles. Each funnel is one predictor's interval as λ grows, from its covered AUROC at the left, 0.971 and 0.960, to its sharp bound, capped at λ = 1: 0.290 to 0.991 and 0.307 to 0.987. The AUROC axis is logarithmic in 1 − AUROC, so that covered AUROCs 0.010 apart stay apart while the bounds reach 0.290; where the funnels meet does not depend on the scale. The dotted rule marks that point, λ* = 0.014; on the missense subset the same comparison breaks at 0.106 (c). The order shown, REVEL ahead, is that of dbNSFP 5.3.1a's columns: with each tool's own release AlphaMissense leads REVEL on the whole panel, 0.9631 against 0.9540, and REVEL's lead on the missense subset breaks at λ = 0.397 rather than 0.106 (Note S61). (b) The comparisons still ordered at each λ, all of them at λ = 0: on the whole panel (black) 866 at λ = 0.05 and 664 at 0.1, where the dotted drops fall, and at λ = 1 the comparisons the bound identifies, 83 on the whole panel and 429 on the missense subset (blue). (c) The breakdown points of the comparisons the bound leaves undecided, 1,093 on the whole panel (upward) and 747 on the missense subset (downward), in bins a fifth of a decade wide; the seven and the one below 0.001 are counted at the left end. Rules mark the medians, 0.112 and 0.303, and pins REVEL against AlphaMissense. (d) The 22 predictors whose training used no labels, evolutionary proxy labels, population-frequency weak labels or a population-genetic fit, and which take no allele frequency as input (Table S40), on the missense subset by covered AUROC: each name with its regime marker, its covered AUROC and a bar counting the other 21 whose sharp bound lies wholly above its own. Fifteen are dominated and seven, printed 0, are not; with the gene-clustered 95% confidence sets of Note S32, 13 of the 15 stay dominated, and the open bars are the two that do not, PROVEAN and CADD_raw. Values are computed from tables/dbnsfp_breakdown_points.parquet in Additional file 3 without resampling; recompute_dbnsfp.py reproduces the confidence sets.
Figure 6. Five widely used scores on 1.43 million ClinVar variants. Single-nucleotide variants at one review star or better: 170,680 pathogenic or likely pathogenic against 1,263,655 benign or likely benign. Panels order scores by reach, and each score's name carries the glyph of its pair-coverage regime on this panel, keyed at the top right with the bound grammar: navy circles for ρ of 0.999 or more (phyloP and phastCons), a steel square for ½ ≤ ρ < 0.999 (CADD, whose ρ is 0.998 although it answers 99.88% of variants) and crimson triangles for ρ below one half (REVEL and AlphaMissense). (a) Discrimination within each of ten consequence classes. Each ring is a class, and the disc inside it has the area of the share of the class the score answers, filled by the AUROC on those variants on the blue scale at top left; the AUROC is printed in every disc at least 6 mm across, and a dash marks a class where the score answers fewer than 30 variants of one label. Under each class name is its number of variants, n. The ten columns omit 7,759 variants, 448 of them pathogenic, most without a consequence annotation (6,986) or stop-lost (292); they enter panels b and c. REVEL and AlphaMissense are defined on missense substitutions: REVEL also answers 97% of start-lost variants, which replace the start methionine, and both carry values in other classes for variants that are missense on another transcript. The within-class AUROCs of the three genome-wide scores sit below their whole-panel values in b: CADD's run from 0.77 to 0.93 against 0.99 on the whole panel. (b) AUROC averages over pathogenic-by-benign pairs, so each bar is that pair space, all pairs end to end: the pairs a score ranks, a share ρ equal to its reach on pathogenic times its reach on benign variants, divide into pairs ranked right, pairs tied and pairs ranked wrong, right plus half the tied being the lower bound; the white remainder, the pairs not ranked, is exactly the width of the sharp partial-identification bound. phastCons ties 16.0% of the pairs it ranks, the other four fewer than 0.1%. CADD ranks 99.8% of pairs and its bound is 0.0019 wide; REVEL ranks 5.6% and AlphaMissense 3.6%, with bounds 0.9441 and 0.9637 wide, so neither is identified above chance once its silence is charged. Beside each bar, the sharp bound as a band between its end caps, the covered AUROC as the regime marker and the must-answer value, the bound's midpoint, as an open diamond, (1 − ρ)(Acov − 0.5) below the marker; where a score answers nearly every variant the three coincide and only the marker is drawn, while REVEL's bound spans 0.054 to 0.998 and AlphaMissense's 0.035 to 0.999, and REVEL falls from 0.958 to 0.526 and AlphaMissense from 0.963 to 0.517. (c) At left, the share of each label the score declines. Then the distribution of each score over the variants it answers, as the midrank percentile among answered variants, pathogenic above the baseline in orange and benign below in grey, each label on its own scale shared by the five scores, so heights compare between scores but not between the two labels. Tied scores share a percentile, which leaves gaps in phastCons's row, and its three bins that run past the scale each hold variants tied at one score and print their shares: 80% of pathogenic, and 36% and 18% of benign variants. Reach, covered and must-answer values, the reaches on pathogenic and on benign variants, whose product is one minus the bound width in b, and missingness-indicator AUROCs are in Table S11.
Figure 7. Calibrated clinical evidence from four scores on ClinVar. The ClinVar panel at one review star or better, pathogenic meaning pathogenic or likely pathogenic and benign meaning benign or likely benign, with the evidence intervals of Pejaver et al. [31] for REVEL, CADD and phyloP and of Bergquist et al. [46] for AlphaMissense; phastCons has no published calibration and is not drawn, and CADD v1.7's PHRED-scaled scores are read with intervals calibrated on an earlier CADD release (Methods). Each variant receives the strongest PP3 or BP4 strength its score reaches, no evidence where the score falls between the intervals (indeterminate), or none because the score is absent (declined). The calibration covers missense substitutions only, so the figure draws missense variants alone: they are 34.2% of the panel's 170,680 pathogenic variants, and the other 65.8%, nonsense 39.3%, splice donor and acceptor together 22.8% and the remaining classes 3.7%, fall outside the calibration, where a laboratory weighs other evidence. Counted over all pathogenic variants, PP3 reaches 28.1% of them for REVEL, 24.4% for AlphaMissense, 25.9% for CADD and 20.0% for phyloP. (a) The 204,191 missense variants, 58,394 pathogenic and 145,797 benign, by strength: BP4 extends left of zero and PP3 right of it, supporting nearest zero, then moderate and strong, and for BP4 very strong, which REVEL alone reaches, at 0.003 or below (736 benign variants and no pathogenic ones). BP4 is drawn in shades of navy and PP3 in shades of crimson, darker for stronger evidence, and the one key above the bars gives each strength its pair of shades, BP4 then PP3. Indeterminate (grey) and declined variants (white within a thin outline) are set apart at right, on the same millimetres per percent as the bars and with an axis of their own. Evidence opposite to the ClinVar label therefore sits on the other side of zero from the evidence that agrees with it, and its share is printed in bold in a column beside zero, joined to its bar by a thin leader: BP4 for 3.4%, 3.7%, 6.8% and 7.7% of pathogenic variants and PP3 for 3.2%, 2.7%, 8.3% and 7.0% of benign ones (REVEL, AlphaMissense, CADD and phyloP). The share receiving evidence that agrees with the ClinVar label at any strength is printed in a column beyond the bars on its side, REVEL's 82.1% PP3 and 79.7% BP4 among them, and the share indeterminate or unscored in the column at right; the classes are named on the first pair, and for every score the upper bar is pathogenic and the lower benign; the shares are rounded separately, so a bar's three may sum to 100.1%. (b) Each score on missense variants, CADD as its PHRED-scaled score, pathogenic variants above the central strip and benign below it, in bins whose edges fall on the calibrated interval boundaries, each bin coloured by the evidence every score in it receives, with a white seam where the level changes; the strip is the calibrated intervals themselves. Bar height is the square root of density within each class, as the height axis is titled, scaled to each score's tallest bin, so the tails that carry evidence opposite to the ClinVar label stay visible; areas are not proportional to shares, and declined variants, at most 7.0% (AlphaMissense, benign), are not drawn. The brackets on REVEL mark the tails that carry evidence opposite to the ClinVar label, which every score shares, and a level too narrow to show its fill is outlined in its colour, as CADD's benign pile-up at PHRED 0.15 or below, which is BP4 strong. The axes stop at CADD 45 and phyloP −10, beyond which lie 0.065% of pathogenic (CADD) and 0.025% of benign (phyloP) missense variants. The evidence rule and the intervals are those of recompute_pp3bp4.py, which prep_fig_evidence.py imports. Shares by class and score are in Table S36; the expert-panel labels that depend on PP3 or BP4, and the AUROC each score loses without them, are in Note S60 and Table S37.
Figure 8. Reach of eight splice predictors on a benchmark and on a deployment background. The six-assay benchmark of Smith and Kitzman [10], 3,912 variants, against their 500,000-variant simulated background from internal protein-coding exons and their 100-bp flanks across 14,577 genes. A predictor's glyph and marks give its pair-coverage regime on the benchmark, keyed at the top right: navy circles for ρ ≥ 0.999 (Pangolin, SpliceAI and ConSpliceML, all at ρ = 1), steel squares for ½ ≤ ρ < 0.999 and crimson triangles for ρ below one half, HAL and S-CAP. Rows follow Table 1's order, reach on the background with ties broken by reach on the benchmark. (a) Which of the eight predictors answer each variant. A signature is the exact set that returns a score; each column is one, drawn as dots (dark, answers; pale, declines) beneath paired bars for its share of the benchmark (grey) and of the background (blue). The columns are grouped by where a signature occurs and ordered within each group by how many predictors answer, then by background share (the benchmark-only pair by benchmark share), so the all-eight signature leads: seven occur in both sets; six more, those of the background's twelve largest signatures that the benchmark lacks, and a pooled column of the other 51 occur only in the background, 11.7% of it; and two occur only in the benchmark, 4.1% of it. All eight score 7.9% of the background against 17.7% of the benchmark. Beneath each column, a square per variant class (essential splice, intron near junction, proximal intron, exon near junction, deep exon), grey for the benchmark and blue for the background, whose area is the class's share of that column's variants in that set, a full square being 100%. At right, each class's share of each set. The benchmark over-represents the signatures that score the most, and most background-only signatures are the gene gaps of SQUIRLS, SpliceAI and ConSpliceML. (b) The same question at gene resolution, on the deployment background alone, over its 3,029 genes carrying at least 50 variants. Cell shade is a predictor's reach on a gene, blue where reach is low; crimson marks a gene it scores none of: five predictors have such genes, SQUIRLS 296, SpliceAI 100, ConSpliceML 78, S-CAP 7 and SPANR 5. The 2,597 genes every predictor scores at least in part come first, then the 432 that some predictor scores none of, grouped by which predictors fail them; the trace above is the mean number of predictors answering a variant of each gene, and the column at right counts the genes each predictor scores none of. (c) What organises the silence of the six predictors that decline anything on the background, measured rather than assumed: the variance in each one's answered-or-declined indicator explained by the gene against the variant class, on the 263,956 variants in those genes, with marker area growing with the number of variants a predictor declines across the whole background, one ink area per variant whatever the shape, and no marker smaller than a 3-pt circle (SPANR's and SpliceAI's take that floor). HAL is class-organised at R-squared 1.000 and SQUIRLS gene-organised at 0.975, but S-CAP is neither, at 0.017 by gene against a chance level near 0.01 for 3,029 genes; MMSplice and Pangolin decline nothing on the background and are not drawn. (d) The lower bound on AUROC is the covered AUROC times ρ, the share of disruptive–non-disruptive pairs a predictor scores both of, so every lower bound lies on or under the dotted line ρ and every upper bound on or over the dotted line 1 − ρ (drawn left of the break); the two cross at ρ = one half, and below it no covered AUROC can put the bound clear of chance. Each predictor's sharp bound on the benchmark is a band with end caps, its covered AUROC the marker and its must-answer AUROC the diamond; a bound narrower than its marker, SPANR's and the three at ρ = 1, and a diamond that would sit under its marker, those of all six at ρ ≥ ½, are not drawn. HAL and S-CAP lie below ρ = one half, with bounds from 0.135 to 0.947 and from 0.259 to 0.861 around covered AUROCs of 0.717 and 0.650. Right of the break in the ρ axis, drawn at a larger scale, the six predictors at ρ ≥ ½ are named in the order of their covered AUROCs, with Pangolin's and SpliceAI's markers set side by side; their sharp bounds are 0.803 (Pangolin), 0.799 (SpliceAI) and 0.731 (ConSpliceML), single values at ρ = 1, and 0.665 to 0.668 (SPANR), 0.632 to 0.690 (MMSplice) and 0.522 to 0.623 (SQUIRLS). Reach by class is in Table S17, and joint availability in Note S38 and Table S18.
Figure 9. The nine-species atlas against two annotation baselines. Catalogued disease- and trait-associated alleles, from Online Mendelian Inheritance in Animals (OMIA) [53] for the eight non-human species at 10:1 (cat 9.92:1) and from ClinVar [54] for human at 1:1, against trinucleotide-matched population negatives (in human, ClinVar benign or likely benign variants); Evo 2-40B zero-shot. Rows follow the species tree in all three panels. (a) The tree is drawn to time from approximate TimeTree divergence estimates (timetree.org), with a 100-My scale bar, except that the 3-My internode between the node joining the carnivores to the ungulates (79 My) and the node joining horse to the cetartiodactyls (76 My) is drawn 0.35 mm long so that the two nodes stay distinct. Each tip carries a PhyloPic silhouette, recoloured and resized: chicken by Edwin Price, dog by Margot Michaud, pig by Gabriela Palomo-Munoz and cattle by Ivan Iofrida under CC BY 4.0, the other five in the public domain (Additional file 2, assets/silhouettes/credits.json). Beneath each name are the panel's positives and negatives. For each species, three AUROCs on one axis, each on its own line: Evo 2 at the 8,192-bp window-mean readout, the change in mean log-likelihood over the window, reference minus alternate (filled amber circle), with the 95% interval of Table 2 as a capped whisker, a two-sample locus bootstrap widened to the variant-level endpoint where that is wider; the four-level snpEff impact ordering (|); and a coding flag (x), each baseline read from the variant's first consequence term and computed per species on all of the panel's variants. The 8,192-bp readout lacks 21 of them: 3 of cat's positives, 15 of horse's negatives and one negative each in dog, cattle and goat (Note S27). Human's 8,192-bp interval, 0.968 to 0.979, is barely wider than its marker, and horse's, whose positives cluster in few loci, is likely too narrow (Table 2). Neither baseline is a learned sequence model or uses labels, and neither is drawn with an interval: in a cross-validated model Evo 2 adds +0.032 (95% CI +0.016 to +0.048) to impact by the species mean (Note S15). The bottom row gives unweighted species means: Evo 2 0.943 [0.913, 0.973], its t interval on 8 degrees of freedom drawn as a heavier bar without caps so that it is not read as a locus bootstrap, coding flag 0.915 and snpEff impact 0.958. Goat's nine positives occupy two loci, so Table 2 gives it no interval (†). Figure 10a compares this readout with Evo 2's 1,001-bp single-token readout. (b) Score distributions at 8,192 bp of the 11,109 variants with that score, negatives (grey) above each species' axis line and positives (orange) below, each a Gaussian kernel density on the drawn scale with Scott's-rule bandwidth, clipped to its class's range and scaled to its own peak, with every positive also drawn as a tick (goat's nine as points, stacked where they would overlap); the dashed line marks a score of zero. The axis is the inverse hyperbolic sine of the score over 0.0003, linear near zero and logarithmic beyond, so every variant is drawn. Each species' AUROC is its circle in a. (c) The consequence of each negative and each positive, from its snpEff annotation, negatives on the upper bar of each pair as in b, in eight groups shaded from light (least severe) to dark (most severe), the two lightest outlined, and keyed by short names: intergenic (up- or downstream and intergenic); intron (intron and intragenic); UTR (UTR and non-coding exon); splice region; synonymous; missense; splice site (splice donor and acceptor); and stop/start (stop gained or lost and start lost). Non-coding groups extend left of zero and coding groups right, so each bar's length right of zero is its coding share, printed at its end: positives are 84.6% to 100% coding in every species, non-human negatives 0.4% to 2.8%, and human's ClinVar benign negatives 61.1%. The coding flag's AUROC in each species is (1 + coding share of positives − coding share of negatives)/2, so panel c accounts for the crosses in a. At right, the negatives whose consequence alters the protein (snpEff high or moderate impact): 83 across the eight OMIA species against 174 in human. Sensitivity at a 10% false-positive rate and the effect sizes are in Note S43.
Figure 10. The readout and the margin over conservation. The AUROC axes, and c's margin axis, share one scale; c and d share rows. Evo 2 is amber, GERP grey; open circles are the 1,001-bp single-token readout, filled circles the 8,192-bp window-mean readout, just above and below a row's line. Capped whiskers are 95% intervals; bars without caps, species means' t intervals on 8 degrees of freedom. (a) Per species in Figure 9's tree order, Evo 2's AUROC at both readouts as Table 2 computes it: 1,001 bp on all 11,130 variants and 8,192 bp on the 11,109 that readout scores (Note S27). The whiskers are Table 2's intervals, two-sample locus bootstraps widened to the variant-level endpoint where that is wider; human's at 8,192 bp, 0.968 to 0.979, is narrower than its marker. Goat's nine positives occupy two loci, so Table 2 gives it no interval (†). Every species rises, by +0.009 (goat) to +0.149 (human) on the 11,109 variants carrying both readouts (Table S1). The bottom row gives the unweighted species means, 0.878 [0.849, 0.907] at 1,001 bp, where species run from 0.825 (human) to 0.951 (goat), and 0.943 [0.913, 0.973] at 8,192 bp, and their difference, +0.065. (b) Top, the 11,109 variants with an 8,192-bp score, to scale and each bar from zero: coding and non-coding partition them, missense is a subset of coding, and orange segments are positives, with n and positive share beside each bar. Bottom, each stratum's AUROC pooled across species at both readouts on its own variants, all carrying both, and the readout gain: +0.092 for all variants, +0.132 coding, +0.172 non-coding and +0.013 missense.
