The 8,192-bp values carry 95% intervals from a two-sample locus bootstrap within species, positive-bearing 100-kb loci resampled whole and negatives individually, B = 2,000, seed 61: all variants 0.973 (0.966 to 0.979, narrower than its marker), coding 0.941 (0.931 to 0.951), non-coding 0.852 (0.801 to 0.899) and missense 0.812 (0.782 to 0.841), the pooled missense value marked by the dotted line; that stratum pools species and is 59% human, so it is read per species in Note S23. (c) Per species, Evo 2's AUROC minus GERP's [57,58] on the 9,529 variants that both Evo 2 readouts and GERP score. The intervals are two-sample locus bootstraps: positive-bearing 100-kb loci on the physical chromosomes are resampled whole and negatives individually, B = 2,000, seed 61, both readouts on the same resample. The column at right gives the positive-bearing loci behind each margin, from 1 in goat and 19 in chicken to 908 in human; a percentile interval over a few tens of loci, as in chicken, pig and sheep, is approximate, and horse's, whose positives concentrate in few loci, is likely too narrow (Table 2). Goat's eight co-scorable positives sit in one locus, so it carries no interval (†). The bottom rows give the unweighted species means, +0.047 [−0.009, +0.104] at 1,001 bp and +0.108 [+0.058, +0.159] at 8,192 bp, and their paired change, +0.061 [+0.027, +0.095]. Per-species margins are in Table S7, with their locus-resampled tests in Note S11. (d) Row for row with c, on the 9,528 of those variants with GERP averaged over both scoring windows: per species, GERP at the variant's own base, one score at both readouts (bar), and GERP averaged over each readout's window (open circle, the 1,001-bp window; filled, the 8,192-bp window). At the foot, the species means: GERP per base reads 0.831, and averaged over the windows it falls from 0.586 to 0.530, below GERP per base in eight of the nine species at 8,192 bp. Evo 2's species means on these variants, 0.878 and 0.939, are c's margins plus GERP per base. GERP per base is therefore the stronger comparator, and c uses it (Note S11).
Figure S2. Evo 2 reads near chance on one porcine cis-eQTL panel. Evo 2-40B zero-shot on high posterior inclusion probability (PIP) candidate causal pig cis-eQTL variants (PigGTEx, SuSiE-inf [75,76]) against within-eGene non-causal controls, matched on eGene, not linkage disequilibrium (LD). (a) The scale the panel is drawn on, in base pairs. Each variant that is not its eGene's lead credible-set variant sits at its distance from that lead against its Evo 2 score — 16,840 of the 20,000, across 1,806 eGenes — with both populations repeated as marginals. Credible sets are organised at 10⁵ bp: a candidate causal sits a median 89,784 bp from its lead and a control 345,717 bp, while the single-token readout conditions on 501 bases of left context, and only 4.7% of the 1,840 non-lead candidate causals lie within 1,001 bp of it. (b) The matched design as pairs: each candidate causal at its own score against the median of controls in its own eGene. The cloud straddles the diagonal, the candidate scores higher in 46.4% of the 2,783 pairs, and 2,217 of the 5,000 never acquired an in-gene comparator. (c) The reading against model scale: below chance at every scale, by 0.008 at 1B, 0.012 at 7B and 0.002 at 40B, while the whole-atlas macro rises; that macro includes human, whose 1B scores are anomalous (Note S15), and without human reads 0.896 at 1B, 0.924 at 7B and 0.939 at 40B. (d) Every scorer tested here and every other signal on the panel, which GERP reaches for only 884 to 1,015 of the 2,000 variants and on which the Evo 2-1B embedding probe is gene-grouped over 3,160 eGenes. The sequence and conservation scorers collapse, including Nucleotide Transformer [60] and three GERP flavours; the open marker on those rows is the same scorer on the coding panel. Distance to the transcription start site reads 0.651 and a gene-grouped probe of concatenated Evo 2-1B embeddings 0.519. Panel definitions and the circularity caveat: Supplementary Note S43.
Figure S3. Score is associated with allele frequency without curated labels, and its limits. All panels use the 1,001-bp single-token variant-delta readout. (a) Pearson standardised residuals of Evo 2 deleteriousness against minor allele frequency, on a ten-by-seven grid of equal-count bins over 28,006 cattle variants; the grid shows departure from independence, not the trend, which is that mean deleteriousness falls monotonically across the five sampling strata (Spearman ρ = −0.099 [−0.111, −0.088]). (b) The same gradient conditioned on conservation, on the 27,234 variants phyloP reaches, whose all-quintiles box re-reads a's ρ on that smaller panel; it holds at fixed conservation in four of five quintiles. (c) The foundation-model increment over conservation alone per species, positive in all nine, n-weighted mean +0.054, unweighted species mean +0.071. (d) What the wild-bat pilot can observe at all. Ten Myotis lucifugus libraries bound the allele number at 20, so a frequency here is a ratio of two small integers rather than a continuous quantity: rows are the allele number actually called, columns the minor allele count, and shade is how many of the 23,888 variants sit in each cell, on a log scale. The 28 hatched cells are impossible rather than empty; the panel annotates why, and gives the modal cell and the resulting 1/AN observability floor. Panels a, b and d use no disease labels; panel c uses the nine labelled panels. Cells in a are Pearson standardised residuals, (observed − expected)/sqrt(expected), with negative cells hatched so the sign survives greyscale; the association in a is decisively detected and the effect is small. In b each variant is scored against its own stratum's median, so 0.5 is an exact null. Full statistics and the non-replication caveat are in Supplementary Note S43.
Figure S4. Flexible calibration shows no robust transfer gain; confidence has limited utility. Evo 2-40B on 11,130 variants from nine species at the 1,001-bp single-token variant-delta readout. (a) Local error versus Platt log-odds; refusing the least-confident 15% removes 35.4% of errors but is class-asymmetric. 82% of missed positives lie left of the high-error band. The right-hand multiplier is that species' lift over random refusal, n.s. where its bootstrap interval contains 1 (2,000 resamples, seed 20260719, uncorrected); goat's 5.5× rests on six errors. (b) Leave-one-species-out isotonic transfer is not distinguished from a global sigmoid under three of four macro ECE estimators (pooled ECE is unstable; Table S22). (c) Macro selective error on the transferred arm falls from 0.058 to 0.038 at 85% coverage, rises to 0.046 at 45%, random refusal flat; the third arm, an in-species oracle, needs target-species labels and is for reference, not a floor. (d) What the transfer does, per call not per species. The two disagree on the probability scale by a mean of 0.052, but the cloud lies along the identity: only 68 of 11,130 calls, 0.61%, cross the decision boundary, all from sigmoid-positive to transfer-negative. (e) Positive-class coverage for marginal and class-conditional (Mondrian) conformal predictors; the latter approaches nominal by abstaining. The marginal predictor abstains on none; each class-conditional point prints its abstention rate. Marginal coverage reaches 22–57% in the three held-out species, 22–73% over all nine; class-conditional reaches nominal in 8 of 9 species and 2 of the 3 held out. In a, cells are log-odds bins shaded by error rate; thin bins keyed on the panel. Panel a uses the Platt leave-one-species-out posterior; panel e, an isotonic map fitted on pooled label-rich species. Asterisked species in a: goat, chicken and pig. Full denominators and estimator sensitivity: Supplementary Notes S44–S55 and Tables S22–S33.
Figure S5. Conservation's missingness is class-dependent. Every AUROC here is at the 1,001-bp single-token variant-delta readout unless stated. (a) Per species, the fraction of variants for which the GERP track returns a finite value, separately for population negatives and catalogued positives across all 11,130 variants. Cell size is constant within a plate; marks are keyed on it. Above each species plate is the gap, positive minus negative reach, with its Newcombe hybrid-score interval, excluding zero in four of the nine. Horse is the extreme case and sheep the control where both classes are equally reachable. The tenth plate is Evo 2, which punches nothing out. (b) The same silence as a set rather than a rate. The whole atlas is one area-true block: column width is the species' share, the upper band the part GERP cannot score, and fill the share of each band that is a catalogued allele. GERP returns nothing for 1,598 of the 11,130, the loss is uneven across species, and it is not random with respect to the outcome: the silent set is 7.0% catalogued alleles against 22.3% among the variants it does score. Evo 2 still separates the silent set, at AUROC 0.884 on all 1,598, at the same readout as the rest of the figure, so the must-answer penalty corrects the comparison and does not claim those variants are unreadable. (c) Supervised baselines on the same panels, as point estimates without intervals: a convolutional network given the real labels loses to zero-shot Evo 2 in all eight species in which it was run, once its own species is held out; goat, at n = 99, is too small to train and hold out. Per-species reach, the protein-language-model comparison and the composition confound: Supplementary Note S43.

## Note S60. Calibrated computational evidence and label circularity

Discrimination is not what a clinical laboratory acts on. ClinGen publishes score intervals at which a tool's output counts as PP3 (pathogenic) or BP4 (benign) computational evidence of a given strength: REVEL, CADD and phyloP in Pejaver et al. [31], AlphaMissense in Bergquist et al. [46]. A score between the innermost PP3 and BP4 bounds is indeterminate and contributes no evidence. The intervals were calibrated on missense variants, so we applied them to the 204,191 variants of the 1,434,335-variant ClinVar panel that ClinVar annotates as missense (Table S36), and on the whole panel every other variant receives no calibrated evidence from these tools.
REVEL gives PP3 evidence to 82.1% of pathogenic missense variants and BP4 evidence to 79.7% of benign ones. It gives the opposite evidence to 3.4% of pathogenic and 3.2% of benign variants, and leaves 14.1% and 14.7% indeterminate. AlphaMissense leaves more pathogenic variants indeterminate, 23.1%, and more benign variants unscored, 7.0% against 1.7% of pathogenic ones, the class-dependent silence of the canonical-transcript file of its release (Note S61). phyloP is the weakest at clinical thresholds, with 33.9% of pathogenic variants indeterminate. Outside missense no calibrated evidence exists for these tools: 65.8% of the panel's 170,680 pathogenic variants are not missense, and on the whole panel REVEL and AlphaMissense return no score for 62.7% and 66.3% of pathogenic variants. Stenton et al. [47] measured the yield of the same recommendations per proband in rare disease; these shares give it by ClinVar class.
Label circularity. A ClinVar assertion may rest on PP3 or BP4 evidence from the tools being evaluated, which would flatter them. The ClinGen Evidence Repository publishes each expert-panel classification with the ACMG/AMP criteria it applied [33], under CC0 1.0 with attribution to ClinGen; the export used is identified in Additional file 3 by its SHA-256. Of its 13,293 classifications, 7 are marked retracted, 11,034 carry a GRCh38 single-nucleotide key, and keeping one classification per variant leaves 7,479 labelled single-nucleotide variants. We converted each classification's applied criteria to the naturally scaled ACMG/AMP points of Tavtigian et al. [49]: supporting 1, moderate 2, strong 4 and very strong 8, negative for benign criteria, with the strength read from a criterion's suffix where one is given; pathogenic at 10 or more, likely pathogenic 6 to 9, likely benign −1 to −6 and benign −7 or less, with BA1 stand-alone. The points reproduce the stated class in 12,617 of the 13,281 classifications with a readable class and codes. Of the 7,479 labels, 4,324 applied PP3 or BP4, and 1,085 reach their class with those points but not without them; a label whose applied criteria miss its class even with those points does not rest on them.
All 7,226 expert-panel variants in the ClinVar panel carry the same class in both, and 4,168 of them are missense. On those, 603 of 3,024 pathogenic and 36 of 1,144 benign labels depend on PP3 or BP4, and removing them lowers each tool's AUROC by less than 0.01, with genes resampled (B = 2,000 over 129 genes; Table S37). The effect is similar across the four tools, AlphaMissense included, so it reflects which labels are easy to call rather than the reuse of one tool's scores. We do not compare against the subset of classifications that never applied PP3 or BP4: which classifications apply computational evidence is itself selected, and that subset differs in gene and consequence composition. The check covers expert-panel labels only; one-star ClinVar assertions do not record their criteria.
Whose scores were reused can be read from the expert panels' own rules. Each panel's criteria specification in ClinGen's Criteria Specification Registry names the tools, if any, it reads for PP3 and BP4, gene by gene; for 39 of the 45 panels whose classifications are in the repository, REVEL is a tool it reads for missense PP3, alone or with others (tables/clingen_vcep_pp3_tools.csv in Additional file 3, the current specification of each gene). Matched to the current specification for their gene, 3,631 of the 4,168 expert-panel missense variants in the ClinVar panel come from panels whose specification names REVEL, as do 584 of the 639 missense labels that depend on those points. Among those 3,631, removing the dependent labels lowers REVEL's AUROC by 0.006 (0.003 to 0.010) and AlphaMissense's by 0.005 (0.002 to 0.009), and the difference, +0.002 (−0.002 to +0.005) on the same gene draws, does not separate the tool whose scores the panels read from one they almost never name; as the two tools' scores are correlated, it excludes a REVEL-specific effect larger than about 0.005 rather than any effect. Two specifications name AlphaMissense beside REVEL, and a classification made under an earlier version of its specification may have followed other rules. Section 9 of recompute_pp3bp4.py reproduces these values.

## Note S61. Identification: the estimand, completions, the transcript join, and within-gene and rare-variant accounting

The target and its bound. For a predictor that scores part of a panel, the target is θ = P(s(X₊) > s(X₋)) + ½P(s(X₊) = s(X₋)) over pathogenic–benign pairs, for a completion s that gives every declined variant a score. Nothing in the data restricts the completion, so θ lies in [ρAcov, ρAcov + (1 − ρ)]: the lower end is attained by placing every declined positive below every negative and every declined negative above every positive, the upper end by the reverse. That is the right bound for what a predictor would have scored on the variants it declines. It does not describe a tool as shipped with a stated no-call rule, whose AUROC is point-identified.
Completions under a stated rule. Over completions that give every declined variant one common score, placed anywhere from below the lowest to above the highest scored value, REVEL's ClinVar AUROC runs from 0.414 to 0.693 and AlphaMissense's from 0.402 to 0.677, while CADD's runs from 0.990 to 0.991, and phyloP and phastCons, which decline almost nothing, keep a single value. Placing every declined variant above, or below, every scored one gives REVEL 0.414 and 0.637, whose average is its must-answer value; a common score inside the scored range reaches 0.693. Under such rules nine of the ten ClinVar pairs are ordered, every one except REVEL against AlphaMissense, against the three identified without a rule. On the 49-predictor panel 202 of the 1,176 comparisons are ordered under every such rule, and 676 on the missense subset, each in the direction of the must-answer order.
Confidence sets. Treating the bound endpoints as estimates, the gene-clustered Imbens–Manski-type sets of Note S32 [34,35] keep 74 of the 83 identified comparisons and 376 of the 429 on the missense subset. They are per-comparison, not simultaneous over the 1,176; made simultaneous by Holm's procedure, they keep 67 and 316 (Note S32).
The transcript join. On the ClinVar panel REVEL, CADD and AlphaMissense come from each tool's own release, joined on GRCh38 position and alleles. REVEL's release carries a value for every transcript it covers, so a variant counts as scored if any transcript carries one, and 52,617 of the 253,041 variants REVEL scores are not missense on ClinVar's own transcript. AlphaMissense is read from the canonical-transcript file of its release, which leaves 11,196 ClinVar missense variants unscored, unevenly by class. Restricted to the 204,191 variants ClinVar calls missense, REVEL reaches 0.9957 of pathogenic and 0.9759 of benign variants, with covered AUROC 0.9725, must-answer AUROC 0.9591 and bound [0.9450, 0.9733]; AlphaMissense reaches 0.9825 and 0.9302, with covered 0.9632, must-answer 0.9234 and bound [0.8804, 0.9664]; CADD, which scores essentially all of them, reads 0.9346, phyloP 0.8872 and phastCons 0.8159. On the 202,643 missense substitutions of the dbNSFP panel, all in the ClinVar panel with the same label, REVEL's own release scores 8,248 that dbNSFP leaves without a REVEL value, 1,852 pathogenic and 6,396 benign, against 2 the other way; AlphaMissense's release scores 1,982 that dbNSFP leaves unscored and leaves 8,227 unscored that dbNSFP scores; and CADD scores every one in both. Reach on the 49-predictor panel is therefore reach as dbNSFP 5.3.1a distributes it, and a tool's own release can reach more; section 1b of recompute_clinvar_reach.py reproduces these counts. Which source is read also moves the breakdown points of Note S67: on the same 202,643 missense substitutions, REVEL's lead over AlphaMissense breaks at λ = 0.397 with each tool's own release against 0.106 with dbNSFP's columns, and on the whole dbNSFP panel AlphaMissense's own release leads REVEL's in covered AUROC, 0.9631 against 0.9540, where dbNSFP's columns put REVEL ahead; section G of recompute_identification.py reproduces these values. The source moves the other comparisons among the three as well. On the missense subset REVEL's pair coverage is 0.905 with dbNSFP's column and 0.979 with its own release, AlphaMissense's 0.973 and 0.924, and CADD's 1 with both; REVEL's lead over CADD breaks at λ = 0.423 with dbNSFP's columns but is identified with the tools' own releases (λ* = 1.88), while AlphaMissense's lead over CADD is identified with dbNSFP's columns (λ* = 1.12) and breaks at λ = 0.398 with the tools' own releases. On the whole panel CADD leads REVEL in covered AUROC with the tools' own releases, 0.9677 against 0.9540, where dbNSFP's columns put REVEL ahead, 0.9706 against 0.9678. Section 1b of recompute_clinvar_reach.py reproduces these values, the pair coverages without licensed input.
Scope-matched differences with genes resampled. Genes come from ClinVar's own gene annotation together with the dbNSFP gene, and variants sharing any gene symbol form one cluster (B = 2,000, seed 61). REVEL leads CADD by +0.0091 [0.0028, 0.0146] on 253,041 variants, AlphaMissense leads CADD by +0.0316 [0.0279, 0.0353] on 193,533, and REVEL leads AlphaMissense by +0.0096 [0.0065, 0.0128] on 192,168; on the variants ClinVar calls missense the three differences are +0.0392 [0.0349, 0.0434], +0.0318 [0.0280, 0.0356] and +0.0096 [0.0066, 0.0128]. These intervals are several times wider than variant-level DeLong intervals on the same differences, and every sign survives them. Restricted to missense variants, REVEL's lead over CADD is four times larger, because its values on variants ClinVar calls non-missense dilute it.
Within gene. Forming pathogenic–benign pairs only within a gene and pooling them, so that each gene weighs by its pair count, 544 comparisons are identified on the missense subset against the pooled 429, and 110 on the whole panel against 83. On the missense subset 400 are identified both ways, 144 within gene only and 29 pooled only, and one shared pair reverses. The median must-answer penalty is 0.023 within gene on the missense subset against 0.035 pooled, and 0.317 against 0.284 on the whole panel. Within-gene pairs are few, 0.03% of the pathogenic–benign pairs on the missense subset, so genes carrying many variants of both classes dominate them. The gene-balanced drop of Note S28, computed on the missense subset, is a median 0.027 across 1,203 genes, beside that subset's median penalty of 0.035.
Rare variants. Joining the missense subset to the ExAC, ESP and 1000 Genomes allele frequencies that ClinVar carries (99.98% of variants join), 88% of pathogenic and 46% of benign missense variants have no frequency in those cohorts, so 'rare' here mostly means absent from them. Restricted to the 165,933 variants below 0.001, 414 comparisons are identified against 429, 389 of them in common and none reversed, and the median penalty is 0.031; below 0.01 the count stays at 429. M-CAP, built for rare variants, reaches 0.970 of benign rare variants against 0.796 of all benign missense variants.
Label age. ClinVar's archived release of fileDate 2021-01-02 postdates every ClinVar snapshot named in the stated training data of the 49 predictors, although four of the 15 trained or tuned on ClinVar name none (LIST-S2, MPC, MVP and gMVP; figures/dbnsfp_49_provenance.csv), so a label that release does not carry with the same class cannot have entered the ClinVar training data of any predictor that dates its snapshot. Thirteen of the 49, among them three VARITY scores that the provenance table records as sharing VARITY_R's training data, also list HGMD among their training data, and a ClinVar label newer than the release can repeat an older HGMD entry. On the dbNSFP panel 232,563 labels are newer in this sense, 108,556 pathogenic and 124,007 benign, and 144,157 of them lie on the missense subset; 220,167 were absent from that release, 12,297 carried another classification and 99 the opposite one. On the newer labels 77 comparisons are identified on the whole panel, all of them among the 83 and none reversed, and 396 on the missense subset, 388 of them among the 429, none reversed; the median must-answer penalties are 0.299 and 0.038. The six whole-panel comparisons that drop out are near ties whose bounds only just separate on all labels. Because the newer labels are 70.8% of the panel, the stronger test sets them against the disjoint older ones: on the 95,765 labels the 2021 release already carried with the same class, 94 comparisons are identified, 71 of which are also identified on the newer labels and 1 reversed there, and 535 on the missense subset, 384 of them also identified and 2 reversed, every reversal between two conservation tracks. The covered AUROC of the 15 predictors that used ClinVar labels in training or tuning rises by a median of 0.006 on the newer labels, against 0.030 for the 31 that did not use them, and 5 of the 15 trained on ClinVar score lower on the newer labels, against 3 of the 31 (Table S41); REVEL, AlphaMissense and popEVE, which used ClinVar only for testing, calibration or evaluation, are in neither group. The newer labels are also easier to separate by gene alone: the sequence-blind gene prior, which no training can reach, reads 0.041 higher on them than on the older ones, so this contrast mixes training overlap with a change in composition. Fitted on the older labels alone, the gene prior reads 0.758 on the newer ones, 13.1% of which lie in genes no older label reaches (recompute_labelfree_baseline.py). Reweighting the newer labels, class by class, to the older labels' mix of consequence group (missense, nonsense, canonical splice site, other), review status (one star, two or more) and decile of the out-of-fold gene prior removes the composition these capture: the gene prior's gain falls from 0.041 to 0.001, a balance check since its decile is a stratum, the median shift becomes −0.005 for the ClinVar-trained predictors against +0.004 for the 31 others, and 10 of the 15 ClinVar-trained predictors score lower on the reweighted newer labels, against 13 of the 31. The older-label mass in strata no newer label occupies, which the reweighting cannot match, is 0.0003. The medians carry no interval, so the reweighted contrast leaves the effect of training overlap unresolved. Against the release of fileDate 2023-01-07, 182,622 labels are newer: 81 identified, 80 of them among the 83, and DANN against phyloP100way_vertebrate reversed, two scores that answer the whole panel and differ by less than 0.01 in AUROC, so the reversal reflects the labels rather than reach; and 392 on the missense subset, 375 of them among the 429, none reversed. On the ClinVar panel the 1,136,238 newer labels leave the three whole-panel orderings and the seven on missense variants unchanged.
The missingness indicator. For both missense-only scores the indicator of whether a value was produced separates the classes better than the values do under the must-answer rule: 0.6116 for REVEL and 0.6145 for AlphaMissense on the whole ClinVar panel, against 0.5256 and 0.5168. That contrast is set by the rule rather than by the values. The indicator's midrank AUROC is 0.5 + (r₊ − r₋)/2, and a scorer that answers everything has a constant indicator whose midrank AUROC is exactly one half, as roc_auc_score returns. Set against the must-answer AUROC, even read orientation-free, the indicator wins whenever |r₊ − r₋| > r₊r₋, whatever the covered AUROC, because the one-half rule discards what the indicator knows. Whether the values add anything is tested by the lexicographic completion, which ranks by the indicator and then by value and beats the indicator by exactly ρ(Acov − 0.5); its gene-resampled interval excludes zero for every one of the 49 predictors, on the whole dbNSFP panel and on its missense subset (Table S12). For REVEL and AlphaMissense the missingness follows declared scope and the transcript join described above, so the indicator is substantially a missense indicator, and stratifying by consequence removes most of it.

## Note S62. Provenance by species and by predictor

Table S39 gives, species by species, the assembly on which the panel was built and verified, the GERP track, the gene-model release and snpEff database, and the source of the population negatives. The 740 OMIA positives fall in 520 genes counted within species, and 420 of those genes carry a single positive; human's 1,500 positives fall in 843 genes. Negatives are drawn from each species' population-variant pool rather than from the positives' genes, so the atlas supports no within-gene comparison. In three species the negatives miss chromosomes that carry positives. The sheep pool covers only the start of the Ensembl variation 109 file, whose chromosomes are sorted as text, and ends within chromosome 2, so sheep's negatives lie on 12 of its 27 chromosomes (1, 2 and 10 to 19). The chicken file of Ensembl variation 104 lacks several chromosomes, so chicken's negatives lie on 19 of its 34 and on none of 1, 3, 7, 10, 11, 15 and 28, which carry positives. Pig's negatives, from the PigGTEx genotypes, lie on its 18 autosomes, so pig has none on X. In the other six species every chromosome that carries a positive also carries negatives. In all, 32 sheep, 13 chicken and 2 pig positives lie on chromosomes without negatives, and 4 more sheep positives lie on chromosome 2 beyond its last negative, at 45.78 Mb, where the sheep pool ends. Without these 51, Evo 2's AUROC at 8,192 bp is 0.938 in sheep, 0.946 in chicken and 0.855 in pig, its species mean 0.939 (0.907 to 0.970), its margin over GERP +0.114 (+0.058 to +0.171), and the comparison is identified in Evo 2's favour in five of the eight species where it can be estimated, and at 1,001 bp in cattle and chicken. Sheep's locus-resampled margin is +0.107 (+0.028 to +0.185), so that margin excludes zero in five of the eight species, as on the whole atlas, and sheep keeps its identification in 89.8% of locus-resampled replicates, against 93.5% with all its positives (table2_cluster_aware_ci.py, Additional file 3). Without only its 32 positives on chromosomes that lack negatives, sheep's margin is +0.065 (−0.053 to +0.154), which includes zero, and it keeps its identification in 67.2% of replicates, so sheep's interval turns on the 4 positives beyond its last chromosome-2 negative. Cattle's population frequencies, used by the label-free panel, come from the FarmGTEx cattle imputed-genotype release [105]; that panel samples cattle chromosomes 1 to 22, and the release's 7,394 samples include repeated tissue samples of a few animals.
Which species carry which claims. Table S39 gives each species' positives, trait alleles, negative source and GERP track, Note S29 its positive-bearing 100-kb loci, and the paragraph above the chromosomes its negatives reach. At 8,192 bp the atlas makes three claims per species about Evo 2 against that GERP track: a margin whose locus-resampled interval excludes zero (Note S11), identification with GERP's and Evo 2's unscored variants bounded, and a verdict that holds in at least 95% of locus-resampled replicates (Note S55). Among the eight OMIA species, only cattle and dog support all three on negatives that reach every chromosome and on more than a hundred positive-bearing loci each; human supports all three too, against ClinVar's benign records rather than population variants. Chicken supports all three, with or without its positives on chromosomes that lack negatives, but 20 of its 28 positives are trait alleles. Sheep supports the first two, its verdict holding in 93.5% of replicates, and still the first two without its 36 positives that its negatives do not reach, its verdict then holding in 89.8%. Goat's nine positives occupy two loci, so none of the three can be estimated, although its point estimates favour Evo 2. Pig, horse and cat support none: they are not identified at the point estimate, and are identified in only 7.8%, 5.6% and 25.4% of replicates. The grading is the same without the 51 positives the negatives do not reach. These three claims set Evo 2 against GERP. Against the snpEff impact ordering, with positive-bearing 100-kb loci resampled whole and negatives individually (B = 2,000) and no correction across the nine species, the impact ordering minus Evo 2 at 8,192 bp is resolved in three species: pig +0.104 (+0.008 to +0.217), where annotation leads, and dog −0.035 (−0.066 to −0.007) and human −0.014 (−0.020 to −0.008), where Evo 2 leads. In chicken, sheep, horse, cat and cattle the interval includes zero, and goat has none. Without pig, whose row confounds species with negative source, the species-mean difference falls from +0.015 to +0.004 (−0.023 to +0.031) (section 20 of recompute_atlas_strata.py, Additional file 3).
Annotation of the positives. The annotation baselines read snpEff's first consequence term, the impact ordering ranking it HIGH above MODERATE above LOW above MODIFIER, while OMIA curates each variant's effect. Of the 740 non-human positives, 708 join a curated effect in OMIA's variant table and 594 of those are coding (missense, nonsense, stop-lost or start-lost). Of the 594, 27 are non-coding by snpEff's first term, 16 curated as missense and 11 as nonsense, and 16 of the 27 are in dog. Counting the 27 as coding, each with the impact rank of its curated class, raises the coding flag's mean over the eight OMIA species from 0.944 to 0.955 and the impact ordering's over the nine from 0.958 to 0.968. Negatives carry no curation and are left as snpEff calls them, so this bounds the effect of the positives' annotation errors rather than giving an error-free reading, and those errors work against the annotation baselines, not for them (analyses/scripts/omia_effect_audit.py and reports/omia_effect_audit.json, Additional file 2; OMIA's table is fetched from OMIA, not deposited).
A cat record on the wrong chromosome. NC_058380.1_27099390_G_A is a DMD allele that OMIA records for X-linked muscular dystrophy, but its accession is that of cat chromosome D4 (refseq_chromosome_map.csv, Additional file 3), where the position falls in TUT7, while on chromosome X the same position falls within DMD (Ensembl 114 gene models), and the stored window it was scored on reads DMD's coding sequence. Its window, read at the DMD site, passes the reference check of Note S41, while tools that map the identifier's accession miss the allele: SIFT's builder finds C rather than G at that position on chromosome D4 and leaves the variant unmapped (atlas_sift.parquet, Additional file 3), and the GERP track holds no value there, although the atlas's per-base GERP column holds one. The identifier is therefore probably wrong at source. The record stays in the atlas; without it cat's AUROC reads 0.889 rather than 0.890 at 8,192 bp and 0.845 rather than 0.846 at 1,001 bp. The PAX3 blue-eye positive, whose identifier carries a dog accession, lies within cat PAX3 on chromosome C1 in the same gene models.
Table S40 gives, for each of the 49 predictors, its dbNSFP rank-score column, its declared scope, the class of labels it was trained on, whether allele frequency is one of its inputs, its reach by class on the whole panel and on the missense subset, its covered and within-gene covered AUROC, and its bound and must-answer penalty. Twenty-six of the 49 were trained on clinical assertion sets, 14 of them with ClinVar labels and a fifteenth, LIST-S2, tuned on them; five learned from population data without clinical labels, seven are unsupervised, CADD and DANN learned from an evolutionary proxy, and nine are conservation tracks. Allele frequency is an input to MetaSVM, MetaLR, MetaRNN, MisFit_S, BayesDel_addAF and ClinPred, and enters indirectly for M-CAP and MutFormer. MutationTaster2021, the version dbNSFP carries, labels ClinVar-pathogenic variants deleterious by lookup [45], so on a ClinVar benchmark part of its accuracy is recall of the labels.

## Note S63. Extended Methods: calibration, conformal and selective prediction

The three trust layers this paper builds are reported here, and none of them changes how the atlas should be read. Transferring an isotonic calibration map from label-rich to label-poor species beats a single two-parameter global sigmoid in five of nine species on the primary calibration-error estimator and in three to nine across the twelve binning rules of Table S22, with a macro difference of 0.002 on the primary estimator and under 0.012 on every estimator reported, so transferring the isotonic map buys little over one fitted curve (Notes S44 to S53 and Tables S22 to S33). A class-conditional conformal layer reaches roughly 0.97 coverage on zero-label species only by abstaining on 50.5% to 65.7% of variants (Note S10, Figure S4e and Table S25). The selective layer's advantage over a trivial always-abstain policy does not survive pooling. Rebuilt at the 8,192-bp readout, two of these conclusions change (Table S30). With donors restricted to the species holding at least 50 positives, as at 1,001 bp, transfer lowers macro calibration error from 0.0285 for a pooled two-parameter sigmoid to 0.0211 (difference −0.0074 [−0.0133, −0.0040]). The sigmoid is fitted without a penalty, because at this readout's score scale the default L2 penalty flattens it, to a macro error of 0.1617. All four estimators favour transfer, which is better in six of nine species by calibration error and in eight of nine by Brier score; admitting every other species as a donor, as Table S30 does, gives 0.0209 against 0.0279. With donors holding at least 50 positives, the two learned abstention policies, a reject option and a conformal rule, cost 0.141 and 0.140 per variant, pooled, against 0.500 for abstaining on every variant; on the deposited posterior (reports/fig4_pervariant_8192.parquet, Additional file 2), whose donors are every other species, the reject option costs 0.144 per variant against 0.146 for a cost-aware threshold that never abstains. The Mondrian conformal layer abstains on no variant at that readout, and its positive-class coverage on the three zero-label species falls to 0.444 in goat, 0.714 in chicken and 0.694 in pig against a nominal 0.90.
Raw Evo 2 scores were mapped to probabilities by isotonic regression [140] fitted on label-rich species and evaluated on label-poor targets, leaving one species out, with target scores outside the donor score range clipped to that range. At 1,001 bp a donor needs at least 50 positives, admitting six species, so goat, chicken and pig are transferred into (Supplementary Table S23) but never donate. In the 8,192-bp trust layer and the packaged tool every other species donates, so each fold fits on the other eight. The conformal layer is the exception: its map and quantiles are both fitted on the six label-rich species, so it is held out in goat, chicken and pig only.
Expected calibration error is assessed on the isotonic map, and the per-variant selective-prediction results on a two-parameter Platt posterior [138] fitted on the same pool, because the transferred isotonic posterior takes only 248 distinct values across 11,130 variants, with 99.63% of variants in tie blocks, so a least-confident-15% boundary would fall inside a tie block in every species. Error totals at the 0.5 threshold throughout Additional file 1 are those of the Platt posterior plotted in Figure S4 and tabulated in Tables S27 to S29 (893 errors); Supplementary Note S54 reconciles this total with the isotonic (861) and Platt (891) columns of the deposited refit. Isotonic transfer was benchmarked against two baselines, trivial in parameter count, fitted on the same sources (a pooled two-parameter global sigmoid and a base-rate-constant predictor), and against an in-species oracle. A nearest-relative transfer is not reported: chicken and pig have no same-clade donor among the six label-rich species, and in goat it rests on nine positives. Prevalence-corrected probabilities use Elkan–Saerens prior correction [142,143], mapping donor-pool prevalence to the held-out panel's own construction ratio. Beta calibration [141] appears in the estimator sweep only.
Conformal prediction used split-conformal inference [139,144] in a class-conditional (Mondrian) construction, calibrated on one half of a stratified 50/50 split of the pooled label-rich species and applied unchanged to zero-label targets. With p the calibrated probability of the positive class, the nonconformity score is the probability assigned to the wrong class, 1 − p for a positive and p for a negative. The split-conformal quantile is the ⌈(n+1)(1−α)⌉-th smallest calibration score, with α = 0.10 (nominal coverage 0.90), computed separately within each class, so a prediction set contains the positive label when 1 − p ≤ q₁ and the negative label when p ≤ q₀; a set containing both is an abstention and one containing neither is empty. Cross-species application breaks exchangeability, the regime treated in [145], so observed coverage carries no distribution-free finite-sample guarantee. A marginal near-singleton construction was rejected on positive-class coverage; every conformal value reported here is the Mondrian arm (Supplementary Note S45).
The selective predictor ranks variants by the confidence |2p − 1| and withholds the least-confident tail, 15% within each species at the reported operating point (coverage 0.85); an error is a call on the wrong side of 0.5 under 0–1 loss. The published figures use a positional tie-break, exposed as tie_policy="rank", whose result in the isotonic arms depends on row order. The released package therefore defaults to refusing whole tie blocks, never part of one, which is deterministic under any row order and refuses fewer calls. The permutation audit behind the choice is in Supplementary Notes S34 and S54.

## Note S64. The comparison is not measured on the same variants

Two accuracy columns placed side by side presume that both were computed on the same variants. Across these nine panels they were not, and the discrepancy is not random (Figure S5): over all 11,130 variants the conservation tracks return a finite value for one class more often than the other, and almost always in the same direction. The positive class is the better-covered one in eight of nine species, sheep being the sole exception: both its classes are reachable at 73.2% and the gap is exactly zero (Supplementary Table S2). Horse is the extreme: Genomic Evolutionary Rate Profiling (GERP) scores 93.0% of its catalogued positives and 44.1% of its population negatives, a gap of +0.489. The gap runs +0.358 in pig, +0.194 in cat and +0.044 in cattle, and its Newcombe hybrid-score interval excludes zero in four of nine species. Every per-species conservation AUROC computed this way, including those reported below, therefore rests on a class-skewed subset of the panel it is quoted for. We establish the skew within our own nine panels and do not survey the published literature.
Sheep separates two claims that are easily conflated. Both its classes are reachable at 73.2%, so its class gap is exactly zero, and it still carries the third-largest must-answer penalty in the panel at −0.169. That penalty comes from total reach, not from class-dependence. The deficit is exactly −(1 − ρ)(A_cov − 0.5), where ρ = r₊r₋ is the share of positive–negative pairs scorable on both sides; for sheep that is 0.732² = 0.536, giving −0.169. Accordingly, at equal reach a better-discriminating score is charged more. The cost of conservation's silence and the class-dependence of that silence are two different claims, and only the first holds in every species. At deployment a prediction must be returned for every variant. The closed-form must-answer penalty is −0.245 AUROC in horse (95% confidence interval, CI, [−0.277, −0.212]) and −0.177 in pig ([−0.268, −0.078]), against an unweighted species mean of −0.102. That accounting is a convention and the penalty depends on it: giving each no-call a concrete value instead of charging every pair touching one at one half takes horse to −0.0100 or −0.0650 and the species mean to −0.0235 or −0.0398, depending on the value chosen (Supplementary Table S2).
The premise that every variant must be answered sits beside a method that itself declines to answer, so the two kinds of silence must be distinguished. Conservation's silence is involuntary, usually unreported and class-shaped. The selective abstention layer reported below declines by design: its silence is chosen, its answered fraction is fixed in advance, and the answered set carries a measured error rate. It is not class-symmetric either, and the accounting we apply to conservation charges it too. Charged the same way, from the per-variant calls deposited in Additional file 2, refusing each species' least-confident 15% retains 0.33 to 0.82 of positives against 0.86 to 0.90 of negatives. That is a mean class gap of 0.331 against conservation's 0.141, and ours is the larger of the two in eight of nine species. The must-answer penalty on that arm is −0.158 by the species mean, against the −0.102 we charge GERP. We read conservation's as a cost and ours as a design choice for one reason: a stated refusal rate is part of a decision procedure, while an undeclared one silently changes the panel a published accuracy refers to. It is not because our refusal falls evenly on the classes.
The mechanism behind that class-dependence is not settled on the defensible test. A consequence-matched Mantel–Haenszel analysis finds a positive class gap separable from zero in none of the five testable species, and dog's matched gap reverses, while a cruder coding and non-coding stratification finds it surviving in four of the eight in which it is defined, goat having no stratum that holds five variants of each class. Both stratified tests are run in all nine species, since every one of the 11,130 variants carries a consequence annotation, but only five species have a consequence category holding at least five variants of each class, so the matched test is defined in those five. The deposited mechanism layer runs on the 11,109 variants carrying an 8,192-bp score rather than the 11,130 of the section's opening paragraph, so its marginal gaps are close to but not the same as the ones quoted there: horse +0.479 against +0.489, cat +0.192 against +0.194. We privilege the matched test, so the mechanism stands unresolved.
The unresolved mechanism costs us less than it might. The marginal gap and its deployment cost stand either way, because reach is a property of the tracks whatever causes it. What the cause decides is generalisation: a coding artefact would not carry to a panel built differently, and if that artefact is the mechanism then the class-dependence merely restates "positives are coding" and reports nothing about conservation scoring itself. We cannot currently exclude that reading.
The missing variants do not merely cost accuracy; they leave the comparison unidentified. Bounding an unscored variant at both extremes gives an interval for conservation's true full-panel AUROC, and one for Evo 2's, and the comparison is identified only when the two intervals are disjoint. At the 8,192-bp readout Evo 2 leads in six of nine species, goat among them, and the remaining three, pig, horse and cat, are not identified in either direction. That count compares point estimates, and goat cannot be estimated, so over the eight species where the verdict can be estimated the count is five. The bound endpoints are estimated from class reaches and a covered AUROC on small per-species samples, so the verdicts carry no confidence statement. Sheep is the least secure of them: its upper bound, from the reaches of Supplementary Table S2 and GERP's AUROC in Table 2, overlaps Evo 2's own interval. Resampled by locus it keeps its verdict in 93.5% of replicates, against at least 99.8% in chicken, cattle, human and dog; goat is not estimable (Note S55). Both sides of that test are measured on the same full panel: both bounds take their reaches over all 11,130 variants, Evo 2's charging the 21 that carry no 8,192-bp score (Note S27). Comparing the full-panel bound against Evo 2 on the co-scorable subset instead would make cat a seventh, by 0.0012, on the variants the bound exists to charge for. At the 1,001-bp readout only three of nine are identified, goat again among them, and two of the eight that can be estimated. At the 8,192-bp readout, under the additional assumption that the unscored variants are no easier than the scored ones, all nine become identified in Evo 2's favour. It is an assumption. Pooled across species by random-effects meta-analysis (DeLong standard errors, between-species variance by restricted maximum likelihood, Hartung–Knapp–Sidik–Jonkman interval with Röver's modification), the 8,192-bp advantage is +0.096 (95% CI [+0.054, +0.138], I² 69%). At the 1,001-bp readout, where the per-species standard errors come from the deposited paired-bootstrap interval half-widths rather than from DeLong, it is +0.035 ([−0.020, +0.090], I² 83%), an interval containing zero. On locus-resampled standard errors for both readouts, leaving out goat, whose co-scorable positives sit in one locus, the pooled margins are +0.092 [+0.052, +0.132] (I² 63%; 95% prediction interval [+0.001, +0.183], which assumes a further species exchangeable with these eight and includes zero when any one of five of them is left out) at 8,192 bp and +0.016 [−0.032, +0.064] (I² 77%) at 1,001 bp; with goat they are +0.099 [+0.058, +0.140] and +0.037 [−0.021, +0.095]. The DeLong-based pool treats variants as independent and is kept for comparison.
The interval that matters for a reader in a tenth species is the prediction interval. On the locus-resampled pool it is [+0.001, +0.183] at 8,192 bp, whose lower limit clears zero by less than its Monte Carlo standard error, 0.001 at 2,000 resamples per species, and [−0.100, +0.132] at 1,001 bp; on the DeLong-based pool, kept for comparison, it is [−0.007, +0.199] and [−0.116, +0.186]. Nine species establish an average advantage at the larger readout; they do not establish that a tenth species will show one. Conservation also gains with panel size while Evo 2 does not: +0.085 AUROC per decade of panel size against −0.033, a contrast of +0.119 [+0.019, +0.171], p = 0.012. That contrast is descriptive, because neither slope is individually significant at nine species and no crossover is claimed.
The same accounting applied to 49 published human predictors is reported above (reach audit in Supplementary Table S12).
Two head-to-head comparisons the must-answer penalty does not make can be made directly, by restricting each pair to the variants both tools score. On the 253,041 variants CADD and REVEL both return, REVEL leads by 0.0091 [0.0080, 0.0102]; on the 193,533 CADD shares with AlphaMissense, AlphaMissense leads by 0.0316 [0.0306, 0.0327], both intervals variant-level DeLong ones that treat variants as independent (with genes resampled they are [0.0028, 0.0146] and [0.0279, 0.0353], Note S61). Scope-matched, the narrow tools win. The penalty and the scope-matched lead answer different questions, and a reader is entitled to both: the first prices a published comparison in which one tool answered far less often, the second asks which tool is better where both answer.
A laboratory needing a genome-wide answer substitutes rather than abstains, so the penalty prices the published comparison and not the deployment. Substituting CADD wherever REVEL is undefined reads 0.9766 under a tool-wins rank rule and 0.9890 under a mean-rank rule, against CADD alone at 0.9914 on the same variants. That is a property of the fusion rather than of REVEL: REVEL's defined set is a strict subset of CADD's, so the substitution runs on the same variants, and percentiles taken inside each tool's own pool let a benign missense outrank a pathogenic nonsense.

## Note S65. At the readouts tested, competence stops at a variant class, not at a genomic region

With every decline charged and the comparison set fixed, competence itself remains to be measured, and it is not uniform (Figure S2; and, class by class on the splicing panel, Table S34 and Supplementary Table S16).
**Table S34. Evo 2-1B, summed over a 1,001-bp window, against purpose-built splice predictors, by variant class**

| Variant class | n | disruptive | Evo 2-1B | 95% CI | SpliceAI | Pangolin | Evo 2 − SpliceAI | p (FWER) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Essential Splice | 246 | 238 | 0.853 | [0.788, 0.911] | 0.968 | 0.928 | −0.116 | 0.01006 |
| Exon Near Junction | 434 | 148 | 0.554 | [0.496, 0.612] | 0.794 | 0.800 | −0.239 | < 0.00002 |
| Intron Near Junction | 643 | 188 | 0.721 | [0.674, 0.768] | 0.904 | 0.917 | −0.182 | < 0.00002 |
| Proximal Intron | 1,170 | 114 | 0.535 | [0.474, 0.596] | 0.656 | 0.656 | −0.121 | 0.00618 |
| Deep Exon | 1,419 | 372 | 0.471 | [0.438, 0.504] | 0.652 | 0.665 | −0.181 | < 0.00002 |

Table S34 notes. 3,912 variants with splice-disruptive calls, measured in cells except for 82 essential-splice calls admitted by rule (Note S35). The Evo 2 column is Evo 2-1B with its log-likelihood summed over a 1,001-bp window, the only readout at which all six assays were scored; on the BRCA1 assay Evo 2-40B at the 8,192-bp readout reads 0.974 (Note S65). SpliceAI and Pangolin use roughly 10,000 nt of flanking sequence, so the columns are not matched on input context (Methods). Intervals on Evo 2 are within-class stratified percentile bootstraps at B = 200,000 (positives and negatives resampled separately at their observed class sizes, so no replicate can lose a class; Essential Splice carries eight negatives). The p-value is family-wise across the five classes by Westfall–Young max-T permutation over 50,000 draws, so no class is read on its own; the smallest p the test can resolve is 1/50,001 = 0.00002, and the three cells shown as "< 0.00002" sit on that floor and are bounds rather than estimates. Rows are ordered by class size, smallest first; Fig. 8a draws the same five classes in a different, positional order. Class negatives are n minus the disruptive count: 8, 286, 455, 1,056 and 1,047 in the row order above. Differences are computed on the unrounded AUROCs and can disagree by 0.001 with the subtraction of the two rounded columns beside them. Pooled over all 3,912 variants Evo 2 reads 0.621 against SpliceAI 0.799 and Pangolin 0.803, a paired difference of −0.1771 (95% CI [−0.2008, −0.1534], DeLong on the shared variants); that pooled comparison is not covered by the family-wise correction.
On the one assay also scored at the 8,192-bp window readout, BRCA1, Evo 2-40B reads 0.974 against SpliceAI's 0.986 and Pangolin's 0.987 on the same 1,386 variants, though there the splice label nearly coincides with the editing assay's loss-of-function call: 222 of its 223 splice-disruptive variants are loss-of-function in the editing assay, and none of its 1,163 others, so that result is not independent of the BRCA1 positive control. Every other statement in this Note is at Evo 2-1B with its log-likelihood summed over a 1,001-bp window, the only readout at which all six assays were scored. Pooled across all 3,912 variants, Evo 2 reads 0.621 against SpliceAI 0.799 and Pangolin 0.803. The paired difference against SpliceAI is −0.1771 (DeLong 95% CI [−0.2008, −0.1534], p = 1.65 × 10⁻⁴⁸, on the shared variants). That interval treats 3,912 variants sitting in six genes as independent, so by the rule we apply to the dbNSFP panel we do not read it as evidence that the difference is real; the cluster unit here is the assay, and an interval on the six per-assay deficits is 7.0 times wider. The cluster-level statement is that Evo 2 trails SpliceAI in six of six assays (exact two-sided sign test, p = 0.031), with per-assay deficits running from −0.073 on RON exon 11 to −0.540 on WT1 exon 9 (Supplementary Note S35 and Table S16).
Essential Splice carries 238 disruptive calls in 246 variants, so it rests on eight negatives, its bootstrap distribution is skewed, and its percentile interval is reported alongside a bias-corrected and accelerated one (Supplementary Note S35). At that class size the readout matters more than anywhere else in the panel: the same variants read 0.617 at 1B, 0.174 at 7B and 0.328 at 40B under the single-token readout of the capacity ladder, against 0.853 here. A permutation null at eight negatives is uninformative, so we read no ordering from these rungs. The two constructions' endpoints differ by at most 0.006, but with eight negatives neither is informative, so the class is reported descriptively.
At 7B on the same variants, with the same window sum, Evo 2 reads 0.627, a change of +0.0057 over the sevenfold parameter increase, and the paired difference against SpliceAI moves from −0.1771 to −0.1715 (DeLong 95% CI [−0.1949, −0.1481], unclustered on the same six assays). It trails SpliceAI in six of six assays at both scales.
The simplest explanation for a model failing on regulatory variants is that regulatory sequence is harder. On this panel that explanation fails. In ClinVar, 3′ untranslated region (UTR) variants read 0.702 [0.614, 0.785] and 5′ UTR variants 0.650 [0.594, 0.704], both regulatory by location, and both resolved against their own minimum detectable difference; that panel is scored at Evo 2-1B. Evo 2-40B reads 0.488 on fine-mapped pig cis-eQTLs and 0.516 on human complex-trait variants, the latter orientation-free on a signed log-likelihood ratio that reads 0.484 as given; both conventions place it within 0.02 of chance (Additional file 2: analyses/results/traitgym_complex_eval.json, written by analyses/scripts/traitgym_complex_eval.py from the scores TraitGym distributes). Conservation, as phastCons-43p, is not at chance on that same complex-trait panel, reading 0.617, so this is a statement about what a zero-shot likelihood readout recovers; it does not show the panel to be unlearnable. The two arms are different checkpoints, so the contrast is between panels and not within one model; the capacity rung below bounds that. Location does not separate these cases. What separates them is that ClinVar's UTR entries are rare alleles of large effect and the eQTL and complex-trait entries are common variants that modulate expression. That is not the only axis on which the panels differ: the eQTL panel is not matched on GC content, repeat context or mappability, and each of those could move an AUROC by more than the deviation we report; with controls matched on allele frequency, in which the two classes do not differ, Evo 2 reads 0.487 (Supplementary Notes S9 and S17).
The eQTL labels are not empty either, and a baseline using no sequence at all shows it: distance to the transcription start site reads 0.651 on the 19,341 of 20,000 variants for which it is defined (Supplementary Note S4). That baseline captures position alone, so the 0.651 demonstrates that the panel carries recoverable structure; it is not evidence of recoverable sequence signal. The baseline falls to 0.5165 [0.5056, 0.5273] once the panel is matched within chromosome under a 0.05-dex distance caliper. The matching did not finish the job: that interval excludes 0.5, and the weaker within-eGene matching leaves the baseline at 0.6282 [0.6144, 0.6429], barely below where it started. Residual position therefore survives both designs, and it does not move the reading away from chance: Evo 2 sits at 0.4852 [0.4747, 0.4957] on the matched panel as stored and 0.5148 [0.5043, 0.5253] negated, both within 0.015 of chance, and the caliper baseline is 0.0165 above chance, a departure of the same size (Notes S9 and S21; Table S20).
Capacity does not relocate that boundary either. Scoring the same ClinVar panel at 7B raises the UTR readings to 0.736 on 3′ UTR and 0.724 on 5′ UTR variants, and lifts overall discrimination from 0.770 to 0.780. The net lift is not uniform: five of the eight consequence rungs improve and three fall, nonsense furthest at −0.048, which is larger than the net gain. However, each class stays on the same side of the boundary. A sevenfold parameter increase does not close the gap to the expression panels at 0.488 and 0.516.
How large that step looks is itself a property of the readout. The figures above are window-summed, and on that readout 1B to 7B lifts ClinVar discrimination by +0.010. Rescored at the single-token readout, holding the panel, the window and the scorer fixed, the same step is +0.033 [+0.020, +0.047], three and a half times larger; on the splicing panel it is −0.005 [−0.021, +0.012]. That interval excludes any gain above +0.012, so the splicing result is a null with the power to be one. A sevenfold parameter increase is worth a different amount on each panel, and a different amount according to how the window is collapsed (Note S39). The same panel resolves the consequence gradient that an 18-variant regulatory rung could not (Supplementary Note S16). Each of eight consequence classes carries 250 negatives and between 54 and 250 positives, and every one is resolved against its own minimum detectable difference and survives family-wise control across the eight (Westfall–Young max-T, 50,000 permutations, worst p = 0.00042). Discrimination runs from 0.850 on missense to 0.650 on 5′ UTR variants.
The gradient runs unevenly; every rung is in Supplementary Table S19. Synonymous variants read 0.790, above nonsense at 0.746. ClinVar's pathogenic synonymous entries are enriched for alleles that disrupt splicing, so the class is not the benign category its name suggests, and a gradient stated as a decay from nonsense to regulatory does not survive this panel. Splicing is the one regulatory readout with strong purpose-built competitors evaluated on identical variants, and its labels are measured in cells, apart from 82 essential-splice calls admitted by rule. The per-class breakdown is in Table S34. The paired difference against Pangolin is −0.182 [−0.206, −0.157] by DeLong on the shared variants. That interval likewise treats the six assays' variants as independent, so we do not read it as evidence the difference is real. The cluster-level statement is that Evo 2 trails Pangolin in six of six assays as well, with an n-weighted mean per-assay deficit of −0.259 (Supplementary Table S16). Evo 2 falls below chance in two of the six assays, reading 0.372 on WT1 exon 9 and 0.462 on FAS exon 6. At this checkpoint and readout, large-effect canonical variants are read and subtler ones are not, and the ordering holds on labels that are experimental for all but a curated minority (Supplementary Note S35); whether it holds at 40B and 8,192 bp is open, since on the BRCA1 assay that readout nearly matches the specialists; at 40B's single-token readout it does not hold, essential-splice variants reading 0.328 there. Context length was varied on the eQTL panel rather than this one, and the claim that context does not rescue the expression panels holds up to 8,192 bp.
A supervised sequence-to-function model has not been run on the expression panels; SpliceAI and Pangolin are exactly such models, and on splicing they beat Evo 2-1B, summed over a 1,001-bp window, by 0.18 in Table S34, which places the boundary without removing it. On the BRCA1 arm, the one assay also scored at the 8,192-bp window readout, Evo 2-40B reads 0.974 against SpliceAI's 0.986 and Pangolin's 0.987 on the same 1,386 variants, where its single-token readout reads 0.552 and Evo 2-1B 0.808, so on that assay the readout rather than the model family carries most of the deficit.
The same boundary is reachable with no curated label at all, in a different species and a different data type (Figure S3).
On 28,006 cattle variants stratified across five minor-allele-frequency bins, mean Evo 2 deleteriousness falls monotonically with frequency: 0.772, 0.511, 0.319, 0.201 and 0.178 from the rarest bin to the commonest, a 4.33-fold gradient across a panel ascertained on frequency and never on disease. Conditioned on phyloP quintiles over the 27,234 of the 28,006 cattle variants (97.2%) that phyloP reaches, the frequency–deleteriousness gradient survives at fixed conservation in four of five quintiles and its interval crosses zero in the least-conserved one, which is the boundary the expression panels mark (Supplementary Note S18). The gradient does order with conservation, ρ running from −0.018 in the least-conserved quintile to −0.118 in the most, but adjacent quintiles are not separable, so we claim no monotone trend across them. The least-conserved quintile is mostly neutral sequence and holds little regulatory sequence, so its null is not by itself evidence that the model reads regulatory variants near chance. Both data types mark a boundary of the same shape, and the second rests only on the site-frequency spectrum in a species whose population panels were assembled for breeding and never for disease.
Combining conservation with Evo 2 in one logistic model beats conservation alone in all nine species, by an n-weighted mean of +0.054 AUROC (95% CI [+0.039, +0.078], species-clustered); the unweighted species mean this paper takes as primary is +0.071, which falls to +0.046 without goat and chicken, the two largest increments. Pooling the nine panels instead gives +0.020 [+0.013, +0.028] on 9,532 variants, which is a different estimand and not the average of the nine. In this Note we lead with the weighted figure because the unweighted one is carried by goat and chicken, and we quote the pooled value where the comparison is variant-level; the unweighted species mean remains primary elsewhere. The increment is small, and it shows that the two scores are not measuring the same thing; the combination is not offered as a tool.
The specialist baselines meet the same problem. A protein language model runs on only three of nine panels and only human is adequately powered; ESM-2 [109] scores 912 human variants and conservation is defined for 881 of them, so ESM-2, conservation and Evo 2 cannot be quoted as one comparison. On the 912 it can score, ESM-2 reads 0.887 against Evo 2's 0.838 on the same variants, a margin of +0.049 [+0.019, +0.079], so where a protein language model applies at all it is the stronger of the two, on 30.4% of the human panel and on no other species. On the 881 both can score, conservation reads 0.860 against Evo 2's 0.837, a margin of +0.023 [−0.011, +0.058] that does not exclude zero, and charging conservation for the 31 variants it cannot call brings it to 0.835.
Supervised baselines fare worse, in two regimes that are not the same test: a 1D convolutional network loses to zero-shot Evo 2 in all eight sufficiently powered species once its own species is held out (Figure S5c). Within species, under cross-validation, a k-mer classifier matches or beats that network in four of the eight while still losing to Evo 2 in all eight. We read them as evidence that neither extracts much beyond composition. Those two comparisons are point estimates and carry no intervals. They are counts of species, not tested differences. A reader may instead read them as evidence that the baselines are too weak to bound anything.

## Note S66. Calibration does not transfer across species, and confidence buys a precision aid, not a screen

The atlas says where the model discriminates. It does not say whether a user should act on the number in front of them, and that is the question a label-poor species actually poses. The obvious route is calibration: fit the map from score to probability where the labels are, and carry it to where they are not [31]. Evo 2's raw variant-delta scores are badly miscalibrated to begin with, at an expected calibration error of 0.318 by the species mean at the 1,001-bp readout, and leave-one-species-out isotonic transfer [140] appears to repair them, to 0.053 (Figure S4b and Table S23). Species by species, the transferred Platt map over-predicts in the middle of the range on the 10:1 panels and under-predicts on human, the one panel built 1:1, where variants given a probability near 0.01 are about 30% positive (Figure S1). Goat, chicken and pig are targets only here and never enter a donor pool. Transfer into them is measured — Table S23 is that test — so what goes untested is the reciprocal direction, a map donated by a label-poor species, and any claim that would need labels those species do not have. That is a limit of the design and not a finding.
That gain is measured against an uncalibrated starting point, and it is the wrong comparison. Transferring a Platt map [138] across species is, by construction, the same operation as fitting one global two-parameter sigmoid: the map carries no species structure, so it is fitted once on the pooled donors and applied unchanged. The two agree numerically in every species under every binning scheme, so there is no difference to interpret. The substantive question is whether a flexible isotonic calibrator beats two global parameters, and it is not resolved: under a paired stratified bootstrap the difference crosses zero in three of the four binning estimators formally tested, and any advantage is bounded above by about 0.014 ECE, a bound against which we set no equivalence margin, the δ this paper fixes being on the AUROC scale (Supplementary Note S48). The null is not a sample-size artefact. Across calibration sets spanning 25 to 10,327 variants the transferred error is flat within noise (Supplementary Note S53). A calibrated probability therefore carries no species-specific warrant in a target with no labels of its own, and the beta family [141] does not rescue it (Note S53).
The confidence ordering is a different matter, and a selective predictor needs only the ordering [13]. Refusing each species' own least-confident 15% of calls withholds 1,673 of 11,130 variants, a realised 15.03% because the threshold is applied within species, and removes 316 of 893 errors (Supplementary Tables S27, S28 and S32). That is 35.4% of the errors for 15% of the calls, a 2.35-fold enrichment over random refusal, with a macro mean lift of 3.42 (Figure S4a). The refusal count is not a pure arithmetic one: the 15% boundary falls inside blocks of tied confidence, so it depends on how the boundary is resolved. The strict corridor used here gives 1,673, taking the least-confident ceiling of 15% of each species by rank gives 1,674, and rounding to nearest gives 1,669; the single variant separating the first two is a correct call, so the error counts and lifts are identical under either rule (Supplementary Table S27). A global threshold would capture more, 38.4%, but would concentrate refusal in the worst-calibrated species and is not a rule a user working in one species can apply (Table S26).
Integrated over the whole curve the same ordering is much less impressive, and the honest summary is the integral rather than the operating point. The area under the risk-coverage curve is 0.0784 against 0.0802 for a random ordering, a 2.3% relative reduction, which is 2.4% of the gain a perfect confidence ordering would deliver, and in two of the nine species the ordering is worse than random. The macro curve flattens at about 85% coverage, and refusing past that knee buys nothing and eventually costs: on the pooled curve for the default calibrator, selective error crosses its answer-everything baseline of 0.080 again at 35% coverage, by which point retained sensitivity has fallen from 0.674 to 0.298 (Supplementary Note S51). We therefore confine the recommended operating range to 85–100% coverage and advise against deployment below 50% (Note S51).
Two controls bear on that operating point, and one of them is unflattering. The knee was located on the same pooled panel on which its benefit is then reported, which makes it an in-sample operating point. Selecting it on the macro curve over eight species and re-measuring on the ninth returns 85% coverage for all nine choices of held-out species. On the held-out species, that selection delivers a mean risk reduction of 0.022, against 0.026 if each species picks its own best coverage on its own data, and it leaves no held-out species worse off. The benefit is nonetheless unevenly earned. Human contributes 61% of the errors while carrying one-ninth of the macro weight, and within human the error-detection ordering is at chance. The aggregate lift is real: a within-species permutation preserving every panel's size and error count places the observed lift outside every one of 10,000 null draws (p = 1/10,001 ≈ 0.0001, the floor this permutation count can resolve; Supplementary Table S31). Human's showing tracks its 1:1 panel composition rather than its labels: down-sampling to a matched 10:1 composition raises its refusal lift from 1.71 to 3.19 and its error-detection AUROC from 0.474 to 0.708. That is a composition control, not a prevalence-corrected re-estimate [142,143] (Table S27; reports/human_prevalence_check.json, Additional file 2).
Neither of the two stronger guarantees we sought holds. A class-conditional conformal layer [139,144], calibrated on the six label-rich species and held out in goat, chicken and pig, reaches its nominal 90% coverage of the positive class in two of those three, against 22–57% for the marginal construction that answers everything, but buys it by abstaining on 50.5–65.7% of calls in those species (Figure S4e). The exception is pig, at 0.889, which is 32 of 36 positives; its exact 95% interval runs from 0.739 to 0.969 and contains the nominal level, so that shortfall is not established. The exchangeability condition fails by construction across species [145], and a transported risk-control certificate does not hold at a strict error target, though it is not universally void: at a permissive target it holds in four of nine species (Supplementary Notes S45, S47). Under a cost matrix pricing a missed positive ten times a false alarm [143], a trivial always-abstain policy beats every learned rule when costs are pooled, 0.500 against 0.540 for the best of them, though not under the unweighted species mean this paper takes as primary (Supplementary Note S49).
The asymmetry that decides how the layer should be described is not the one we would have chosen. Abstention suppresses false alarms far better than it catches missed positives, and at the default threshold it still misses about two of every five catalogued positives in the eight non-human species (Supplementary Table S29). It is a precision aid and not a safety net. Whether it can flag regulatory variants without labels is examined and not claimed (Supplementary Note S50). The accounting is released as glmtrust, which wraps a scorer so that it can abstain and audits a comparison for coverage bias. Its benchmark reproduces the 8,192-bp layer, the arm the package ships, and not the 1,001-bp figures quoted above (Note S52 and Table S30).
A benchmark of 97 missense predictors compares each pair only on the variants both scored, and reports that its ranking survives the gaps: filling every missing prediction with each predictor's most benign score moves one method's win rate down by six points and four others up by about the same amount, and otherwise leaves the order close to intact [11]. Stability of an order and identification of a difference are not the same property. Every pair there is scored on a set the other pairs do not share, so a win rate can be reproducible while the quantity it summarises is not common to the comparisons it is averaged over. That fill-in is a convention and not a bound: assuming what a method declined to score was benign flatters a narrow predictor when its silence falls on negatives and penalises it when the silence falls on positives, moving the AUROC by exactly (r₊ − r₋)/2 relative to the half-credit figure. Charging the silence at chance is a different convention, and it is not our invention: it follows CAFA's full evaluation mode [8,9] applied to a ranking metric, though not CAFA's own rule, which zero-fills, and Smith and Kitzman [10] reached the same problem independently for splice predictors by reporting each tool's genome-wide call rate. The bounds are what the unscored pairs give at their two extremes, [ρ·A_cov, ρ·A_cov + (1 − ρ)]; the half-credit figure is their midpoint, and it is the width of that interval, 1 − ρ, that this accounting reports.
The concurrent preprint our atlas replicates [26] does not charge the comparator for its silence, and that is what changes the reading. Separately, evaluations of pre-trained DNA language models on regulatory tasks [17] and on causal regulatory variants [61] report that learned representations give little advantage over simple encodings on exactly the class where we find Evo 2 near chance (Figure S2), at 0.488 on fine-mapped pig cis-eQTLs, though rare ClinVar 3′ UTR variants read 0.702. Those studies fine-tune and we do not, so the agreement is between two different questions that happen to return the same answer.
What survives the repair is a narrower claim. Rare large-effect regulatory variants are read; common expression-modulating ones sit at chance, at every capacity we tested, and location predicts neither outcome. How far that scope extends across checkpoints and architectures is set out in Supplementary Note S22. Splicing is the hardest test of that boundary we could assemble, because it is the one regulatory phenotype with purpose-built supervised competitors evaluated on identical variants and with labels that are cell measurements apart from 82 essential-splice calls admitted by rule, though for the 296 MLH1 variants, 7.6% of the panel, Smith and Kitzman [10] collected published RT-PCR and minigene results rather than assaying de novo. Evo 2 loses it: SpliceAI [3] and Pangolin [80] lead by 0.18 AUROC, Evo 2 trails in six of six assays and falls below chance in two. The test is not matched on input context, and that is the first thing to say about the margin — SpliceAI and Pangolin take roughly 10,000 nt of flanking sequence and Evo 2 [19] was given 1,001 bp. Inside the panel, at 1B over the summed window, the same boundary reappears: essential-splice variants are read and deep-exonic ones are not, an ordering that depends on readout and checkpoint (Note S65), and deep-exonic and proximal-intron variants, the bulk of the panel, both turn on exon definition, which acts over a range longer than the window we gave the model (Note S35).
Capacity does not move the blind side, and neither does a longer window at this readout. Across the capacity ladder discrimination rises modestly where the model is already competent, by +0.033 [+0.020, +0.047] from 1B to 7B on ClinVar, and not at all where it is not, −0.005 [−0.021, +0.012] on splicing. This ladder sits on the 8,192-bp-trained line at 1B and 7B while its 40B rung is the context-extended checkpoint [19], so the 1B-to-7B step is the capacity contrast and any comparison reaching 40B spans the context-extension training as well; we read that rung as descriptive and rest no capacity claim on it. The nine-species ladder above is the other way round, its 7B rung being context-extended, so there the 7B-to-40B step holds the curriculum fixed but still moves from 2.4 to 9.3 trillion training tokens (Note S4), and no step varies capacity alone (Note S39). On the 20,000-variant eQTL panel a 2× window at a fixed readout moves AUROC by 0.002 against 0.011 for changing the readout at a fixed window. The models that predict expression variants are supervised sequence-to-function models reading far longer inputs, Enformer [136] at roughly 200 kb and Borzoi [137] at about half a megabase, so this panel cannot say whether supervision or input length separates them from Evo 2. An alignment-informed model at a comparable window [20] is the fork that would separate the two (Note S22).
A user in a label-poor species also needs to know what a score inside that boundary is worth. Calibration is the obvious answer, and on this panel it fails. Mapping score to probability where the labels are and carrying the map to where they are not [31] is not shown to beat fitting two global parameters: the difference crosses zero under three of four binning estimators and is bounded above by about 0.014 ECE, so a positive-class probability of 0.83 carries no species-specific warrant in a target with no labels of its own. The raw-score-derived confidence ranking retains limited selective utility, and a selective predictor needs only the ordering [13]. Withholding each species' least-confident 15% of calls removes 316 of 893 errors, a 2.35-fold enrichment over random refusal (Figure S4). That is an operating point and not a summary: integrated over the whole curve the ordering is worth 2.3% against a random one and is worse than random in two of nine species. Two stronger guarantees fail outright, because exchangeability breaks across species [145] and, under pooled costs pricing a missed positive ten times a false alarm [143], a trivial always-abstain policy beats every learned rule. Abstention suppresses false alarms far better than it recovers missed positives, so the layer is a precision aid and not a screen (Note S53).
Four limits are cross-cutting. The rest sit with the claims they bound.
