First, our positives are ClinVar [54] pathogenic variants and catalogued OMIA [53] disease- or trait-associated alleles and our negatives are population variants [23] matched on trinucleotide context (in human, ClinVar benign or likely-benign records), so the atlas measures the separation of catalogued positives from population negatives and not pathogenicity within a matched sequence class. Second, these reference assemblies are very likely inside a pretraining corpus of roughly 9.3 trillion nucleotides [19]. No variant-effect labels are, which is why we describe the scoring as zero-shot, but sequence familiarity may differ by species. At 1B the reference window's likelihood alone is at chance on both human panels; at 7B it is anti-predictive on ClinVar and reads 0.5736 on splicing, above the 0.5713 of the nine-species panel, so that confound is real but modest in the marginal statistic. Third, label quality was audited against assay-grade evidence [30,50] on the human panel only. The eight OMIA [53] catalogues are not adjudicated to a clinical standard, so per-species figures outside human should be read as bounded by their label quality. Fourth, the nine panels are not annotated to a common standard and their class composition differs by an order of magnitude: 61.1% of human negatives are coding against 0.4% to 2.8% in the eight non-human species, so a per-species AUROC is partly a statement about what that species' panel contains, and the macro mean averages over nine discrimination problems that are not the same problem.
Each main claim has an observation that would overturn it, and the instruments already exist. The accounting claim fails if a gene-balanced re-evaluation on an independently assembled predictor panel, of the kind released by Livesey and colleagues [11,12], shows the pooled-to-within-gene drop exceeding the must-answer penalty for every predictor on it. It already exceeds it for the eight near-complete-reach scores on our own panel, which is what the reach stratification predicts and not a falsification. The boundary claim fails on a panel of common, expression-modulating variants in coding sequence that the model reads well, which cannot be built from Additional file 2 as deposited because the expression table carries no consequence annotation. The benchmark claim fails if a benchmark's reach matches the deployment background [10] after composition standardisation for all eight tools. Pangolin [80] already matches at both ends, at 1.000, so the criterion turns on the other seven, whose gaps run from 0.014 to 0.175. None of these is an exotic experiment, and we would rather name them than have them found.

## Note S67. How far each comparison survives: breakdown points, the frontier and a dominance map

The covered AUROC and the sharp bound are two ends of one family of assumptions. The covered AUROC treats the pathogenic–benign pairs a predictor leaves unscored as if they resolved like the pairs it scores, and the bound assumes nothing about them. Between the two, let a share λ of the unscored pairs resolve arbitrarily and the rest like the scored ones, a contamination model after Horowitz and Manski [38]. The AUROC the predictor would reach on the whole panel then lies in [A − (1 − ρ)λA, A + (1 − ρ)λ(1 − A)], with A its covered AUROC and ρ its pair coverage: a point at λ = 0 and the sharp bound [ρA, ρA + 1 − ρ] at λ = 1. Two predictors are ordered at λ when their intervals are disjoint, and the least λ at which they are not is the breakdown point of the comparison [39], λ* = (Ahi − Alo)/((1 − ρhi)Ahi + (1 − ρlo)(1 − Alo)), with hi the predictor of higher covered AUROC. A comparison is identified exactly when λ* > 1. The model sets one λ for both predictors of a comparison, and it acts on pathogenic–benign pairs, not on variants: the sharp bound at λ = 1 is attained by placing every unscored variant at an extreme, but at an intermediate λ no scoring of the unscored variants need attain the interval, so λ* is a pair-level measure of how far the covered AUROC's assumption must fail. λ* needs four numbers a benchmark already has, the two covered AUROCs and the two pair coverages. Additional file 3 deposits it, with those four numbers, for all 1,176 comparisons on both panels (tables/dbnsfp_breakdown_points.parquet); section G of recompute_identification.py reads the values below from that table, and glmtrust reach reports λ* and the frontier for any panel (Additional file 2).
On the whole panel the comparisons still ordered at λ = 0.01, 0.02, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.75 and 1 number 1,107, 1,058, 866, 664, 450, 289, 179, 101 and 83, and on the missense subset 1,163, 1,146, 1,106, 1,051, 894, 808, 631, 515 and 429 (Figure 5b). Those that are not identified break at a median λ of 0.112 (0.303 on the missense subset), so half of them keep their order only while nearly nine in ten declined pairs resolve as the scored ones do, seven in ten on the missense subset. Close covered AUROCs break almost at once: REVEL against CADD_raw at 0.004 and ESM1b against AlphaMissense at 0.005 on the whole panel. REVEL's lead over AlphaMissense breaks at λ = 0.014 on the whole panel and 0.106 on missense variants, where both predictors, as dbNSFP distributes them, answer most variants.
These breakdown points are point estimates. Resampling genes as in Note S32, with 2,000 draws (seed 0) and each comparison's leader held fixed, the one-sided 95% lower bound of λ*, set to 0 when at least one draw in twenty reverses the two covered AUROCs, is 0.011 for REVEL against AlphaMissense on the whole panel and 0.076 on the missense subset, 0.002 for ESM1b against AlphaMissense and 0 for REVEL against CADD_raw. It is 0 for 42 of the 1,093 undecided whole-panel comparisons, every one of them with λ* below 0.05, and for 56 of the 747 on the missense subset; counted at these lower bounds, 829 and 622 whole-panel comparisons stay ordered at λ = 0.05 and 0.1, against 866 and 664. AlphaMissense's lead over CADD_raw on the missense subset, identified at the point estimates with λ* = 1.12, has a lower bound of 0.69 and is one of the 53 identified comparisons the 95% sets of Note S32 do not keep. Section 15 of recompute_dbnsfp.py reproduces these values from the licensed panel.
Where declared scopes agree, the bound still ranks, and the predictors trained without clinical labels are ranked on labels none of them trained on, although four of them, AlphaMissense, PrimateAI, popEVE and MisFit_D, learned from population data, on which many benign labels also rest. Of the 22 dbNSFP predictors whose training used no labels, evolutionary proxy labels, population-frequency weak labels or a population-genetic fit, and which take no allele frequency as an input (Table S40), 15 are worse on the missense subset than another of the 22, their bound lying wholly below that predictor's, and seven are undominated: AlphaMissense, ESM1b, popEVE, MisFit_D, the two Eigen scores and MutationAssessor (Figure 5d). On the whole panel nine of the 22 are undominated: CADD_raw, AlphaMissense, ESM1b, MisFit_D, popEVE, MutationAssessor, PROVEAN, SIFT and SIFT4G. With the gene-clustered 95% confidence sets of Note S32, 13 of the 15 stay dominated on the missense subset, PROVEAN and CADD_raw dropping out, and 12 of the 13 on the whole panel, PrimateAI dropping out; those sets are not simultaneous over the comparisons among the 22, but, made simultaneous by Holm's procedure over the 1,176 comparisons, they keep the same 13 and 12, and recompute_dbnsfp.py reproduces these counts. Label circularity through PP3 and BP4 evidence can still act on these comparisons wherever a ClinVar assertion used one of the tools, a dependence Note S60 measures on expert-panel labels. Benign labels assigned from population frequency move these AUROCs further: 560 of the 1,144 benign expert-panel missense labels of Note S60 reach their class only through BA1, BS1 or BS2, and removing them raises the AUROC of each of the four tools there, from 0.013 for AlphaMissense to 0.034 for phyloP, every interval excluding zero with genes resampled, and each by more than removing the labels that depend on PP3 or BP4 lowers it; sections 11 and 12 of recompute_pp3bp4.py reproduce these values.

## Note S68. A human missense predictor carried to the atlas through the orthologue

A species with no missense predictor of its own can borrow a human one by writing each variant as the human substitution at the aligned residue. Hu and Xie scored OMIA missense alleles this way with AlphaMissense, ESM-1b and PolyPhen-2, reporting AUROCs of 0.55 to 0.61 against OMIA's non-deleterious alleles [55]. The atlas lets the same rule be charged for what it declines and set against Evo 2, which needs no orthologue. Its negatives are population variants, a different contrast, so the values below are not comparable with theirs.
Each non-human atlas variant whose snpEff consequence includes missense_variant enters: 534 animal missense variants (455 positive, 79 negative) in 416 genes. Its protein change is found on the gene models of the Ensembl release that annotated its species (Table S39), on the canonical transcript where the release tags one and otherwise the longest coding sequence, with the reference base checked against that release's coding sequence. Its human orthologue is Ensembl Compara's, from the same release, read from both the human and the animal homology dumps and one-to-one where one exists. The candidate human proteins are those AlphaMissense [44] scores, found through UniProt's ID mapping [102] and read as AlphaMissense's own sequences; the animal protein is aligned to each by global alignment with affine gap costs [146] under BLOSUM62 [147] (gap open 11, extension 1, end gaps free), the candidate of highest identity is kept, and a variant transfers when its reference residue aligns to an identical human residue, taking AlphaMissense's prediction for that human position and the animal alternate residue. The table is tables/atlas_missense_transfer.parquet in Additional file 3, built by provenance/build_atlas_missense_transfer.py from inputs it checks against their recorded sizes and MD5 digests, and section 13 of recompute_atlas_strata.py reproduces every value below.
The rule reaches 401 of 455 (88.1%) positives and 42 of 79 (53.2%) negatives, with Wilson intervals of 84.8% to 90.8% and 42.3% to 63.8%, a class gap of +0.350 (+0.239 to +0.454) with loci resampled (Figure S8a). The aligned human residue differs for 46 (26 positives), the release's Compara names no human orthologue for 28 (19 positives), the residue faces a gap in the alignment for 16 (8 positives), and one positive has no protein change on its release's gene models. The 28 include the eight cat CMAH blood-group alleles: CMAH was inactivated in the human lineage after the Homo–Pan divergence [148], so no human predictor can score them. By species, positives transfer at 57 of 75 in cat, 111 of 119 in cattle, 11 of 17 in chicken, 119 of 128 in dog, 4 of 7 in goat, 45 of 48 in horse, 20 of 24 in pig and 34 of 37 in sheep, and negatives at 1 of 3, 26 of 36, 0 of 3, 10 of 24, 2 of 2, 2 of 8, 0 of 2 and 1 of 1 (Figure S8b).
AlphaMissense reads 0.783 (0.710 to 0.851) on the 443 variants that transfer (Figure S8c, d). With a pair coverage of 0.469, its must-answer AUROC is 0.633 (0.589 to 0.677) and its sharp bound [0.367, 0.899]. Evo 2 scores 533 of the 534 and reads 0.750 (0.692 to 0.806) at 8,192 bp, 0.751 at 1,001 bp, and GERP 0.684 on the 497 it scores, so the comparison with Evo 2 is not identified, nor that with GERP. On the 443 variants both answer, AlphaMissense leads, 0.783 against 0.697, by +0.086 (+0.007 to +0.174). Every value is pooled over the eight species, since only cattle and dog carry more than ten negatives, and every interval comes from 2,000 replicates of the two-sample locus bootstrap of Note S55, drawn within each species. Weighted by pairs within species rather than pooled, AlphaMissense's lead is +0.102 (+0.012 to +0.196) on the same replicates.
The rule's silence is class-dependent, as declared scope is on the dbNSFP and ClinVar panels: population variants sit more often where the human residue differs or has no counterpart, so an accuracy quoted on the transferred variants alone describes a different variant set from Evo 2's. Where both answer, the carried human predictor leads Evo 2, as REVEL and AlphaMissense lead CADD on the variants they share in the main text. The comparison has limits of its own: the 79 negatives are few and mostly in cattle and dog, some positives are trait rather than disease alleles (Table S13), and the scores are human predictions read at an aligned position, not predictions for the animal protein.

## Note S69. Judging each comparison inside the predictors' declared scopes, and covered AUROCs printed side by side

Neither the whole panel nor its missense subset matches every comparison to its predictors' scopes. The whole panel judges a missense predictor on nonsense and splice-site variants it never offered to score, and the missense subset judges two genome-wide predictors on the 62% of the panel that is missense. Scope matching judges each comparison on the variants inside both predictors' declared scopes instead. Each predictor's scope is read from its documentation, as the Scope column of Table S40 records it, and the scopes nest: the whole panel for 14 predictors (the nine conservation tracks, bStatistic among them, CADD_raw, DANN, MutationTaster and the two BayesDel scores), coding substitutions for three (fathmm-XF_coding and the two Eigen coding scores) and missense substitutions for the other 32. A pair meets on the narrower of its two scopes, and the confidence sets are those of Note S32 recomputed on each pair's variants.
Under that rule 431 of the 1,176 comparisons are identified, 377 of them with 95% confidence, against 83 on the whole panel and 429 on the missense subset, and all 49 predictors appear in an identified pair, against 23 on the whole panel. With the sets made simultaneous as in Note S32, 316 of the 431 stay identified under Holm's procedure and 322 under the max-T bootstrap. Two readings bracket the primary one. MetaSVM and MetaLR declare nonsynonymous SNVs, and MetaRNN rare nonsynonymous SNVs though it scores them all; read as every nonsynonymous consequence (missense, nonsense, stop-lost and start-lost), the three leave 393 comparisons identified. The five predictors whose scope Table S40 records as rare missense variants alone, M-CAP and the four VARITY scores, judged only on the 188,926 missense variants that carry no frequency at or above 0.01 in the three cohorts ClinVar reports, the sensitivity threshold of Note S61's rare-variant rule, leave 435. Of the 431, 181 join two predictors neither of which is a conservation track, 52 of them with neither listing ClinVar among its training data, and 115 of the 231 comparisons among the 22 label-free predictors are identified.
Scope matching removes the cost of a declared scope, not that of class-dependent silence inside it: 18 of the 49 reach the two classes unequally by at least 0.05 inside their declared scope, most fathmm-XF_coding at −0.224 and GERP_92_mammals at −0.207, both declining pathogenic variants more often than benign ones.
A benchmark that prints each predictor's covered AUROC in one table invites a reader to order them, a comparison made on different variants for each predictor. For every pair of the 49 we set that side-by-side difference against the head-to-head difference on the variants both score, with genes resampled on the draws of Note S32 (B = 2,000): none of the 83 identified comparisons reverses, meaning that the two differences take opposite signs, and none of the 429 on the missense subset, so the bound never orders a pair against its head-to-head result. Where the bound decides nothing, 58 of the 1,093 undecided comparisons reverse, 35 of them with gene-clustered 95% confidence per comparison and 22 under Holm's correction for the 1,093, on normal p-values from the same draws; on the missense subset 6 of the 747 reverse, none of them resolved. AlphaMissense trails CADD_raw by 0.007 side by side but leads by +0.025 (+0.021 to +0.028) on the 205,373 variants both score. Sections 12 and 13 of recompute_dbnsfp.py reproduce every value in this note from the licensed panel, and section 1b reproduces the 431 and the counts drawn from them without it, from Additional file 4's reach indicators and the covered AUROCs of dbnsfp_breakdown_points.parquet and dbnsfp_coding_scope.parquet (Additional file 3).

## Note S70. The accounting inside each gene

The panel's comparisons pool pathogenic–benign pairs across genes. Inside a gene, a predictor's reach by class, covered AUROC and bound can be read on that gene's missense variants alone, and 132 genes carry at least 30 missense variants of each class. In each, a predictor is dominated when another's lower bound exceeds its upper bound. Additional file 3 deposits every gene's accounting, 6,468 rows of reach, covered AUROC and bound (tables/dbnsfp_per_gene_bounds.parquet), and section 14 of recompute_dbnsfp.py recomputes the table from the licensed panel and checks it against the deposited copy.
Within a gene most predictors score nearly every missense variant, so the bounds are narrow and most comparisons are decided at the point estimates, a median of 987 of the 1,176 in a gene: a median of one predictor is undominated in a gene, and 39 of the 49 in at least one. The predictors undominated in the most genes, ClinPred in 44 genes, MetaRNN in 32 and BayesDel_addAF in 26, were each trained on ClinVar labels and each take allele frequency as an input, and either can separate the classes within a gene. AlphaMissense is undominated in 23 of the 132 genes and REVEL in 11, and 19 predictors lead the must-answer AUROC in at least one gene. Compared only with each other, among the 22 trained without clinical labels or allele-frequency inputs, 13 are undominated in at least one gene, AlphaMissense in 74 and popEVE in 34.
These are point estimates on a few dozen to a few hundred variants of each class, and they carry no interval. Where one predictor is undominated, we resampled the gene's pathogenic and benign variants separately (B = 2,000, every predictor on the same draws) and asked whether its lower bound stays above every other predictor's upper bound in at least 97.5% of draws: in 2 of the 87 genes where one predictor is undominated, its lead survives resampling, BayesDel_addAF in COL7A1 and ClinPred in HNF1A. Inside a gene, then, the number of labelled variants limits the ordering more than reach does. A ranking pooled across genes need not hold in a given gene, as calibrated performance need not [40], but a gene's own labels rarely name its leader either, and the table gives the bounds each gene allows, to be read with that sampling error.

## Note S71. Calibrated evidence as likelihood ratios, and ClinVar's variants of uncertain significance

ClinGen's calibrations set each tool's PP3 and BP4 intervals so that a strength reaches the target odds of pathogenicity, 2.08 for supporting, 4.33 for moderate, 18.7 for strong and 350 for very strong evidence, with the reciprocals for BP4 [31,49]. A level's likelihood ratio on ClinVar's missense variants, the share of pathogenic variants at that level over the share of benign ones, averages over the level, so it asks whether the level delivers those odds on these labels on average, a weaker test than the calibration's own, which sets them at the level's inner threshold. Table S42 gives each level's ratio on all labels, with genes resampled (B = 2,000; ClinVar's first gene symbol, a variant without one being its own cluster), and the stratum, of all labels and the five below, in which it comes closest to its target: labels the 2021 release carried, those added between the 2021 and 2023 releases, those added after 2023, one-star labels and labels at two stars or more.
Every PP3 and BP4 level of the four tools meets its target odds at its point estimate in every stratum, though labels resting on the tools' own evidence (Note S60) can inflate these ratios. REVEL's PP3 strong level, for example, reads 217 on all labels, 163 (125 to 221) on the labels the 2021 release carried and 149 on those added between the two releases, against a target of 18.7, and the calibrations hold on the newest labels too. The calibrations set each strength at its level's inner threshold, where the level's evidence is weakest. Read in the 1% of a tool's scored missense variants nearest that threshold inside the level (the whole level where it holds fewer), every level meets its target there except CADD's BP4 supporting level, which reads 0.56 (0.47 to 0.65) against 0.48, an interval that reaches the target. At that threshold phyloP's levels read 5.23 and 9.99 (PP3 supporting and moderate) and 0.36 and 0.076 (BP4 supporting and moderate), against 2.08, 4.33, 0.48 and 0.23. Section 17 of recompute_pp3bp4.py reproduces these ratios, phyloP's without licensed input. The indeterminate band, which carries no evidence, moves with label age: phyloP's reads 0.89 on the labels the 2021 release carried and 1.44 on those added after 2023, against 1.18 on all labels. A declined variant, taken as its own level, carries a ratio of 0.18 (0.11 to 0.27) for REVEL and 0.25 (0.15 to 0.38) for AlphaMissense, since both decline benign missense variants more often than pathogenic ones. A decline nonetheless carries no evidence: no calibration assigns it any, and the ratio reflects which variants each release leaves out, not a rule a laboratory could carry to other variants.
ClinVar's variants of uncertain significance are where a laboratory most needs computational evidence. The archived copy of the release that built the reach panel holds 2,097,787 single-nucleotide missense variants classified as of uncertain significance at one review star or better, and its labelled records are exactly the reach panel's, which confirms the copy (tables/clinvar_missense_vus_counts.parquet, built by provenance/build_clinvar_vus.py). AlphaMissense's own release declines 2.7% of them, between the 1.7% of pathogenic and 7.0% of benign labelled missense variants it declines, and its calibrated intervals [46] give PP3 evidence to 14.3% and BP4 evidence to 47.4%, leaving 38.2% indeterminate or unscored, against 24.9% of pathogenic labelled missense variants (Table S36). Section 8 of recompute_pp3bp4.py reproduces these shares from the deposited counts without licensed input, and section 6 every cell of Table S42.

## Note S72. Functional labels: BRCA1 saturation genome editing and LDLR uptake against ClinVar, and AlphaMissense on the BRCA1 panel

A label that no computational evidence can enter checks the tools' ClinVar results for concordance, though on BRCA1 it cannot test circularity through PP3: BRCA1's expert panel reads BayesDel rather than REVEL, AlphaMissense or CADD for PP3, and 55 of the 65 BRCA1 single-nucleotide classifications in the ClinGen Evidence Repository apply the functional criteria PS3 or BS3. The saturation-genome-editing classes of BRCA1 [50] are measured, not assigned, so no PP3 or BP4 point reaches them. The panel is on GRCh37; BRCA1 lies in a stretch of chromosome 17 whose GRCh38 coordinate is the GRCh37 one plus 1,847,983, and that offset finds 314 of its missense substitutions in the ClinVar panel, against 142 and 126 for the offsets one base either side. On the 308 BRCA1 missense substitutions that saturation genome editing and ClinVar both label at one star or better, the functional class, loss of function against functional or intermediate, and ClinVar's agree on 300.
AlphaMissense, REVEL and CADD read 0.944, 0.926 and 0.902 on the functional labels and 0.954, 0.938 and 0.931 on ClinVar's, the same order on both, and REVEL minus AlphaMissense on the functional labels is −0.018 (−0.048 to +0.011) with residues resampled (B = 2,000). ClinVar's labels read each tool slightly higher, on eight discordant labels and without an interval; on this gene they do not reorder the three. The check covers one gene, and BRCA1's ClinVar classifications can themselves cite functional assays such as this one. Section 7 of recompute_pp3bp4.py reproduces these values.
On all 2,077 missense substitutions of the saturation-editing panel, AlphaMissense, read from its own release by protein substitution (tables/brca1_alphamissense.parquet, 1,831 distinct substitutions, built by provenance/build_brca1_alphamissense.py), separates loss-of-function variants at 0.889 against Evo 2's 0.822 at 8,192 bp, a lead of +0.067 (+0.034 to +0.099) with residues resampled (B = 2,000; recompute_brca1.py). As on the atlas's transferred variants (Note S68), the missense predictor leads where it answers.
LDLR allows the test of circularity through PP3 that BRCA1 does not. Its expert panel, the Familial Hypercholesterolemia VCEP, reads REVEL for PP3 and BP4, and of its 233 LDLR single-nucleotide classifications in the Evidence Repository, 33 reach their class only with PP3 or BP4 points and 82 apply PS3 or BS3. Tabet et al. [51] measured LDL uptake for nearly every LDLR substitution, scaled so that 0 and 1 are the medians of nonsense and synonymous variants, and propose a score of 0.04 or below, set on the clinical diagnoses of UK Biobank carriers rather than on ClinVar, as the threshold for a functional mutation; that class is the functional label here. Their uptake scores, distributed by MaveDB under CC0 1.0 (urn:mavedb:00001269-a-1), are carried to the GRCh38 single-nucleotide variants of each substitution in tables/ldlr_functional_labels.parquet, built by provenance/build_ldlr_functional_labels.py. On the 721 LDLR missense variants that the assay scores and ClinVar labels at one star or better, 685 pathogenic and 36 benign, the two labels agree on 182: all 146 variants at or below the threshold are pathogenic in ClinVar, as are 539 of the 575 above it. AlphaMissense, REVEL and CADD read 0.706, 0.616 and 0.572 on the functional labels and 0.971, 0.972 and 0.968 on ClinVar's, so REVEL, behind AlphaMissense on the functional labels, draws level with it on ClinVar's: REVEL minus AlphaMissense is −0.090 (−0.148 to −0.032) and +0.001 (−0.022 to +0.020) with residues resampled (B = 2,000), a shift of +0.091 (+0.028 to +0.149) on the same draws. Reuse of REVEL's evidence would move the difference that way, but the labels that record the reuse carry little of it: removing the 33 that depend on PP3 or BP4, all among the 721, lowers REVEL's AUROC on ClinVar's labels by 0.002 (0.000 to 0.005) more than AlphaMissense's. The rest reflects what the functional labels ask, loss of uptake against partial or normal uptake among variants ClinVar mostly calls pathogenic, and there AlphaMissense leads; on LDLR the two labels measure different things, and the test cannot separate reuse that the Evidence Repository does not record from that difference. Sections 15 and 16 of recompute_pp3bp4.py reproduce these values.

## Note S73. GPN-Star, a second genomic language model, on the chicken and human atlas variants, and SIFT's reach on the atlas

GPN-Star learns constraint from whole-genome alignments along an explicit phylogeny, and its authors release precomputed scores for several species, two of them in the atlas: chicken, on galGal6, the atlas's GRCg6a, and human, on GRCh38 [21]. For the chicken model (score set gg6) and the human default model (gpn-star-hg38-m447-200m), the calibrated log-likelihood ratio (llr_calibrated, which the release describes as calibrated for mutation rate) of each atlas variant's alternate allele was read from the release's tracks by range request; the reference allele's track carries an explicit zero at every atlas variant, so each atlas reference allele is the release's. The table is tables/atlas_gpnstar.parquet in Additional file 3, built by provenance/build_atlas_gpnstar.py, and values read through the release's Parquet files agree with it for all 3,308 variants.
GPN-Star scores every atlas variant of both species. At 8,192 bp Evo 2 reads 0.954 in chicken and 0.974 in human, and GPN-Star 0.931 and 0.969, GPN-Star minus Evo 2 differences of −0.022 (−0.096 to +0.054) and −0.005 (−0.012 to +0.002) with positive-bearing 100-kb loci resampled as in Note S55 (B = 2,000), so neither model is resolved ahead of the other in either species. Where GERP scores, GPN-Star leads it by +0.200 (+0.077 to +0.356) and +0.095 (+0.082 to +0.109), so in these two species the margin over conservation is not particular to Evo 2. Section 14 of recompute_atlas_strata.py reproduces each value.
SIFT [1] is the missense predictor Ensembl's Variant Effect Predictor [56] computes for these species, and it was read for every atlas variant through Ensembl's REST API, release 116, and for sheep through its release-110 archive (tables/atlas_sift.parquet, built by provenance/build_atlas_sift.py). Ensembl serves the atlas assemblies of eight of the nine species: human, goat, cat, horse, pig and chicken on the same assembly in release 116, sheep's Oar_rambouillet_v1.0 in release 110, the last release to carry it, and cattle on ARS-UCD2.0, whose sequence equals ARS-UCD1.2's at all ten 8,192-bp golden windows and at every cattle variant's reference base. Release 116 serves neither CanFam3.1 nor a mapping from it, and CanFam3.1's last release, 104, has no REST server, so the 2,497 dog variants are not mapped, and 4 cat positives are not mapped because the genome does not carry their reference base. Every response was validated: two sentinel sequences from the golden windows in each sequence request, and in each VEP response the expected assembly, the species' transcripts and the verified reference base in VEP's own reference codon, with two replicate requests required to agree.
SIFT scores amino-acid substitutions only, so its reach follows the atlas's composition: positives at 75 of 121 in cat, 122 of 188 in cattle, 15 of 28 in chicken, 7 of 9 in goat, 50 of 71 in horse, 652 of 1,500 in human, 24 of 36 in pig and 37 of 56 in sheep, and negatives at 3 of 1,240, 33 of 1,880, 2 of 280, 2 of 90, 8 of 710, 241 of 1,500, 1 of 360 and 2 of 560. On average it answers 63.1% of positives and 2.8% of negatives, and in human 43.5% of positives and 16.1% of negatives. Its bound [ρA, ρA + 1 − ρ] contains [ρ, 1 − ρ] whatever its covered AUROC A, and ρ is below 0.02 in each of the seven animal species, so there neither Evo 2 nor GERP can be ordered against it, whatever SIFT's scores. Section 15 of recompute_atlas_strata.py reproduces every value in this paragraph from the deposited table.
SIFT's scores are deposited in the same table as sift_score, the lowest over the transcripts that carry one; Ensembl computes them and places no restriction on its data (LICENSING.md in Additional file 3). Read with lower scores as more deleterious, SIFT's covered AUROC is 0.828 (0.795 to 0.858) in human and 0.657 (0.556 to 0.757) in cattle, the two species with more than ten scored negatives, with positive-bearing 100-kb loci resampled as in Note S55 (B = 2,000); in the other six species it rests on one to eight negatives and reads 0.884 in cat, 0.867 in chicken, 0.714 in goat, 0.858 in horse, 0.833 in pig and 0.797 in sheep. In human, where it answers far more negatives, its bound is then [0.058, 0.988], which contains Evo 2's 0.974 and GERP's [0.805, 0.884], so there too neither is ordered against it. On the variants SIFT scores, Evo 2 at 8,192 bp reads 0.903 in human and 0.652 in cattle: it leads SIFT in human by +0.076 (+0.043 to +0.109), with loci resampled as above, and in cattle neither is resolved ahead of the other, −0.005 (−0.123 to +0.112). Section 15 of recompute_atlas_strata.py reproduces each value.

## Note S74. The rule applied to published benchmarks

A published ordering of two predictors is only as decided as their reaches allow, and many benchmarks print enough to check. We screened 28 benchmark and tool articles on missense, splicing and non-coding predictors, published from 2018 to 2025 and selected by us rather than by a systematic search (tables/literature_audit_papers.csv in Additional file 3, with the reason for each exclusion), and kept the 16 that print AUROCs [36,59,149,150,151,152,153,154,155,156,157,158,159,160,161,162]. For each printed AUROC we recorded what the article says about the variants the predictor scored, and where it says it: per-class scored counts, printed or recovered by exhaustive integer search over the printed sensitivity, specificity, accuracy and, where printed, PPV, NPV or MCC, keeping every split that reproduces them to their printed precision; the number unscored alone, the pair coverage then taken over every split of it between the classes; the scored share alone, the pair coverage then approximated as its square; a comparison on a common set; or nothing. Every value was read a second time against its source, which found no transcription error.
Each printed AUROC A with pair coverage ρ is bounded by [ρA, ρA + 1 − ρ], or by A ∓ (1 − ρ) where the article fills unscored variants with a value, and an ordering the article states between two predictors is identified when the leader's lower bound clears the other's upper bound for every admissible split, not identified when it does for none, and dependent on the split otherwise. Eight of the 16 rank predictors by AUROC while reporting how many variants each scored. Seven of the eight state at least one ordering that is not identified, counted over every ordering each states, those its values leave unassessable included: 23 of 330 in Li et al., 18 of 66 in Wang et al., 3 of 20 in Rastogi et al., 7 of 192 in Radjasandirane et al., 14 of 44 in Barbosa et al., 3 of 29 in Riepe et al. and 4 of 12 in Strauch et al. Rastogi et al. also rank all of their predictors in a column of their Supplementary Table S1, and of the 305 further orderings its rank column implies, 122 are not identified; only the 20 their text states are counted above. The eighth, Leman et al., prints only the share each predictor scores on its larger set, so ρ is taken there as that share squared, an approximation rather than a bound, and on it the four stated orderings are identified. Over the eight, 72 of the 697 stated orderings (10.3%) are not identified. Of the other eight articles, Malhis et al., Gunning et al. and Lord et al. compare predictors on variants every one of them scores, so their stated orderings are identified by construction; Caron et al., Rentzsch et al. and Rowlands et al. do not report how many variants each predictor scored, so theirs cannot be assessed; and Cannon et al. and Cubuk et al. print counts but state no AUROC ranking.
Two examples show what the check changes. Wang et al. rank FATHMM-XF first at 0.8033 on non-coding ClinVar variants, ahead of FATHMM-MKL at 0.7954 and ReMM at 0.7848, both of which score every variant. FATHMM-XF scores 446 of 515 pathogenic and 1,688 of 1,850 benign variants, the one split its printed metrics allow, so charged for the rest it could lie anywhere from 0.63 to 0.84, and neither ordering is identified. Barbosa et al. state that PDIVAS has the highest auROC on deep intronic variants: PDIVAS at 0.974 with 27 of 484 variants unscored, not identified above Pangolin at 0.973, which scores all 484; PDIVAS declares only variants more than 50 bp from a splice site, and the bound charges it on the panel as the article compares it. Barbosa et al., Rastogi et al. and Radjasandirane et al. also report analyses that account for coverage, and Wang et al. print each predictor's missing rate beside the ranking; the check concerns only the AUROC orderings stated.
Where the printed values leave the class split open, as a bare count of unscored variants or as several splits that fit the printed metrics, the split is bracketed, and 14 stated orderings, 1 in Rastogi et al. and 13 in Radjasandirane et al., depend on it. Radjasandirane et al. are read in the second version of their preprint [150], since published in Genomics under a new title. Section H of recompute_identification.py derives every pair coverage from tables/literature_audit_predictors.csv and reproduces every count above without licensed input.

## Note S75. Readouts that reorder models on an independent benchmark

TraitGym distributes, for its Mendelian-trait test set of 338 causal variants, each matched to nine controls [61], the zero-shot scores of 17 models, and its workflow documents more than one readout for many of them: the log-likelihood ratio and its absolute value for each genomic language model, the embedding Euclidean distance and inner product where they were computed, and one readout each for Enformer, Borzoi, CADD, Sei and three conservation tracks, 34 readouts in all, each with the orientation the workflow declares. Its metric is average precision within each chromosome, weighted by the chromosome's share of variants; our values equal its leaderboard's for 12 of the 17 models, and for the other five the leaderboard exceeds every readout TraitGym distributes, so those five are compared on the distributed readouts only. CADD reads 0.7122 and GPN-MSA's log-likelihood ratio 0.6945, as on the leaderboard; by the same metric Evo 2-40B reads 0.557 against 0.527 for phastCons-43p, 0.655 for phyloP-100v and 0.696 for phyloP-241m. By AUROC, Evo 2-40B's log-likelihood ratio reads 0.8495 in the orientation the workflow declares (0.1505 as distributed), against 0.8594 for phastCons-43p; TraitGym annotates every variant in the set with a noncoding consequence.
For each pair of models, every pairing of one readout of each gives a difference, and 26 of the 136 pairs, of the 115 in which either model offers a choice of readout, change order with the pairing. In 7 of them both orders are resolved, pairing by pairing and without adjustment for the pairings compared: a pairing that puts either model ahead does so by more than 1.96 standard errors, the errors from 2,000 resamples of the 19 chromosomes, the unit TraitGym itself resamples, each drawn chromosome weighted by its number of variants. Each of the 7 involves GPN-MSA, and in each the pairings that put GPN-MSA behind read it by Euclidean distance or inner product. The count is 5 with a Bonferroni correction over the pairings within each pair, 10 with percentile intervals in place of standard errors, and 9 when the causal variants' loci are resampled, each matched group drawn with the causal variant it was matched to. The causal variants chain into 107 clusters within 10 kb, and the count is the same with the chaining widened to 100 kb or 1 Mb. For example, Borzoi leads GPN-MSA read by Euclidean distance by 0.229 but trails GPN-MSA read by its log-likelihood ratio by 0.259. On this benchmark as on the atlas, a comparison between two models must name their readouts, or it answers a different question for each choice of them. Sections 3 and 4 of recompute_strand.py reproduce these values from tables/traitgym_mendelian_readouts.parquet.

## Note S76. The fitted components against the DOME recommendations

The article fits few models, and none is its subject: Evo 2 and every published predictor are scored as released. The fitted components are the sequence-blind baselines of the dbNSFP panel, the cross-validated logistic models that measure what Evo 2 adds to annotation and conservation (Note S15), the calibration and selective-prediction layers (Notes S44 to S54 and S63), the supervised floor and embedding probe of Note S33 and the k-mer composition classifier of Note S5. For these, following the DOME recommendations for supervised learning in biology [163]:
Data. Every fitted component uses deposited panels (Additional files 2 to 6), whose sizes and class counts are stated where each is used, except the supervised floor's 500-bp sequence windows, which are not deposited. The gene and consequence baselines are fitted out of fold, in five label-stratified folds over eight seeds, so no variant's own label enters its prediction; calibration transfer holds each species out of its own map (Note S54); the supervised floor is evaluated leave-one-species-out and by 5-fold stratified cross-validation within species (Note S33).
Optimisation. The gene baseline is an empirical pathogenic rate shrunk towards the training folds' rate with a pseudo-count of one, and the gene-and-consequence baseline shrinks the rate of each gene and consequence pair in the same way towards its consequence's rate, itself shrunk towards the training folds' rate; the pseudo-count is not tuned. Across ten estimators of the pair's rate (pseudo-counts of 0.1, 1 and 5 towards the training folds' or the consequence's rate, 1 and 5 towards the gene's, add-one, and the unshrunk rate), the gene-and-consequence baseline reads 0.959 to 0.975, above CADD's must-answer AUROC of 0.968 in 7 of them and above every other predictor's in all ten, and the bound rule identifies it above 13 to 23 of the 49 (recompute_labelfree_baseline.py). The gene baseline alone, at 0.880, lies above the must-answer AUROC of 45 of the 49 predictors and is identified above 10 of them and below 3. The gene-and-frequency baseline of the missense subset is an unpenalised logistic regression on the logit of the gene rate, the base-10 logarithm of the largest ExAC, ESP or 1000 Genomes frequency ClinVar carries (floored at one in a million, a variant without one read as zero) and whether any frequency is present. Within the training folds each row takes its gene rate out of fold, so no row's rate has seen its own label, and the held-out fold takes it from all the training folds (recompute_labelfree_baseline.py). The combination models are logistic regressions on standardised scores, fitted under 5-fold cross-validation (Note S15). Calibration is a two-parameter sigmoid or an isotonic map fitted on donor species (Note S48), and the supervised floor's architectures, schedules and seeds are listed in Note S33. Resampling units and replicate counts are in Table S38, and each script records its seeds.
Model. Each fitted model is a formula of a few parameters or a monotone map, or, for the supervised floor, a small network or boosted tree deposited with its code in Additional file 2; glmtrust implements the baselines and the calibration and abstention layers.
Evaluation. Discrimination is AUROC with the resampling unit of Table S38, calibration error is reported under twelve binning rules (Note S44), and every comparison is charged for what it declines. The claims of the paper rest on released predictors and on bounds, which no fitted component changes.

## Figure S1. Reliability of the transferred calibration, species by species

Evo 2-40B at the 1,001-bp single-token readout, 11,130 variants. One panel per species, in the tree order of Figure 9, and a last panel with all nine pooled; in each, ten equal-mass bins, each marker a tenth of the panel's variants at the bin's mean predicted probability and its observed share of positives, against the diagonal of perfect calibration. Panel titles give n and the share of positives. The map over-predicts in the middle of the range on the 10:1 panels and under-predicts on human, the one panel built 1:1, where variants given a probability near 0.01 are about 30% positive; human's miscalibration is the largest under every transferred map (Table S23) and survives a prevalence correction (Table S24). No expected calibration error is printed: the estimators disagree on this posterior (Note S44), and Tables S22 and S23 give them.

## Figure S2. Evo 2 reads near chance on one porcine cis-eQTL panel

Evo 2-40B zero-shot on high posterior inclusion probability (PIP) candidate causal pig cis-eQTL variants (PigGTEx, SuSiE-inf [75,76]) against within-eGene non-causal controls, matched on eGene, not linkage disequilibrium (LD). (a) The scale the panel is drawn on, in base pairs. Each variant that is not its eGene's lead credible-set variant sits at its distance from that lead against its Evo 2 score, with both populations repeated as marginals; dashed lines mark the two readout window lengths, 1,001 and 8,192 bp, and dotted lines in the upper marginal each population's median distance. Credible sets are organised at 10⁵ bp, while the single-token readout conditions on 501 bases of left context and never reads the variant's downstream sequence. (c) The reading against model scale: below chance at every scale, while the whole-atlas macro rises; that macro includes human, whose 1B scores are anomalous (Note S15), and without human reads 0.896 at 1B, 0.924 at 7B and 0.939 at 40B. The sequence and conservation scorers collapse, including Nucleotide Transformer [60] and three GERP flavours; the open marker on those rows is the same scorer on the coding panel. Saturation genome editing of BRCA1 [50] assayed 3,893 single-nucleotide substitutions as cell-survival depletion in haploid HAP1 cells; deleteriousness is the negated function score. The quilt's colour scale diverges about the median Evo 2 value of the experimentally functional substitutions (+0.00097), not about zero, so blue marks a substitution below that median rather than an effect of opposite sign. (b) Per-residue deleteriousness on the RING (PDB 1JM7) and BRCT (PDB 1T29) folds, predicted above measured, each map independently min–max scaled. The uncoloured chain beside RING is BARD1, its partner in 1JM7, drawn without colour because it carries no BRCA1 variant; the grey spheres are the structural zinc ions, and the two labelled side chains are the zinc-cage cysteines C24 and C61. (c) Per-residue predicted against measured deleteriousness by domain, the 11 linker residues in grey: Spearman ρ = +0.52 across all 326 scored residues [+0.43, +0.59]. Variant-level discrimination is AUROC 0.874 [0.858, 0.889], and 0.822 [0.797, 0.846] on the 2,077 missense substitutions alone. Per-domain correlations, the zinc-ligand readings, the start-codon miss, the substitutions VEP does not map to the canonical transcript and the comparison with the published Evo 2-1B value are in Note S43.

|---|---|---|---|---|
| Human | 3,000 | 0.974 | [0.968, 0.979] | 0.825 |
| Cattle | 2,067 | 0.974 | [0.958, 0.987] | 0.900 |
| Dog | 2,496 | 0.970 | [0.952, 0.984] | 0.888 |

| Pig | 396 | 0.863 | [0.753, 0.957] | 0.848 |
| Horse | 766 | 0.941 | [0.895, 0.978] | 0.883 |
| Cat | 1,362 | 0.890 | [0.838, 0.938] | 0.848 |
| Chicken | 308 | 0.954 | [0.913, 0.986] | 0.861 |

Both columns are computed on the same variants, every variant carrying an 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood score, 11,109 of the 11,130 in the panel, so the two readouts are matched variant-for-variant and isolate the readout effect: +0.065 AUROC by the species mean, +0.092 pooled [+0.083, +0.101]. Table 2 is computed on the full 11,130-variant panel and this table on the 11,109 carrying an 8,192-bp score, so n differs in the five species that lose variants at 8,192 bp (horse 15, cat 3, and one each in goat, cattle and dog; Note S27), and the 1,001-bp AUROC shifts with it at three decimals in goat, horse and cat. That panel difference is the whole of the residual, and the readout effect above is computed on the matched subset. The intervals in this table are variant-level and are not cluster-robust. Goat's [0.907, 0.994] is therefore the unclustered interval, and it is printed here for completeness only: goat's nine positives occupy two loci, eight of them within a 570-bp span, giving 1.2 effective independent positions, which is why Table 2 declines to print an interval for goat at all. Read goat's AUROC as a descriptive point estimate, as Table 2 does.
**Table S2. Reach: what the conservation baseline can be run on**

| Species | n | positive scorable | negative scorable | class gap | deployment AUROC penalty |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Goat | 99 | 0.889 | 0.800 | +0.089 | −0.081 |
| Chicken | 308 | 0.964 | 0.882 | +0.082 | −0.034 |
| Pig | 396 | 0.750 | 0.392 | +0.358 | −0.177 |
| Sheep | 616 | 0.732 | 0.732 | +0.000 | −0.169 |
| Horse | 781 | 0.930 | 0.441 | +0.489 | −0.245 |
| Cat | 1,365 | 0.936 | 0.742 | +0.194 | −0.106 |
| Cattle | 2,068 | 1.000 | 0.956 | +0.044 | −0.014 |
| Dog | 2,497 | 0.921 | 0.911 | +0.009 | −0.062 |
| Human | 3,000 | 0.963 | 0.957 | +0.007 | −0.029 |

Conservation scores are undefined where the alignment or track is unavailable, and missingness is marginally associated with class in these panels; the consequence-matched analysis does not establish its cause. Every per-species conservation AUROC computed this way is therefore computed on a class-skewed subset of the panel it is drawn from, whose full-panel value is unidentified without an assumption about the unscored variants; we establish that within our own nine panels and do not survey the published literature. The missingness is also spatially structured rather than random: a Wald–Wolfowitz runs test on the no-call pattern rejects randomly scattered no-calls in the three largest panels (human z = −13.87 negative and −13.94 positive, dog −10.86, cattle −7.31). That cuts both ways: it is independent evidence that the holes are structural, and it means the per-variant reach intervals of the Figure S5 legend assume an independence of missingness that the runs test rejects, so they understate uncertainty in exactly those three panels. The test only detects clustering on the scale of the spacing between sampled variants, which is 0.41 Mb in human, 0.58 Mb in dog and 0.97 Mb in cattle, and 2.42 Mb in pig and 2.43 Mb in horse. It has no power against kilobase-scale alignment gaps, so non-rejection in a panel is not evidence that the no-calls are unclustered. The penalty column above charges every ranking pair that touches a no-call at one half, following the full evaluation mode of the Critical Assessment of Functional Annotation (CAFA) in charging for silence, though CAFA itself assigns zero. That is a scoring convention and not a forecast of what conservation would emit if it were forced to answer, so we give the alternatives here. Under the paper's rule the penalty is −0.1019 by the unweighted species mean and −0.2448 in horse. Giving each no-call the conservation score's own median value and then taking AUROC over the whole panel gives −0.0235 and −0.0100. Drawing each no-call from the conservation score's own score distribution gives −0.0398 and −0.0650. The two alternatives are milder because the scored negatives sit at low conservation and the scored positives at high, so a filled-in value inherits information from that arrangement. We keep the first rule throughout this paper, and a reader who prefers a different one should take these figures in place of the tabulated ones.
**Table S3. Baseline suite**

| Species | n (matched) | GERP | phyloP | phyloP − GERP | Evo 2 @8,192 | Evo 2 @8,192 − phyloP | Evo 2 @1,001 | Evo 2 @1,001 − phyloP |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cattle | 1,921 | 0.828 | 0.931 | +0.103 | 0.978 | +0.047 | 0.909 | −0.022 |
| Chicken | 274 | 0.725 | 0.793 | +0.068 | 0.956 | +0.163 | 0.862 | +0.069 |
| Human | 2,880 | 0.874 | 0.925 | +0.051 | 0.974 | +0.049 | 0.825 | −0.100 |

GERP is the sole cross-species conservation baseline because it is the only per-base score available for all nine assemblies (Methods). For chicken (galGal6/GRCg6a UCSC phyloP77way) and human (hg38/GRCh38 UCSC phyloP100way), which carry a genuine UCSC phyloP track, and cattle (bosTau9), whose phyloP comes from Zenodo record 13332541 (Roslin) over a Cactus 241-way alignment rather than from UCSC, so that row rests on a different alignment and species set from the two beside it, we recomputed conservation discrimination from phyloP queried at each variant position. All four scores above are evaluated on exactly the same variants (the intersection of the 8,192-bp-scored, GERP-scorable and phyloP-scorable sets; cattle n = 1,921), so every comparison is like-for-like. Two things follow. First, phyloP is a stronger baseline than GERP in all three species, so every Evo 2-versus-conservation margin reported against GERP is an upper bound rather than a conservative one; GERP is kept as the primary comparator for uniformity across all nine assemblies, not for strength. Second, and more important, Evo 2 at the reference-implementation 8,192-bp readout beats even phyloP in all three, so the readout head-to-head (Table S7) is robust to using the stronger baseline; at the 1,001-bp readout, by contrast, phyloP beats Evo 2 in two of three, reinforcing that the single-token readout understates the model.
phyloP was queried with pybigtools from genuine bigWig tracks; chromosome coordinates were mapped through the UCSC chromAlias, and no positive variant was dropped for want of a coordinate in any species. Per-variant phyloP is deposited for human only (analyses/results/human_phylop_pervariant.parquet, Additional file 2), from which the human row recomputes; for cattle and chicken the cross-check kept only its summaries (reports/phylop_crosscheck.json, Additional file 2), so those two rows cannot be recomputed from the deposit.

| Species | n | positives | single-token, 1 kb | single-token, 8 kb | window-mean, central 1 kb | window-mean, 8 kb | left context and scorer | aggregation |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Goat | 98 | 9 | 0.950 | 0.951 | 0.948 | 0.955 | +0.001 | +0.004 |
| Chicken | 228 | 28 | 0.863 | 0.916 | 0.952 | 0.947 | +0.054 | +0.031 |
| Pig | 236 | 36 | 0.843 | 0.866 | 0.878 | 0.861 | +0.023 | −0.005 |
| Sheep | 256 | 56 | 0.903 | 0.957 | 0.965 | 0.961 | +0.054 | +0.004 |
| Horse | 271 | 71 | 0.884 | 0.899 | 0.957 | 0.945 | +0.015 | +0.046 |
| Cat | 322 | 122 | 0.840 | 0.877 | 0.895 | 0.887 | +0.037 | +0.010 |
| Cattle | 388 | 188 | 0.893 | 0.931 | 0.969 | 0.973 | +0.038 | +0.042 |
| Dog | 400 | 200 | 0.890 | 0.929 | 0.979 | 0.979 | +0.039 | +0.050 |
| Human | 400 | 200 | 0.814 | 0.875 | 0.974 | 0.972 | +0.061 | +0.097 |

Both readouts are accounting choices, not results. The +0.065 species-mean advantage of the 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood over the 1,001-bp single-token variant-delta (Fig. 10a) confounds two choices: a longer model context and averaging surprise across the window. We separate them on 2,599 atlas variants (at most 200 positives and 200 negatives per species), reading three quantities from a single 8,192-bp forward pass per sequence, which hold the model and context fixed and vary only the aggregation scope, and a fourth from the separate 1,001-bp pass. The left-context-and-scorer term is the gain of the single-token readout as its left context grows from ~0.5 kb (the 1,001-bp window) to ~4 kb, a step that also moves from the streamed scorer to the resident one; the aggregation term is the further gain of the full-window mean over that single position.
**Table S9 (continued)**

|  | left context and scorer term | aggregation term | total |
|---|---|---|---|
| species mean (± SE) | +0.0359 ± 0.0066 | +0.0309 ± 0.0107 | +0.0668 ± 0.0149 |
| pooled (95% CI) | +0.0377 [+0.0286, +0.0469] | +0.0374 [+0.0267, +0.0483] | +0.0751 [+0.0611, +0.0898] |

The total recovered here (+0.0668 species mean) reproduces the readout effect of Fig. 10a (+0.065), evidence that the effect size is representative even though the subset is not class-balanced: it retains every positive in seven species and keeps at most 200 positives and 200 negatives in each, so dog and human are held at 200 positives and the per-species positive fraction runs above the ~9% of the full panels, reaching 50% in dog (human's full panel is already 1:1). The split into context and aggregation is path-dependent. Taking context first (single-token readout, window 1 kb to 8 kb) then aggregation (single to window-mean at 8 kb) gives context +0.0359 and aggregation +0.0309; taking aggregation first (single to window-mean at 1 kb) then context (window-mean 1 kb to 8 kb) gives aggregation +0.0708 and context −0.0040. The total minus the two first steps, each measured from the single-token 1-kb cell, is −0.0399. That residual is not a factorial interaction and does not show that the two factors overlap: the fourth cell of a complete two-by-two, a window mean at 1,001-bp conditioning context, is not part of the design, so the aggregation-first path substitutes the central-1-kb mean of the 8,192-bp pass, which moves conditioning context as well as aggregation. The +0.0349 gap between the two single-factor conditions is likewise a difference between conditions rather than an interaction term. A 'roughly equal halves' reading holds only under the first ordering. What is robust across orderings is that the averaged window can stop at 1 kb: the central 1-kb window-mean exceeds the full 8-kb window-mean in six of nine species (dog's two values tie at the printed precision) (species mean 0.946 against 0.942), so extending the averaged window past 1 kb adds nothing. As a correctness check the full-window mean recomputed here reproduces the deposited 8,192-bp mean-log-likelihood to within 0.0003 pooled AUROC.

AUPRC stays well above the base rate in every species, so the discrimination at this readout is not a base-rate artefact. The one setting where precision-recall collapses is the matched control of the concurrent cross-species work cited in the main text (AUPRC 0.166 against a ~9% base rate, versus its 0.717 AUROC), which is why we quote both metrics in Note S6. Both figures are that paper's, read from its text and held by no artefact here; its 0.717 is not the 0.717 this paper reports for HAL's covered AUROC.
**Table S11. Reach and must-answer accuracy for five human scores on ClinVar**

| score | reach (all) | reach (pos.) | reach (neg.) | covered | must-answer | penalty | missingness alone |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| phastCons | 0.99997 | 0.99999 | 0.99996 | 0.8443 | 0.8443 | 0.00002 | 0.5000 |
| phyloP | 0.99997 | 0.99999 | 0.99996 | 0.9217 | 0.9217 | 0.00002 | 0.5000 |
| CADD | 0.99879 | 0.99935 | 0.99872 | 0.9914 | 0.9904 | 0.00095 | 0.5003 |
| REVEL | 0.17642 | 0.37313 | 0.14985 | 0.9575 | 0.5256 | 0.43192 | 0.6116 |
| AlphaMissense | 0.13493 | 0.33665 | 0.10768 | 0.9631 | 0.5168 | 0.44635 | 0.6145 |

1,434,335 single-nucleotide variants at a review status of one star or better (170,680 pathogenic and 1,263,655 benign, the positive and negative classes of this panel). Covered is AUROC over the variants each score can score; must-answer charges every pair touching an unscorable variant at one half, a CAFA-style full-evaluation convention (CAFA itself assigns zero). Missingness alone is the AUROC of the indicator of whether a value was returned, discarding the values. For a score that answers everywhere the indicator never varies and its midrank AUROC is exactly 0.5, as roc_auc_score returns; a value of 0.5000 can also mean reach that is near-complete and equal in both classes.
The two missense-only scores carry a missingness AUROC above their own must-answer AUROC. That is a statement about this panel's composition and not about either method: reach tracks consequence class here, and ClinVar's pathogenic variants are missense-enriched, so the indicator is largely a missense indicator. Table S12 narrows the confound by restricting to the variant classes dbNSFP annotates, where no predictor has a missingness AUROC above its own must-answer AUROC under the signed test, although 30 of the 48 with a defined missingness AUROC do read orientation-free. That comparison is descriptive; the test of the values, what ranking by the indicator and then by value adds to the indicator, excludes zero for all 49 (Note S61).
**Table S12. Reach and its class dependence for 49 dbNSFP predictors**

| reach band | n predictors | median reach | median covered | median must-answer | median penalty |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| reach >99.9% | 5 | 1.0000 | 0.7866 | 0.7866 | 0.0000 |
| reach 70 to 99.9% | 13 | 0.9211 | 0.9218 | 0.8312 | 0.0486 |
| reach <70% | 31 | 0.5885 | 0.9375 | 0.6226 | 0.3149 |
| missense: reach >99.9% | 6 | 1.0000 | 0.8507 | 0.8507 | 0.0000 |
| missense: reach 70 to 99.9% | 43 | 0.9362 | 0.9410 | 0.8706 | 0.0479 |
| missense: reach <70% | 0 | — | — | — | — |

These 49 predictors are audited on the 328,328 nonsynonymous and splice-site variants dbNSFP annotates (166,149 pathogenic, 162,179 benign), of which 202,643 are missense, across 15,606 genes; the class-gap counts below use a bootstrap resampling genes rather than variants (2,000 draws), and per-predictor intervals are in the deposited artefact. Predictors are grouped by reach into three disjoint bands and medians are taken over the group. Cut instead at nested reach thresholds, the same 49 give counts that overlap rather than partition: the 18 predictors reaching 75% of the panel carry a median penalty of 0.0217, the 12 reaching 90% carry 0.0009 and the 8 reaching 99% carry 0.0000. Rows marked missense repeat the accounting on the 202,643 missense substitutions (15,389 genes), with the same 2,000-draw gene bootstrap. The bands are cut on reach, not on ρ as the figures' pair-coverage regimes are (Figure 1), so on the missense subset GERP_92_mammals sits in the 70 to 99.9% band at ρ = 0.447 (Figure 4).

| quantity | value |
|---|---|
| class gap with a gene-clustered interval excluding zero | 44 of 49 |
| ... and a gap of at least 0.02 in magnitude | 39 of 49 |
| missingness alone outscoring must-answer, orientation-free | 30 of 48 |
| ... the same test taking the sign as given | 0 of 48 |
| predictors with a negative class gap, for which the signed test cannot fire | 34 of 49 |
| complete-reach predictors returning a penalty of exactly 0.0000 | 1 of 5 |
| missense: class gap with a gene-clustered interval excluding zero | 39 of 49 |
| missense: ... and a gap of at least 0.02 in magnitude | 25 of 49 |
| missense: ... and a gap of at least 0.05 in magnitude | 14 of 49 |
| missense: pairwise comparisons identified | 429 of 1,176 |
| missense: ... of which neither side is a conservation track | 180 of 429 |
| pairs feasible with both covered AUROCs at one half or above | 506 of 1,176 (1,174 missense) |
| comparisons identified if unscored variants are no easier than scored | 131 of 1,176 (488 missense) |
| comparisons identified at two review stars or more | 100 of 1,176 (566 missense) |
| values adding to reach: lexicographic gain, gene-clustered interval excluding zero | 49 of 49 (49 of 49 missense) |
| gene × consequence prior, out of fold, against the best must-answer AUROC | 0.974 against 0.968 |
| covered AUROC below the out-of-fold gene prior on its own covered set | 18 of 49 (27 against gene × consequence) |

Restricting to the classes dbNSFP annotates reverses the direction of the reach bias rather than removing it: here the missense-oriented predictors reach the negatives far more than the positives, where on the whole ClinVar panel they reach the positives more. Because the pooled missingness AUROC is the identity 0.5 + gap/2, a negative gap puts it below one half and the signed test cannot fire. Read orientation-free, the indicator still exceeds most of these predictors' must-answer AUROCs, which says that their reach tracks the label here, not that their values carry less information: the must-answer rule scores every pair touching an unscored variant at one half and so ignores what the indicator knows. The test of the values keeps that information: ranking by the indicator and then by value beats the indicator by ρ(A_cov − 0.5), and the gene-resampled interval on that gain excludes zero for all 49 predictors, on the whole panel and on the missense subset. This panel relocates the composition confound instead of clearing it (Note S28). What it does establish is the reach accounting itself. We draw no ranking of these predictors from this panel, for the reason set out in Note S28.
**Table S13. OMIA trait alleles: which positives are not disease variants, and what removing them does**

| Species | n | positives | trait positives | AUROC (full panel) | AUROC (disease only) | Δ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Goat | 98 | 9 | 8 | 0.959 | — | — |
| Chicken | 308 | 28 | 20 | 0.954 | 1.000 | +0.0464 |
| Pig | 396 | 36 | 13 | 0.863 | 0.952 | +0.0888 |
| Sheep | 616 | 56 | 27 | 0.962 | 0.990 | +0.0276 |
| Horse | 766 | 71 | 44 | 0.941 | 0.949 | +0.0079 |
| Cat | 1,362 | 122 | 38 | 0.890 | 0.971 | +0.0805 |
| Cattle | 2,067 | 188 | 28 | 0.974 | 0.982 | +0.0077 |
| Dog | 2,496 | 227 | 25 | 0.970 | 0.979 | +0.0098 |
| Human | 3,000 | 1,500 | 0 | 0.974 | 0.974 | +0.0000 |
| Mean, eight species |  |  |  | 0.941 | 0.975 | +0.0336 |
| Pooled | 11,109 | 2,237 | 203 | 0.973 | 0.985 | +0.0118 |

OMIA catalogues single-gene traits and disorders together, so 203 of the 737 scored non-human positives are trait alleles: coat and feather colour and type, morphology such as polydactyly and tail length, production traits such as double muscling and fecundity, blood groups, gait, and resistance to infection, including eight of goat's nine positives, which are PRNP alleles associated with resistance to scrapie. The class of each positive is curated from its OMIA phenotype string and deposited with it (atlas_positive_annotations.parquet in Additional file 3), where the rules and every correction to a phenotype-string pattern are stated; a phenotype that joins a trait to a disorder, such as white coat with deafness, counts as a disease, and the two cat CMAH records whose phenotype names only a 2019 blood-typing panel count, like the other CMAH alleles, as blood-group alleles. Removing the trait alleles and rescoring the identical panel at the identical 8,192-bp readout raises discrimination in every OMIA species that keeps at least five disease alleles, so their presence works against the reported values.
Trait positives removed: 203 of the 737 scored OMIA positives (27.5%), with 44 of 71 in horse and 20 of 28 in chicken. The mean over the eight species that keep at least five disease alleles moves from 0.941 to 0.975, and the pooled AUROC from 0.973 to 0.985. Over the same species and variants the impact ordering moves from 0.954 to 0.974 and the coding flag from 0.906 to 0.928, so the trait alleles hold back the annotation baselines as well. Goat keeps one disease allele, so its disease-only AUROC is not estimated.
**Table S14. The consequence gradient is not the model's alone: GERP through the identical analysis**

| Consequence class | n positives (GERP-scorable) | Evo 2 (1,001-bp single token) | GERP | Evo 2 − GERP |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| nonsense (stop-gain) | 110 | 0.954 | 0.834 | +0.120 |
| splicing | 67 | 0.772 | 0.900 | −0.128 |
| missense | 310 | 0.896 | 0.879 | +0.017 |
| regulatory | 21 | 0.579 | 0.720 | −0.141 |

The same positive-count-weighted estimator as the Evo 2 gradient, with GERP in place of Evo 2, but not the same variants, and we state that rather than let the table imply it. GERP is defined only where its track returns a finite value, so this arm runs on the GERP-reachable subset: different positive counts in all four classes (nonsense 110 against Evo 2's 112, splicing 67 against 70, missense 310 against 324, regulatory 21 against 18), a different species set (cattle enters every GERP class and carries none in the Evo 2 arm; goat leaves the nonsense class), and a strictly smaller negative pool. The "Evo 2 − GERP" column therefore differences two AUROCs computed on non-nested sets, the same reach mismatch Table S2 quantifies, arising here inside our own control. Read the two columns as two gradients that share a floor on the regulatory class rather than as a difference: conservation also reaches its floor there, so that floor reflects a constraint structure both scores inherit rather than a decay peculiar to the model. The regulatory cell rests on 21 GERP-reachable positives against 18 unmatched ones and is the least well powered of the four.
**Table S15. How much the human figure depends on which benign variants are drawn**

| Negative set | n negatives | AUROC | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|
| ClinVar B/LB, all | 17,376 | 0.851 | [0.836, 0.866] |
| ClinVar B/LB, coding only | 4,377 | 0.811 | [0.795, 0.826] |
| ClinVar B/LB, noncoding only | 12,999 | 0.865 | [0.850, 0.879] |

The human arm's 1,500 negatives are a matched sample from ClinVar's benign half; every one of them is a Benign/Likely_benign record, so human is the one species whose negatives are clinically adjudicated rather than catalogued population variation. This rescores the same 1,500 positives against a larger and differently composed sample of the same catalogue, at the identical 1,001-bp readout on GRCh38. Both draws are ClinVar B/LB, so what this bounds is sampling and composition sensitivity, not the population-versus-adjudicated question, which the human arm cannot pose.
Against the atlas panel's own 1,500 benign negatives the same positives give 0.825, so the human figure moves by −0.014 (coding-only) to +0.040 (noncoding-only) with the benign draw; the range is asymmetric, the noncoding draw is the larger move, and it is not a ±0.03 band, within a single catalogue.
**Table S16. Splicing benchmark by assay: Evo 2-1B, summed over a 1,001-bp window, against SpliceAI**

| assay | n | disruptive | Evo 2 (1B) | SpliceAI | difference |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| BRCA1 | 1,386 | 223 | 0.808 | 0.986 | −0.178 |
| FAS exon 6 | 189 | 115 | 0.462 | 0.729 | −0.267 |
| MLH1 | 296 | 160 | 0.692 | 0.966 | −0.274 |
| POU1F1 exon 2 | 941 | 96 | 0.605 | 0.961 | −0.356 |
| RON exon 11 | 598 | 409 | 0.557 | 0.630 | −0.073 |
| WT1 exon 9 | 502 | 57 | 0.372 | 0.912 | −0.540 |

Six assays scored independently, five of them saturation mutagenesis and the sixth the 296 curated MLH1 variants (Note S35). A pooled deficit means little if one exon drives it. All values are the Evo 2-1B checkpoint with its log-likelihood summed over a 1,001-bp window; on the BRCA1 assay Evo 2-40B at the 8,192-bp readout reads 0.974 (Note S65). The n-weighted mean of the six per-assay deficits against SpliceAI is −0.2626, larger than the pooled deficit of −0.177 given with Table S34 because the assays differ in both size and difficulty; dropping MLH1 moves it to −0.2617, and restricting to the four massively parallel splicing assays widens it to −0.3139. Because the panel is six assays in six genes, the assay and not the variant is the cluster unit for any inferential statement here. Treating the six per-assay deficits as the sample gives a mean of −0.281 with a 95% interval of [−0.448, −0.114], a half-width 7.0 times that of the pooled DeLong interval (Table S34) — the same direction of disagreement between closed-form and cluster-aware standard errors that we report on the dbNSFP panel — so we do not read the pooled interval as evidence that the difference is real. The distribution-free statement is the sign count: six of six, exact two-sided sign test p = 0.031. Against Pangolin the six per-assay deficits are likewise all negative, with an n-weighted mean of −0.259.

In this constructed background, 432 genes are unreachable by at least one predictor and none is unreachable by every predictor with holes. A union of answered sets would therefore reduce gene-level zero reach substantially, but it would require a defined score-combination rule and a new evaluation of class-dependent selection.
**Table S19. ClinVar consequence rungs at Evo 2-1B: discrimination, power and multiplicity**

| consequence | positive | negative | AUROC | 95% CI | MDE | p (FWER) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3 prime UTR variant | 54 | 250 | 0.7023 | [0.6143, 0.7851] | 0.122 | < 0.00002 |
| 5 prime UTR variant | 160 | 250 | 0.6497 | [0.5938, 0.7043] | 0.082 | 0.00042 |
| missense variant | 250 | 250 | 0.8496 | [0.8140, 0.8831] | 0.072 | < 0.00002 |
| non-coding transcript variant | 250 | 250 | 0.7167 | [0.6715, 0.7607] | 0.072 | < 0.00002 |
| nonsense | 250 | 250 | 0.7462 | [0.7020, 0.7885] | 0.072 | < 0.00002 |

All values are the Evo 2-1B checkpoint on the 1,001-bp window readout. Intervals are percentile bootstrap at B = 200,000 where available. MDE is the Hanley–McNeil minimum detectable difference from chance at 80% power. The p value is adjusted for the family-wise error rate (FWER) across the eight rungs by Westfall–Young max-T over 50,000 permutations — a separate resampling from the B = 200,000 bootstrap above. The smallest p that permutation count can resolve is 1/50,001 = 0.00002, so the seven cells shown as "< 0.00002" are bounds, not estimates.
Every rung is resolved against its own minimum detectable difference and survives family-wise control across the eight.

Macro-averaged ECE for the transferred isotonic map, the trivial two-parameter global sigmoid, and an in-species oracle, across twelve binning rules. The formal paired test was run on four of these (ece_grid): 3 of 4 are indistinguishable, with mass15 dissenting in transfer's favour.
**Table S22 (continued)**

| Species | mass10 → effective | mass15 → effective |
|---|---|---|
| Goat | 6 | 9 |
| Chicken | 5 | 6 |
| Pig | 6 | 7 |
| Sheep | 5 | 7 |
| Horse | 4 | 6 |
| Cat | 6 | 8 |
| Cattle | 5 | 7 |
| Dog | 5 | 7 |
| Human | 8 | 11 |

Why equal-mass rules disagree with equal-width rules. The transferred isotonic posterior takes only a few dozen distinct values per species, so an equal-mass rule cannot deliver the bin count it is asked for. Requested versus achieved bin counts are tabulated above.
This degeneracy is the source of the sign disagreement above. It is one-sided: it collapses the realised bin count for the isotonic arm alone, so the sigmoid's upward bias keeps growing with the requested count while the isotonic arm's saturates. Note S44 sets out that mechanism and the bias-subtracted comparison, which leaves no isotonic advantage at any rule.

Both aggregations of the four formally tested estimators (eight cells). The paired test was run on equal-width and equal-mass binning at 10 and 15 bins, under macro and pooled aggregation. The two pooled cells disagree in sign (width10 favours transfer, width15 favours the sigmoid), which is the direct evidence that pooled ECE is unstable on this posterior and why the macro aggregation is used throughout.

The marginal construction abstains far less but lets positive-class coverage collapse, on exactly the variants a clinician most needs to catch. This is why the conformal layer reported in Notes S63 and S66 is the Mondrian (class-conditional) arm. Only goat, chicken and pig are held out. The isotonic map and the conformal quantiles were fitted on the pooled variants of the other six species, so those rows are in-sample. Positive-class coverage in the three held-out species rests on 73 variants: goat is 9 of 9, chicken 27 of 28 and pig 32 of 36. Those coverages have a floor, because an abstention set contains both labels and so covers a positive. The table reports abstention pooled over the two classes. Split by class in the Mondrian arm, it is 0.222 in goat, 0.393 in chicken and 0.222 in pig on positives, against 0.533, 0.650 and 0.700 on negatives. Abstention concentrates in the negative class, and the share of positives receiving a set containing the positive label alone is 0.778, 0.571 and 0.667. No cell carries a confidence interval; pig's exact 95% interval runs from 0.739 to 0.969 and contains the nominal 0.90.

Refusing each species' own least-confident 15% of calls defers 1,673 of 11,130 (a realised 15.03%, the threshold being applied within each species) and captures 35.4% of all errors (316 of 893, on the Platt posterior of Table S27), a lift of 2.35 over refusing at random. Refusing the worst 15% globally instead, ignoring species, defers 1,669 calls and captures 38.4% (343), lift 2.56.
The minimum sits at 85% coverage; refusing beyond it buys nothing and eventually costs, as the retained set becomes small and unrepresentative.

The deployed (Platt) arm, full trajectory to 20% coverage. The isotonic table uses the isotonic arm and stops at 30%; the per-variant deliverable is the Platt arm (Note S63), whose deposited trajectory also reports the retained-set sensitivity. Its error rises monotonically below the 80–85% minimum and crosses the do-nothing baseline of 0.0802, so below that crossing the layer is worse than answering every variant; sensitivity collapses in step while specificity climbs toward 1.0; pushed hard, the layer answers 'benign' to almost everything.
**Table S27. Selective prediction per species**

| Species | n | errors | refused | errors removed | lift | lift 95% CI | resolved |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Goat | 99 | 6 | 15 | 5 | 5.50 | [3.09, 9.90] | yes |
| Chicken | 308 | 12 | 47 | 4 | 2.18 | [0.53, 4.18] | no |
| Pig | 396 | 20 | 60 | 12 | 3.96 | [2.54, 5.66] | yes |
| Sheep | 616 | 21 | 93 | 15 | 4.73 | [3.37, 6.34] | yes |
| Horse | 781 | 31 | 117 | 14 | 3.01 | [1.90, 4.24] | yes |
| Cat | 1,365 | 61 | 205 | 30 | 3.27 | [2.52, 4.14] | yes |
| Cattle | 2,068 | 111 | 311 | 59 | 3.53 | [2.93, 4.19] | yes |
| Dog | 2,497 | 86 | 375 | 37 | 2.86 | [2.19, 3.57] | yes |
| Human | 3,000 | 545 | 450 | 140 | 1.71 | [1.52, 1.93] | yes |
| **Pooled** | **11,130** | **893** | **1,673** | **316** | **2.35** | **–** | **–** |

Each species refuses its own least-confident 15% of calls, ordered by the confidence functional |2p − 1|; an error is a call on the wrong side of the 0.5 threshold. Lift is the fraction of that species' errors removed divided by the fraction of its calls refused, so 1.0 is what random refusal achieves. Because the 15% boundary can fall inside a confidence tie block, the refused count for a species can differ by a variant or two between deposited artefacts, so re-deriving the boundary arithmetically gives a per-species total of 1,669 (rounding to nearest) or 1,674 (rounding up) against the 1,673 the thresholding actually produced. The refused counts here are the deposited ones, so this table, Table S26 and the recompute layer agree. Recomputed from the deposited per-variant file, whose per-variant posterior is the two-parameter Platt map (not the isotonic map used for the ECE analysis); the isotonic posterior takes too few distinct values to order variants for selective refusal (Note S63).
Lift 95% CIs and the resolution flag are the deposited bootstrap intervals (seed 20260719, 2,000 resamples); a lift is resolved only when its interval excludes 1. Chicken is the one species whose interval spans 1 (not distinguishable from random refusal).
Human has the lowest lift of the nine while carrying 545 of the 893 errors, so the species with the most errors is the one where confidence helps least, which is a property of its 1:1 panel composition rather than its labels: at a matched 10:1 composition its lift rises to 3.19. Seven of the nine per-species lifts exceed the pooled figure, because pooling weights by error count and human dominates that count. The highest lift rests on six errors and should not be read as a species ranking.
**Table S28. The confusion matrix behind the 893 errors**

|  | predicted negative | predicted positive | total |
|---|---|---|---|
| **actually positive** | 731 (missed) | 1,509 (caught) | 2,240 |
| **actually negative** | 8,728 (correct) | 162 (false alarm) | 8,890 |
| **total** | 9,459 | 1,671 | 11,130 |

At the 0.5 decision threshold on the calibrated probability, before any abstention. Recomputed from the deposited per-variant file on the same Platt posterior as Table S27, for the reason given there.
Pooled sensitivity 0.674 [0.654, 0.693], specificity 0.982 [0.979, 0.984] (deposited bootstrap), overall error rate 0.0802. The 893 errors are 731 missed positives against only 162 false alarms, so 81.9% of all errors are missed positives. That is the asymmetry the selective layer does not fix, because refusal removes false alarms far more effectively than misses.
